# Commentary on Michel Henry, *Philosophie et phénoménologie du corps: essai sur l'ontologie biranienne*

Citation is by the pagination of the Presses Universitaires de France edition (Épiméthée; 1st ed. 1965, 4th ed. 2001), given in the form (PPC 12). References to Maine de Biran follow Henry's own sigla: E for the *Essai sur les fondements de la psychologie* (Tisserand edition, vols. VIII–IX), D followed by volume and page for the Tisserand *Œuvres*.

Four misreadings dog this book, and the commentary guards against each throughout. First, Henry's "subjective body" is not the lived body of Merleau-Ponty or the *Leib* of Husserl's *Ideen II*; Henry insists in the preface to the second edition that he wrote in ignorance of Merleau-Ponty and that his conception is opposed to both German and French phenomenology on precisely this point. The subjective body is not a body given in a privileged mode of experience but a body that is not given at all in the sense of appearing at a distance. Second, the "transcendental body" is not a Kantian condition of possibility standing over against the empirical body; it is the ego itself, and the identification is strict. Third, Henry's "ontology" is not regional ontology in Husserl's sense, and his "phenomenology" does not name the study of phenomena as they show themselves in the world — his central move is to deny that the original mode of manifestation involves showing at all. Fourth, "effort" in Maine de Biran is not a psychological datum recovered by introspection; Henry's whole polemic against the nineteenth-century French reception turns on refusing to read Biran as a psychologist of inner life.

The book's terminological center is the distinction between two modes of givenness: being given at a phenomenological distance (*distance phénoménologique*), and being given immediately, without distance. Henry uses "transcendent being" for whatever is given in the first mode and "absolute subjectivity" or "immanence" for the second. This distinction, taken over from Biran's contrast between exterior knowledge and reflection, does all the work in the book, and every later thesis is a specification of it.

## Introduction. The Apparent Contingency of the Question Concerning the Body and the Necessity of an Ontological Analysis of the Body

Henry opens with a question against himself. If ontological research aims at a phenomenology of the ego, then raising the problem of the body inside that project looks like a contingent detour — a specification that forgets the inquiry's real object. He plants a note here recording that the book was originally a chapter of *L'essence de la manifestation*, detached because of its length; the self-directed question is therefore not rhetorical but a genuine structural embarrassment inherited from the larger work. The stakes are set by a single premise the whole Introduction will attack: that the body is transcendent being, a denizen of the world where subjectivity does not reside.

If that premise holds, then treating the body philosophically requires converting the inquiry into a study of the world and of how certain worldly items are constituted for us. Henry presses the difficulty further. Among all transcendent items deserving their own investigations, why privilege the body? Granting that such research belongs to philosophy generally, it would have nothing to do with the ontological analysis of subjectivity. The problem is not that the body is unworthy of study but that studying it would seem to require abandoning the region from which the inquiry defined itself.

A first line of defense would ground the contingency of the question in the contingency of its object: having a body is the contingent fact par excellence. Henry rejects the framing. What is at issue may not be a fact at all. If the relation of body to consciousness is what grounds our very idea of contingency — what makes it possible for anything to show up for us as a contingent fact — then it is a structure, and one that does not merely inhere in human nature but defines it. The move is transcendental in form: the candidate for contingency turns out to be the condition under which contingency is thinkable.

Characterizing man by consciousness or subjectivity would then be an abstraction, if every determination of subjectivity were intelligible only through relation to the body. Henry's example is shame (*pudeur*), where the mind's heightened presence to itself is at once a heightened and painful consciousness of the body; he supports it with Jean Wahl's formulation that the more a man feels himself as spirit, the more he simultaneously feels himself as body with its sexual character. The two terms grow together rather than varying inversely. Our question, then, is the anticipation in the order of discourse of a structure in things themselves: reality is not composed of self-enclosed autonomous entities but of dialectical structures, and man is one of them.

The mind–body structure is the most dialectical of all, and Henry follows Kierkegaard's *Concept of Anxiety* in calling it a fundamental paradox — fundamental because it actually functions as a ground. The tragic, the comic, the sense of having a body, exhibitionism, timidity: these are not affective accidents befalling human nature from outside but determinations for which human nature must account. Note the inversion Henry performs on the affective register. He is not deriving moods from a prior structure; he is reading the moods as evidence that a structure must be posited to make them possible.

Beneath such feelings lies a deeper tonality determining the affective and existential modalities through which individual and collective human history passes. Henry asks whether this fundamental tonality is not the very moment of the dialectical union of consciousness and body. This is the seed of his mature doctrine of affectivity as the essence of manifestation, developed in *L'essence de la manifestation*: affectivity is not one region of experience among others but the original self-revelation of life. Here it appears only as a hypothesis about what underlies particular moods.

The paradox is the knot of existence and the origin in which its various attitudes are rooted. Philosophical reflection was bound eventually to notice it, since the central phenomenon of incarnation could not stay indefinitely hidden. If incarnate being rather than pure consciousness is the original fact one must start from, the inquiry must leave the sphere of subjectivity to work out a problematic of the body — and this now looks required by the nature of things rather than contingent. Henry then turns the term back on itself: it is that nature which now shows up as contingent. We are the primordial contingency.

What does this constitutive contingency of the relation between consciousness and body mean at the ontological level? Correctly interpreted, it can mean only one thing: relative to the sphere of subjectivity, which coincides with that of existence itself, the other ontological region in which something like a body can appear is heterogeneous, essentially different. So long as inquiry moves exclusively inside subjectivity, it meets nothing that could be called a body — not even our own. Encounter with a body becomes possible only when one steps outside that original sphere. Henry is stating the position he will overturn, not endorsing it; the whole book argues that the passage is unnecessary because there is a body inside the sphere.

What lies outside subjectivity is transcendent being, but several ontological regions remain distinguishable within it. A famous philosophy identified body with extension, a reality composed of parts outside parts. Henry asks whether our body can coincide with that *partes extra partes* body of physical nature. It cannot: our body is a living body, and as such belongs to a region whose phenomenological characters mark it as an autonomous essence, not to be confused with Cartesian extension, whatever deductions the sciences may construct between the two orders.

But biology, despite its phenomenological footing, is a science. Its advances occur within the transcendent masses of scientific knowledge, are in principle indefinite, and do not aim at matching our immediate experience — which for the scientist is mere appearance. Henry draws the consequence sharply. If our body were a biological entity, our knowledge of it would coincide with scientific knowledge, so that only a future man at the ideal terminus of scientific development would truly know what our body is, and one could no longer see what knowledge a primitive could have of his body, or how he could arrive at the opinion that a body belongs to him. The argument is a reductio: the biological body cannot be the original body, because knowledge of the original body is not deferred to the end of inquiry.

The biological body is in a sense a cultural object and as such historical, in its appearance as in its modifications, which are just those brought by the development of science. Henry appends a long note repudiating historicism at the ontological level: man is not essentially historical, what is originary in him persists identical through the centuries, and morality shows the same permanence — each generation, as Kierkegaard says, faces the same task as the last. Even granting that the human body presents historically variable characters through diet, dress, sexuality, and fashion, these concern the ways man represents and comports himself toward his body, not the original body. What is historical is cultural objects and the human attitudes bearing on them; the ontological ground founding both remains indifferent to that evolution and is presupposed by it. The note matters because it fixes the target: Henry's reduction is designed to reach a stratum that historical and cultural variation cannot touch.

We today do have an original relation to the biological body, and we are all historical beings to the extent that we subscribe to our scientists' science and its representations find echo in common sense. But it is not from that relation that we draw our first knowledge of the body, nor the behaviors in which it is implicated. We do not wait to have read the latest biology before running, jumping, walking, or raising an arm, and reading it changes nothing in our primitive powers. Nothing is more inoperative than science with respect to our conduct and to the primordial knowledge conduct always presupposes. This "primordial knowledge" — a knowing that is not representational and is not acquired — is the book's real subject; the term *pouvoir* (power) appearing here will become the central category of Chapter II.

Far from biology being able to clarify that knowledge, it is founded on it; it cannot explain what it presupposes as its condition of possibility, as the ontological horizon within which it finds objects, offers explanations, and first poses its problems. The foundation runs one way only.

Henry now specifies the reduction he is performing, in order to mark the boundaries between originary knowledge and the various knowledges founded on it. Our body is a living body, but this cannot be understood biologically. If life is not primitively an object of scientific experience for us, is it not given in naive experience as a transcendent structure? Alongside inert objects, tools, and cultural objects there are, in our immediate surroundings, beings we call living. The problem of knowledge of the body would then be solved by describing the characters such living realities present. A difficulty arises: among living bodies a distinction seems required between the body of an amoeba, or even of higher animals, and that of man — Henry brackets plant life deliberately in a note — since in man's case we have not merely a living body but a human body, whose properties are so particular that we seem to face a new structure having as little in common with the preceding ones as those had with inert being.

This yields three transcendent bodies: the biological body, whose truth is settled by the sciences that determine and constitute it; the living body as it appears in naive experience, given with the characters proper to perception; and the human body, itself the object of an original experience whose characters do not reduce to those of the two preceding, and which seems to demand a new science. Henry adds a question about the ordering: considering the perception of the human body alone, can one discern what in it belongs to a living being and what belongs to man, and does the structure of the animal body lie within the human as an element or as a foundation?

These distinctions and questions are, in Henry's eyes, unimportant — because they do not touch the fundamental ontological problem. Whatever region we finally assign our body to, biological, living, or a *sui generis* human region, in every case we deal only with specifications and structures of transcendent being in general, however great the differences between them. So long as that holds, the problematic of the body remains contingent relative to the project of fundamental ontology and alien to a genuinely first philosophy. The contingency of the question, and more deeply the contingency of the body's belonging to human reality, are insurmountable if the body is heterogeneous and irreducible with respect to transcendental subjectivity itself. This is the pivot of the Introduction: the debate over which transcendent region the body occupies is idle, because every answer leaves the original problem untouched.

The alternative would be to concede that first philosophy cannot be identified with an ontological analysis of subjectivity or the exploration of that region alone. Henry rejects this too, and diagnoses why the body's contingency looks so total. It is an illusion generated by a fictional starting point. A "pure" man — abstract man reduced to pure subjectivity — has no motive to ask about a body he lacks, or which is at most an accessory and contingent appendix. A disembodied subject like the Kantian spectator of the Paralogisms is a pure mind surveying the world; his own body can neither enter into his knowledge of the universe nor become an object of special inquiry, and counts at best as an empirical curiosity devoid of philosophical dignity. But man is an incarnate subject, his knowledge is situated in the universe, and things are given to him under perspectives oriented from his own body. The appeal to perspectival orientation is conventional; Henry uses it only to force the dilemma he now states, and his own solution will not rest on it.

Should the body then be the theme of a research bearing on real man rather than the abstract man of idealism — the being of flesh and blood we all are? If first philosophy coincides with such research, its field can no longer be circumscribed to subjectivity alone; its object is the dialectical structure binding consciousness and body, or existence as the existence of a real, incarnate being.

Henry sets out three exclusive options. Either the body need not be taken into account in the definition of man, and we may pursue the study of the subjective ego while claiming to account for human reality in its authentic being and totality. Or this is an abstract standpoint from which primitive concrete reality cannot be described, and the philosophy of subjectivity must yield to a realism or existentialism starting from situation, corporeity, incarnation, with the courage to study what human reality's own status implies — contingency, finitude, absurdity. Henry's compressed reference to "absurdity" identifies the target as the existentialism of his French contemporaries, and the phrasing concedes their courage while denying their premise.

Or, third: the central phenomenon of the body does not escape a phenomenological ontology built on an analysis of subjectivity, because the body in its original nature belongs to the sphere of existence that is subjectivity itself. This is Henry's own position, and the rest of the book establishes it.

He then blocks a weaker version of that third option. Intentionalities are directed on the transcendent structures called biological body, living body, human body; the study of subjectivity would thus seem to refer us back to a body's existence. But such a body could be only a determination of the other in general. Nothing would confer on it, within the sphere of the non-ego, the character of belonging to the ego that seems essential to it, and nothing would motivate inserting a problematic of the body into a philosophy of the first person. Henry's point is that intentional analysis reaches the body only as correlate, and a correlate cannot be mine in the required sense; ownness cannot be constituted from the side of the object.

The thesis follows in its strongest form: our body is primitively neither a biological body nor a living body nor a human body; it belongs to a radically different ontological region, that of absolute subjectivity. To speak of a *transcendental body* is not an implausible and gratuitous assertion. It is to see the necessity of answering affirmatively the question whether the body is known by us in the same way as any other intentionality of the ego's life, and whether its being should receive, in a phenomenological ontology, the same status as the being of intentionality in general and of the ego. It is to grasp the conditions that alone let us account for a body situated at the heart of human reality: a body that is an I. Read the italicized formula strictly — not a body belonging to an I, nor a body through which an I acts, but a body identical with the I.

The philosophical systems have held very diverse theories of the body while agreeing in one decisive doctrine: that our body's being belongs to the world. So firmly established did this seem that no one thought to doubt it. Henry names the exception. The first and in truth the only philosopher in the long history of human reflection who saw the necessity of determining our body originally as a subjective body is Maine de Biran — "this prince of thought," who deserves to be regarded alongside Descartes and Husserl as one of the true founders of a phenomenological science of human reality. A note documents that Biran was fully aware of his originality, quoting his claim to consider knowledge of one's own body from an entirely new point of view. The comparison to Descartes and Husserl is exact rather than flattering: what Henry credits to all three is the discovery of a starting point, not a body of doctrine.

How could this discovery pass unnoticed, and why was Biran's work so rarely understood? Henry answers by way of Biran's isolation in nineteenth-century French philosophy. It is customary to place him at the origin of a current running through Lachelier, Boutroux, Ravaisson, Lagneau, to Bergson — a "spiritualist" current marked by attention to the inner life and an introspective tendency. Henry calls this a heavy misreading that definitively compromised understanding of the work. The historical filiation exists, but Biranian thought has nothing to do with introspection, nor with the inner life as the neo-Kantians understood it, nor with Bergsonian intuition. This is the misreading-guard the commentary must hold to throughout: every subsequent appeal to *effort*, *sentiment*, or *conscience* in Henry's reconstruction is an ontological claim, never a report of introspective data.

The filiation is nonetheless intelligible. The absence of any ontology of subjectivity in Kantianism, and the consequences of that absence for the problem of inner life, necessarily pushed Kant's heirs toward thoughts that could partly fill the gap. But the reason for the interest taken in Biran was the very reason it could never be genuinely understood, since the interest came from philosophers moving within presuppositions incompatible with Biranism's central intuition. That intuition could be received by nineteenth- and twentieth-century French philosophy only at the price of a distortion all the more dangerous for being entirely involuntary — a fall measuring the distance between an authentic conception of subjectivity and "psychology."

Isolated from the philosophers who thought they were continuing his work, Biran was still more isolated from the public, by the very nature of his enterprise. Research belonging to first philosophy finds little echo not because it is difficult or consists in fantastic constructions varying with temperament, but because it lacks everything most people count as sensational, interesting, or original — because its object is the humblest, the most banal, the most common thing there is. Henry is describing his own project as much as Biran's, and the criterion is methodological: first philosophy's object cannot be exotic, because it is what is always already in play.

Biran did not deceive himself about this solitude or its motives. Henry quotes his declaration that he raised his feeble monument for the small number of men who cultivate the inner sense, marking his passage through a desert, uncultivated country that travelers are little curious to visit, so that it might tell those who come after what thoughts occupied a friend of the science of man.

That feeble monument — one of the greatest the human spirit ever built, drawing its materials from the desert country that is the original site where all constitution occurs and where first philosophy must move — is what Henry proposes to decipher, in order to gather its teaching and use it as a guiding thread for his own ontological analysis of the body. The formulation fixes the book's method: Biran is a guiding thread, not an authority, and Chapter VI will turn against him on the question of passivity.

## Chapter I. The Philosophical Presuppositions of the Biranian Analysis of the Body

Henry opens by denying that Biran's discovery of the subjective body was an accident. It is inscribed in a context that makes it necessary, and that context is a phenomenological ontology. Biran took the problem of the ego as his theme, saw quickly that it could be resolved only by an ontological analysis of the concept of subjectivity, and found that this analysis compelled him to pose the problem of the body on entirely new bases — a problem that, correctly interpreted and situated, leads back to the problem of the ego with which it is identical. Biran's teaching therefore reduces to a formula: a body that is subjective and that is the ego itself. Defining man as body, Biran brushes against materialism, but the appearance conceals the opposite intention, which is to undercut materialism at its foundation. The circularity Henry describes — ego to subjectivity to body to ego — is not a defect but the structure of the demonstration, since the terms turn out to be one.

Before setting out Biran's theses on the body, Henry proposes to retrace the general project of the ontology in which they belong. He announces three studies: the phenomenological presuppositions of Biranian ontology, the transcendental deduction of the categories, and the theory of the ego and the problem of the soul. These become §§ 1–3.

### § 1. The Phenomenological Presuppositions of Biranian Ontology

The difficulty in grasping the originality and decisive importance of Biran's theses lies in his terminology. It is not new; Biran formulates radically novel ontological propositions in a traditional and apparently clear vocabulary, so that their content risks passing unnoticed and inviting misreadings that permanently block comprehension. The only remedy is to upset the language, accepting the ungrateful and easily mocked task of subjecting it to the worst treatment, even if heaviness of expression is the only visible gain. The greatest thinkers did not shrink from this; the modest subprefect of Bergerac did not think himself entitled to such license. The paradoxical result is that, despite the French simplicity of his style, his thought remained misunderstood longer than Kant's or Heidegger's. Henry therefore sacrifices the clarity of Biran's style to recover the clarity of his thought, expressing the principal theses of Biranism within Henry's own terminology. The charge of cavalier treatment would hold only if one mistook his intention, which is to gather a very great philosopher's teaching more humbly and faithfully. This is a methodological warrant for everything that follows: the reader should expect Henry's vocabulary, not Biran's, and should not treat divergence from Biran's wording as evidence of infidelity.

For Biran there are two kinds of knowledge and consequently two kinds of being. In the first, being is given through the mediation of a phenomenological distance; this is transcendent being, and Biran calls the corresponding knowledge exterior knowledge. In the second, being is given immediately, with no distance, and this being is not just any being but the self, whose being is thus determined solely by the manner in which it is given to us. Biran calls this second form "reflection," and a system of ideas founded on it a "reflexive system." Henry immediately posts the warning that governs the entire book: Biran's term means exactly the contrary of what we ordinarily mean by reflection, since ordinary reflection names the operation by which what was given immediately moves away from us and, through phenomenological distance, falls under the jurisdiction of the transcendental horizon of being. Without this observation an essential confusion enters, and it was under cover of that confusion that the neo-Kantians and many others could take themselves for Biranians. Biran abets the confusion by sometimes using "reflection" in its classical sense and sometimes in a third sense — Henry cross-references Chapter VI, p. 235 — but the whole context of his philosophy shows the original sense is the one just defined. Note that Henry derives being from givenness rather than the reverse: what a being is follows from how it gives itself.

The passages explicitly treating reflection attach the qualifier "simple" to it, which suffices to exclude the mediate signification of classical reflection. Biran speaks of the simple and perfectly clear ideas of reflection. Reflection is identified with the original source of all evidence, namely the *cogito* as Biran understands it: not a reflexive and intellectual act, but an action, an effort, a movement. This is the first appearance of the book's central substitution. Where Descartes finds a thinking substance, Biran finds a movement — and the ontological status of movement is what Chapter III will settle.

Henry assembles a series of Biranian texts to establish the identification of reflection with effort understood as spontaneous conscious movement. Biran situates himself in the standpoint of reflection without leaving the fact of consciousness. He distinguishes two essentially diverse kinds of science that have vainly been assimilated: those attaching to objects of imagination or exterior representation, and those concentrating on the subject of reflection or internal apperception. He holds that the idea of the individuality of the self and of everything belonging to it can be drawn only from intimate reflection or the feeling of effort. Decisive texts forbid understanding Biranian reflection as mediate knowledge introducing distance between itself and its object, as objectification, as the emergence of a transcendent being: speaking of an organic center from which, physiologically considered, human action would emanate, Biran says the self is there objectified under an individual image rather than conceived under the reflexive and unrepresentable idea proper to it. He asks whether there are not intimate thoughts and volitions that can in no way be read off outside or represented by any sort of image, and answers that conceiving them would require being identified with the active and knowingly productive force of such acts, with the self, which feels or apperceives itself in its operations but does not see itself as object and does not imagine itself as phenomenon. He states flatly that all modes or operations whose ideas we can acquire only by intimate reflection are absolutely unrepresentable. The load-bearing word in this dossier is "identified": knowledge of the self is not a relation to the self but coincidence with it.

Henry now fixes his own definition. "Reflection" designates the knowledge included in every intentionality and which is not knowledge of what that intentionality aims at. The term is intelligible because it signifies not transcendence but what does not go toward the world, what returns to itself and stays near itself in its distance from every thing. What Biran expresses by the word is the very depth of subjectivity, its intimate life, as opposed to transcendent being in general, which has no interior dimension and which Biran often designates by "image" — a term recalling the Spinozist image, mute and as though painted on a tablet. Reflection is thus opposed to the movement of transcendence and cannot designate a particular case of it, namely classical reflection. Two things are established at once: that intentionality has a self-knowledge that is not itself intentional, and that this self-knowledge is not a second intentional act. Henry's later doctrine of auto-affection is already fully in place, only under Biran's name.

The term itself Biran borrowed from Locke, but out of desperation, for want of better, and only to oppose Condillac's school and its sensualist terminology. Biran glosses consciousness as knowledge-with, knowledge of self along with that of something; there is an interior knowledge, an intimate faculty of our thinking being which knows that such modifications occur and such acts are performed, and without this reflective knowledge there would be neither ideology nor metaphysics — so a name is needed for this interior knowledge, since "sensation" cannot say everything. Biran called for a new terminology that would state the central intuition of his thought and believed it alone could bring to light the real foundation of the science of human reality, a "metaphysical evidence": if we had an express language suited to reflection, there would be a metaphysical evidence as there is a mathematical one. Henry's citation of the desperation is not incidental. It licenses his own violence to Biran's vocabulary: Biran himself judged the vocabulary inadequate to the intuition.

With that express language Henry proceeds to the central problem of Biranian philosophy and of all philosophy, as the *Essai* formulates it: is there an immediate internal apperception? The question directly challenges what Henry calls ontological monism — the position that nothing can be given to us otherwise than within and through the mediation of the transcendental horizon of being in general. He refers the reader to section I of *L'essence de la manifestation* for the full treatment. For Biran there is no "being in general"; his philosophy is a genuine ontological dualism, and he states that it mattered to signal the existence of two orders of facts and to recognize the necessity of two kinds of observation appropriate to them. Ontological monism is Henry's name for the thesis he takes to unite Kant, Husserl, and Heidegger despite their differences, and the dualism at issue is not mind and body but two modes of manifestation.

Biran reflects on the ontology of nature as he finds it in Bacon. That ontology is at once a phenomenology and a methodology, natural phenomena being submitted to a determinate method. But are there not other phenomena, and does a new method not then impose itself? The problem is whether the method of observing, classifying, and analyzing can be absolutely the same in its aim, direction, and means when one passes from the science of ideas representing external objects to that of the modifications or acts which concentrate the self within its own limits.

This is to pose the problem of psychology in its relations with the philosophy of nature, the theme of Biran's *Essai* — whose full title Henry recalls is *Essai sur les fondements de la psychologie et sur ses rapports avec l'étude de la nature*. Considering psychology as the science of interior facts, it does not appear that it could be founded on a method different from the one used since Bacon in all the natural sciences. Biran shows on the contrary that these other phenomena, the interior facts, must be studied by a wholly different method because they are given in a wholly different way.

How are they given? Though Biran says repeatedly that there are two kinds of observation, Henry insists the second kind is not an observation at all — not interior observation running parallel to exterior observation, the one reaching natural facts and the other psychic facts. Such interior observation would correspond to our introspection, to intuition, to classical reflection, all of which Biran absolutely rejects as foundations for his psychology. Conscious life cannot be given in a transcendent inner experience; it remains that it is given in a *transcendental* inner experience. Henry attaches a definition that fixes the term for the whole book: by transcendental inner experience he means the original revelation of lived experience to itself as accomplished in a sphere of radical immanence, in conformity with the fundamental ontological process of auto-affection, whose eidetic structure he analyzed in detail in *L'essence de la manifestation*. Biran's own text supports the distinction unconditionally: if the first art, that of interior observation, was happily cultivated by Locke's psychologist disciples, has the second and far more difficult art, that of interior experience, really been practiced? The distinction between interior observation and interior experience is the hinge of the section, and Henry's misreading-guard depends on it. Any reading that makes Biran an introspective psychologist has collapsed experience into observation.

What Biran effects here is the substitution of a transcendental phenomenology for classical empirical psychology, and building such a phenomenology goes together with constituting an ontology of subjectivity. Because there is such a thing as transcendental inner experience, an ontology of subjectivity is required and a transcendental phenomenology — Biran's "subjective ideology" — is possible.

The argument for this runs through the faculties. Because all intentionalities in general, and hence the essential intentionalities of consciousness, know themselves originally in the immanence of their own being and in their immediate accomplishment, we are able to name them and acquire their idea. Biran observes that the faculties have no mirror of their own to reflect them externally; like the eye, they apply themselves to all the objects in their range without seeing or knowing themselves. Imagination creating or reproducing a sensible idea does not imagine itself; memory cannot apperceive itself in the present; reasoning, judge of the most remote relations, does not judge or reason itself. How then can each faculty, unable to represent itself or apply itself to itself as object of knowledge, be known, and by what means could we have acquired the ideas corresponding to imagining, remembering, judging, reasoning, willing? Henry appends a note of the first importance: the thesis Biran seems here to assert is exactly what Henry calls ontological monism, but one must understand that these faculties know themselves without seeing themselves, by a knowledge of another kind — as Biran affirms a few lines later. Biran will say of the eye and of the body in general exactly what he says here of the faculties, and this assimilation within a single ontological status, that of transcendental inner experience, will suffice for the prodigious discovery of the subjective body. The note is the structural key to the book. The body enters the ontology by the same argument that admits the faculties: it is knowable and yet unrepresentable, therefore it is known in immanence.

The answer can only be the exercise of a special sense, which Henry, mixing Biran's terminology with his own, calls a transcendental inner sense. The exercise of this sense is to what happens within us what exterior sight is to objects; but unlike the latter, interior vision carries its own torch and illuminates itself with the light it communicates. Henry reads this determination of intentionality as carrying a conception of original truth as subjectivity: the primitive fact carries its criterion with it without borrowing it elsewhere. Transcendental inner experience, the milieu where original truth is accomplished, is therefore also the source of all our ideas of the faculties, and so serves as the foundation of transcendental phenomenology. Biran confirms it: one cannot deny that there are positive ideas attached to the terms expressing the real operations of perceiving, willing, comparing, reflecting, so one must examine whether their origin might be referred to some particular interior sense by which the individual would be in relation with himself in the exercise of his operations — whence one would conceive the natural foundation of a science of our faculties, a properly subjective ideology. The self-illuminating torch is not a metaphor Henry can afford to leave decorative: it states that in this domain the medium of manifestation and what is manifested coincide, which is precisely what "no phenomenological distance" means.

The project of that subjective ideology also includes the idea of a return to a sphere of absolute certainty on which it must be founded. Such certainty derives from the very structure of the experience in which its content is given, in the absolute transparency resulting from the absence of all distance, in immanence. Biran: our intimate sense is the most perfect way of knowing, as being the only immediate one. The primitive fact must be established by employing the sense specially and exclusively appropriate to it. Henry observes that "fact" and "sense" are one, the two terms referring to the ontological and the phenomenological aspect of a single essence whose disclosure presupposes building an ontology of subjectivity. This identity of fact and sense forecloses the objection that Biran posits an inner faculty and then a separate inner datum for it; there is one essence described twice.

Biran was the only philosopher of his century who painfully felt the absence of such an ontology and understood the necessity of constituting it. He says simply that philosophers perhaps exaggerate the impotence of all our means of knowing primitive facts, and that we must be given the just measure of those means. Once that is done, the pessimism marking nineteenth-century theory of knowledge is dispelled — the pessimism that makes such theory nothing but a theory of the world, whose knowledge is never anything but knowledge of the object. The ontological deficiency producing this pessimism strikes the most valid systems at the heart, in particular the Kantian, whose essential lacuna Biran circumscribes in a phrase: Kant in no way determined the primitive facts, which he confuses with the first passive modifications of sensibility. This charge against Kant is the seed of Chapter VI. Henry will eventually turn a structurally similar charge against Biran himself, on the ground that Biran too failed to think passivity ontologically.

Developing our means of knowing to their just measure is to glimpse the existence of an absolute knowledge and of the sphere of absolute certainty it delimits; to see the necessity of building an absolute science, of which the science of the faculties is one element, a phenomenological science of human reality endowed with apodictic certainty; to bring to light the possibility, in Biran's phrase, of setting out the elements of the science of the human mind.

Disclosing that sphere of absolute certainty, which is also a sphere of absolute existence, presupposes a division between what falls under such certainty and what cannot claim it, at least directly. To build a science endowed with absolute certainty is to make that division, reducing the vast field of human knowledge to that of original and absolute knowledge given phenomenologically in apodictic evidence — in other words, to carry out the phenomenological reduction. Biran's discovery of a second kind of observation was not made in the course of an external inventory of our cognitive powers; the moment of the discovery coincides with the phenomenological reduction, with which it is one. Henry's claim is strong and should be registered as such: Biran did not describe a reduction, he performed one, and the doctrine of the two knowledges is its result rather than its premise.

Biran's entire work is one vast phenomenological reduction, as one sees in the theory of the faculties just expounded and in the theories of the category, the soul, and finally the body, which Henry will study in turn. The idea of such a reduction can also be read in the constant movement by which Biranian thought opposes everything that is construction, theory, hypothesis, probability, and denounces in advance all the errors that would be avoided if a more fixed line of demarcation were drawn between the domain of hypothetical truth and that of absolute truth. Hence the results acquired by scientific methods cannot claim absolute truth — not so much because they are provisional or imperfect in their domain, but because they do not belong to a domain where anything like absolute truth is possible. Biran: hypothetical or conditional results obtained by that route, having no means of interior verification, cannot solve any problem of this order; whatever they might be, they would not even touch questions posed from a reflected standpoint. The distinction is one of domain, not of degree. Even a completed science would not encroach on the sphere of absolute truth.

It has never been said more forcefully, Henry comments, that the sphere of absolute science of human reality has no relation to that of the sciences and is therefore wholly independent of it. A truly first philosophy must hold to the phenomenological given in which being and appearing are identical, an identification realized in principle in the sphere of subjectivity — and also, though in another way, Henry notes, in the first phenomenological stratum of transcendent being. Failing to hold to such a given, one risks confusing it with various transcendent constructions, mixing all the planes, posing quantities of false problems, and being driven to the worst inconsistencies: as if, Biran's example runs, I denied the real perception of colors on the ground that, not knowing the luminous fluid in itself or the disturbances it communicates to the retina, I could not perceive its effects. The example is well chosen because it exhibits the specific error of grounding a phenomenological given in its physical conditions and then declaring the given unreal when the conditions are unknown.

Force — that most metaphysical and suspect entity — enters Biran's philosophy only after undergoing the reduction, when its notion is reduced to its last degree of simplicity, to all it can be for us. This is the sense of the Biranian thesis of the immateriality of forces and of the important idea, already marking the inflection of Biran's thought toward mysticism and toward a philosophy of absolute immanence, of the denial of any possible notion of an absolute foreign force. Henry flags the mystical inflection without developing it; the point for now is that force in Biran names nothing beyond what is given in effort.

The examples could be multiplied indefinitely, since this movement of reduction originates all Biran's analyses. But one must again beware the terminology, for Biran calls "relative" what Henry has been calling "absolute," and "absolute" what precisely falls under the reduction and is merely possible, substantial, ontological, abstract. Speaking of the idea of force and activity, the *Essai* says that it is when conceived as relative before being conceived as absolute that it can furnish the principle of psychology or the science of ourselves. Henry notes in passing that when evidence is at issue Biran uses the same terminology as Henry, speaking for instance of absolute evidence. The inversion is worth holding onto, since it is exactly the kind of terminological trap Henry announced at the section's opening.

So it is the return, effected by the reduction, to an original sphere of certainty that will let science rise on a true base — not a notion but existence itself. Biran locates the origin of science in the actuality of the ego, where the self is an actual and no longer virtual force: there and only there is the origin of science identified with existence itself, not ontological or abstract but real or felt. This phenomenological foundation of the science of the faculties and of all absolute science will moreover be the true object of metaphysics — the title of the first Appendix to the *Essai* — which ceases to be a body of transcendent constructions and identifies itself, as a certain science, with psychology. Biran: one could not contest to a metaphysics thus circumscribed within a wholly psychological field the reality and certainty or evidence of its object; that would indeed be a positive science, that of the facts of intimate sense linked to one another and to a first self-evident fact serving as its base, and objections attacking the reality of a wholly abstract metaphysics could not reach it, since the questions it raised would never leave the enclosure of interior facts.

For Biran, true metaphysics is a psychology. His thought nevertheless does not sink into psychologism, since the psychology it promotes is in reality a transcendental phenomenology, an absolute science with apodictic certainty. Henry adds in a note that many nineteenth-century philosophies do succumb, in particular those claiming Kant, which on psychological questions fall into psychologism no less than the empiricist philosophies they take themselves to oppose. As for the ontology every metaphysics elaborates, in the case of a metaphysics circumscribed to a wholly psychological — that is, phenomenological — field, it is nothing other than a phenomenological ontology. The defense against psychologism rests entirely on the earlier distinction between interior observation and interior experience; take that away and Henry's Biran is Locke's disciple after all.

One now understands what a philosopher like Biran could mean by research into the foundations of psychology. Psychology as a science requires a foundation, and that foundation is furnished by the sphere of existence where transcendental inner experiences are accomplished, the sphere of absolute certainty to which the reduction has led us back. But psychology in its content is constituted by a number of statements and propositions, a series of judgments whose body forms a science. Biran's thesis amounts to the claim that the judgments of psychology are founded judgments, or as he says intuitive judgments — an expression marking that the judgment rests on something anterior and more primitive. That anterior something, perfectly self-sufficient and in no way requiring a judgment to express it in order to be complete, and therefore the absolutely concrete, is immediate intuition or internal apperception, that is, transcendental inner experience. Henry adds a note whose consequence is severe: relative to life, psychology is contingent, and as a science it is an essentially historical being. The science founded on life adds nothing to life. Hence the question about psychology's foundation was indeed the one the *Essai* posed: is there an immediate internal apperception?

The nature of the intuitive judgment is clear from a text on the *cogito*, around which the whole psychology will be built. Before the proposition "I think, I exist" can be expressed by the separate signs "I," "think," "exist," the existence of the self is given by internal apperception or immediate intuition. The intellectual act uniting thought and existence as inseparable attributes of the essence of the subject "self" is an intuitive judgment, which rests on signs; the intuition is independent of them. Henry draws the consequence for the Cartesian literature: the interminable discussions of the *cogito* really concern only the intuitive judgment, the predicative form that overcomes, in the relation of subject and predicate, a division it has itself posited. Intuition, that is transcendental inner experience, knows no such division; it is independent of predicative judgment and of predicative life in general, and escapes in principle all discussion, reasoning, and critique. This disposes of the whole dispute over whether the *cogito* is an inference by relocating the *cogito* beneath the level at which inference and its denial are formulated.

Intuitive judgments nonetheless carry a certainty that reflects the absolute certainty of the primitive fact on which they are founded. The set of these judgments, of which the *cogito* as psychological axiom is the first, constitutes rational psychology, whose content coincides with that of Biran's *Essai*. They are not linked by threads of deduction properly so called; each is in a sense founded directly, so that its truth, though no longer the original truth, escapes the vicissitudes of memory. Biran: the intuitive judgments whose series I could prolong are so many different expressions of the same fact of consciousness. The absence of deductive linkage is a structural feature, not a defect of exposition: each judgment draws its certainty from the same single fact rather than from its predecessors, which is why the memory of earlier steps is not load-bearing.

The first intuitive judgment of personal existence, the *cogito*, is not merely a reflexive judgment; and reflexive judgment is not the psychologist's privilege but a natural judgment which, in natural language, spontaneously expresses natural life — a judgment as old as our existence. Henry closes the section by denying the philosopher any special access. The *cogito* is not a technical achievement of philosophy but the articulation of something everyone already lives.

### § 2. The Transcendental Deduction of the Categories

Henry stipulates the term first. "Categories" names the ideas of force, cause, substance, unity, identity, person, and freedom, which Biran designates as first notions, reflexive abstract ideas, principles, original ideas, and apparently also faculties. Subjective ideology, the science of the faculties, includes the study of the categories as well as of notions like imagination, memory, judgment, and reasoning; Henry notes that Biran, commenting on Leibniz, speaks explicitly of the faculties of the understanding and of forms or categories. The resulting indeterminacy in the extension of "faculty" does not matter and is rather significant, since in Biran the theory of categories runs strictly parallel to the theory of faculties in the narrow sense: both reduce to disclosing the sphere in which all ideas, faculties or categories alike, have their source — the sphere of absolute subjectivity. To deduce the categories means, for Biran, showing that they have a mode of existence prior to the one in which they appear to us as ideas properly so called, and that they find their foundation in that more original mode. Understanding the theory of categories therefore requires a philosophy furnishing a status for that mode of existence.

That mode of existence is subjectivity as a sphere of absolute immanence. Only the idea of transcendental immanence lets us grasp what the category must be for Biran: not a supposed and unfelt activity, but something like a transcendental inner experience. Since the problem of the categories is thus immediately bound up with the status of subjectivity, it is because Biranism is first an ontology of subjectivity that it could solve the problem of the categories — a solution Henry calls a remarkable complement to Kant's theory. Because the subject cannot exercise any of its own faculties without knowing it, any more than it can know one without exercising it, it becomes true to say that the subject knows the categories, a proposition which in Kant's philosophy has no context. The complaint against Kant is precise: Kant's categories are conditions the philosopher posits, not something the subject possesses; the Kantian "I think" that must accompany all representations has no experiential status of its own.

What was needed was to give "transcendental" a radically immanent signification, so that the ensemble of *a priori* conditions of the possibility of experience would no longer float in an indeterminate region and in the transcendence of a quasi-Platonic heaven. What makes experience possible cannot simply be posited as required to satisfy philosophical intelligence, cannot be a supposed activity. To say such activity is felt is to say it exists phenomenologically as an incontestable given, as an experience — or, in Biran's terms, that speculative psychology is at the same time practical. And because it is genuinely transcendental, resting on a sphere of absolute immanence, the Biranian deduction of the categories is not really a deduction but a simple reading of the phenomenological characters of the ego. For the same reason, each category's deduction is independent and refers directly to the ego, just as each intuitive judgment of rational psychology is founded immediately on its corresponding intuition. This paragraph settles the section's title. "Deduction" is retained for its Kantian resonance and then emptied of its Kantian procedure; nothing is derived from anything.

Henry has thus given a first explanation of the relation of internal apperception to the original ideas — the title of a section of the *Essai*, which displays Biran's originality in tying the problem of the categories not to mind or reason but to subjectivity. As the *Essai* says: as soon as one starts from the absolute there is no longer any base; one is outside experience. That the Biranian deduction must be read as a reduction to the sphere of immanence is shown further by Biran's critique of the philosophies against which he defines his own thought, and by his exposition of the deduction of the principal categories. These become subsections (a) and (b).

Biran's critique is aimed at empiricism and rationalism at once. The problem is to define the being of the categories — their nature and the region to which they belong. Empiricism knows only one ontological region, that of transcendent sensible being, in which there exist only facts in the sense of natural phenomena. There is therefore no region proper to the categories, which are nothing but purely artificial ideas of classes or genera, mere collections of modes abstracted from sensations and dependent on the nature of those compared sensations. However abstract such ideas may be, it remains to be understood what power performs the abstraction from the sensible given; Hume's recourse to sentiment is a disguised appeal to subjectivity, of which empiricism can give no theory, since it is an ontological monism claiming to know only one kind of experience and one kind of evidence. Henry notes that Husserl too read the demand for a radical return to subjectivity as the hidden sense of Hume's philosophy. Biran: the ideologists, admitting only one kind of experience relating entirely to representative sensation, absolutely deny the reality of everything non-physical; and elsewhere, that the exclusive type of all clarity or evidence is taken from exterior images. Henry adds in a note that the empiricism Biran refutes is not only the English variety, since a French empiricism deeply influenced the direction of his thought through the critique of it.

Henry then italicizes the crucial correction. Biran rejects empiricism not because it *cannot* account for our experience of the world and the ideas that experience implies, but because it knows only that form of experience — and on the same ground he rejects rationalism. The distinction matters because it removes the dispute from the terrain of explanatory adequacy. Empiricism's failure is not that its derivations do not work; it is that it recognizes only one mode of givenness.

There are, Biran says, two kinds of ideas: abstract general ideas formed by comparing sensible qualities or modifications, which are logical and necessarily collective abstractions; and reflexive abstract ideas, which do not grow poorer as their extension increases, are always universal and simple, and possess a proper and real value of their own. The problem is the status of these reflexive abstract ideas, which are just the categories. It will emerge that rationalism can confer on them no defined and satisfying status, because it too lacks an ontology of subjectivity and so is helpless when the moment comes to determine rigorously the mode of being of innate ideas and of the *a priori*. Against rationalism one must say that all knowledge derives from experience, because the condition of possibility of experience is itself an experience. Because the category was for him precisely an experience — a specific one — Biran could circumscribe an entirely original ontological region which, while being the source of all experience, was no less phenomenologically given and known. The formula "the condition of possibility of experience is itself an experience" is Henry's compressed statement of the entire section, and it is what makes his "empiricism" compatible with his transcendental claims.

Both schools are thereby judged together. Biran asks why the metaphysicians hold that the first and directing notions of cause, substance, unity, identity reside in the soul *a priori*, independent of or prior to experience, while others want them deduced by generalization or abstraction from facts given by exterior experience, that is from sensations. The answer: misrecognizing the character and nature of the primitive facts with which such notions are identical at their real source, and taking them at the degree of generalization to which repeated use of linguistic signs raises them, they cannot recognize their primary character as facts. An earlier passage of the *Mémoire* had explicitly drawn together the doctrine reducing all faculties of the understanding to logical forms or categories and the one seeing in them only the abstract characters of a single transformed sensation. Leibniz and Condillac fall under a single critique whose sense is the demand for an ontology of subjectivity. Registering that these two fall together is the point of the whole subsection: innatism and sensationalism are variants of one error, having lost the level at which categories are facts.

Absent such an ontology, the category can only be an abstract term — as it literally is in empiricism, but no less, Henry insists, in rationalism. As a condition of the possibility of experience charged with synthesizing it, the category, if not taken within an immanent theory, becomes a transcendent term = x, like any other transcendent term = x. One can indeed show the necessity of admitting it, but one has thereby built only an explanatory hypothesis; one has left the sphere of certainty that is subjectivity, one erects theories of knowledge whose content can only fall under the phenomenological reduction, and one has not yet grasped the conditions a transcendental phenomenology of knowledge must satisfy. The result is that between the empiricist method, which abstracts the categories from sensible experience, and the reflexive method, which merely realizes in a mind = x those same categories it has likewise learned by reading them off sensible experience as what is required to account for it, there is no essential difference. The charge is that rationalism reaches its categories by the same route as empiricism and then relocates them; the list of categories is still read off the world.

Two texts of the *Mémoire* confirm this, one on the empiricist method, the other on the method rationalism actually employs. Bacon moved from a real distinction between certain effective products of intelligence to a hypothetical division of the faculties or powers supposed to form them; so the encyclopedic order did not follow from a real and *a priori* division of the soul's faculties, but rather that division was conventionally established *a posteriori*, on the basis of the encyclopedic order itself. And: all the metaphysical distinctions or precisions one might make within these standpoints — Henry notes the reference is to Kant, Descartes, and Leibniz, just examined in that order — refer only to an abstract world of possibles where science is placed before existence itself. As soon as one seeks to rejoin them to the world of realities they remain inapplicable, and their authors, returning to the first data of experience, are forced to employ these as they result from the oldest habits, unable to go further back. So metaphysics, claiming the right to judge experience by giving it laws, instead receives its own from experience, bends to its customs, and sanctions rather than rectifies them. Henry lets this do heavy work. The transcendental philosopher who claims to legislate for experience is in fact ratifying the sedimented habits of ordinary experience, having no independent access to the conditions he posits.

What Biran asks instead is that we identify science with existence, understanding that existence is already a science — not an imperfect and provisional one, but the origin of all science and of truth. The source of experience does not lie behind it; experience is its own origin. Biran's reproach to Descartes, Leibniz, and Kant is that in them the source of all reality is realized outside all consciousness. Hence in Leibniz the analysis of metaphysical propositions is identical with the geometrical method of resolving equations, and in Kantianism a mediation must intervene in the process of bringing the categories to light, which are hardly known but rather posited indirectly. Henry adds the pointed note: this is why Kant needed a guiding thread to discover the list of categories. The remark is decisive for the section's claim that Biran's procedure is not a deduction. A philosophy whose categories are immanent needs no guiding thread, because it does not have to hunt for them.

Lacking an ontology of subjectivity, the categories can only float in that region elevated above all experience, so that the final critique of rationalism is Biran's: the soul has the power to perform reflected acts and see what is in itself; more, it sees what is outside only by means of certain forms or ideas that are in it alone; but there is no immediate or actual apperception of these forms or ideas, and the soul can grasp them only in the intuition of things. Though aimed at Leibniz, this text has for Henry a general and properly infinite significance, since it displays how the deep demand of Biranian philosophy is for a transcendental phenomenology of knowledge resting in turn on an ontology of subjectivity. Biran summarizes his own critique of the modern systems: the point was only to show the origin these systems had in certain excursions of the mind beyond the limits of the fact, which alone can give science a real base. The Leibniz text is well chosen because it concedes everything except the one thing at issue — the soul possesses the forms, and still has no immediate apperception of them.

The problem at the center of the discussion is the *a priori*. In Biran's text the term is pejorative, synonymous with the "absolute" Henry glossed earlier. Speaking of metaphysics in the first appendix to the *Essai*, Biran says one can victoriously attack *a priori* the reality of its object as a science of the substance of the soul or of any absolute and *a priori* principle. The *a priori* designates what, following a tendency to the absolute, is posited and realized outside all possible observation, in that region elevated above all experience.

What then does Biran's critique of the *a priori* mean? It is essentially directed against admitting a transcendent term = x at the origin of our experience. Certainly what stands at that origin cannot be the content or object of our experience, since that content or object presupposes a condition of possibility which is precisely the *a priori*. But if the *a priori* is something we posit — to account, say, for the possibility of experience — it becomes in turn a transcendent term and can no longer render the services expected of it. Experience cannot be conceived without an *a priori* that makes it possible, but that *a priori* can render experience accessible to us only if it is situated within ourselves and coincides with the very being of our intentionality. What is at issue is not the idea of the *a priori* but its phenomenological status: Biran's critique amounts to saying that the *a priori* cannot be a transcendent term, known or unknown, situated before or behind us, but belongs to the sphere of absolute immanence. This is the section's most exportable argument, and it is directed at transcendental philosophy as such: whatever is posited to explain experience thereby acquires the status of an object and forfeits its explanatory office.

Interpreted within an ontology of subjectivity and understood in the light of its own internal demands, the idea of the *a priori* is finally admitted into Biranism. Treating the synthetic judgments that are the intuitive judgments of rational psychology, Biran declares that one could also say these synthetic judgments are *a priori*, not because they are independent of all experience, but because they emanate directly from the primitive fact of existence. More: related to the primitive fact of existence, and so finally situated in its true place, the *a priori* is what alone lets us understand that existence can be a science and that the being of the ego consists in an original knowing. Having rejected innateness, having declared that the supposition of something innate is the death of analysis — meaning that admitting at the origin of things, as principle, a transcendent term unknown in itself is only a recourse to a *deus ex machina*, whatever name, mind or matter, one gives it — Biran subjects innateness to the reduction and recovers it in the sphere of immanence, endowed with its true sense, at the heart of what for him is ultimate reality, the phenomenological transcendental ego. And then an infinite question arises which, had we the means to receive it, would lead into a region where the very essence of ipseity can be contemplated: if the self is not innate to itself, what could be? Henry marks the question as beyond his present resources, but it names the theme — self-affection as the ego's being given to itself without having produced itself — that his mature work makes central.

Turning to the deduction proper, Henry states that it takes on in Biranism a wholly particular significance without equivalent in any other philosophy. To deduce a category is to determine its status in a phenomenological ontology, to disclose the mode of existence originally its own — not to expatiate on the necessity of admitting it as a condition of the possibility of experience. The category will indeed prove a condition of possibility, but in a wholly different sense: that without subjectivity there is neither world nor experience for us. The category is thus original truth itself, and that truth is the condition of possibility of all experience, the original ontological possibility; but this original ontological possibility is not itself anything "possible" — it is a fact, it is given, it is a transcendental inner experience. Ontological possibility is subjectivity, and therefore has not to be deduced but simply read and known in the original sphere of existence that is its own. The deduction is in reality a reduction: to deduce a category is to reduce its being to what it originally is, this time irreducibly. Hold onto the paradox stated here, since it recurs throughout the book: the condition of possibility is not a possibility but an actuality, because the mode of being of the immanent is to be given rather than to be entailed.

Before being deduced — before its being has been reduced — the category is a category of the thing, appearing in the element of transcendent being as a character of that being. It cannot maintain itself in such a situation, with a status not originally its own. It must be deduced, that is, a reduction must intervene in which its being undergoes a transformation and passes from the sphere of the transcendent to that of immanence and subjectivity.

Henry works this through causality. It is first a causality of the thing, a causality in the thing. But since the thing is only a spatial and sensible determination, there is no room in it for causality, which is thus only an obscure force hidden behind it. The thing's behavior, moved by an unknown agent, has a magical air; one may make an effort to reduce that behavior to its visible and objective characters, but one has not thereby exorcised the idea of cause, which remains philosophy's remorse. The phenomenological reduction performed by empiricism and positivism is true on its own plane, that of transcendent being: it shows that on such a plane there is no room for the idea of causality, and that all the representations this idea can arouse there could only be fantastic representations of obscure forces and magical powers. Henry's concession is genuine. Hume is right about the world; his error is confining the inquiry to the world.

The idea of causality, in Biran's terms, is homogeneous with none of the sensible ideas under which we represent exterior phenomena. Whence this lack of homogeneity, Biran asks; why does the existence of a productive force or efficient cause present itself so obstinately to our mind while the cause remains veiled to imagination and eternally hides its face and manner of operating? The problem of the origin of the idea of causality is an ontological problem. If the idea does not take its source in transcendent being, then accounting for it requires disclosing another region of being — an ontology able to receive the original being of causality. The deduction of the categories is possible only within an ontology of subjectivity. The obstinacy of the idea is Henry's evidence: an idea with no possible source in the region where it is applied must have a source elsewhere.

Absent such an ontology, the critique of philosophies limited to transcendent being — as if it could suffice to itself, without raising the problem of its possibility and foundation — cannot be fully developed. One will show, for instance, that the manifold of sensible intuition can be unified in a consciousness only if submitted to the category of causality, which then appears as a condition without which there would be no possible world for us. But from the fact that the idea of causality is indispensable to constituting a human world and to the existence of human experience, it in no way follows that man possesses such an idea. Positing causality as an *a priori* condition of experience does not yet supply the idea's origin. There is a world for us if that world is submitted to the category of causality — but whence do we have a category such as causality, where do we get this idea, since it is not from the manifold of sensible intuition? The idea comes from elsewhere, it is *a priori*; but what is this elsewhere, and by what mystery is the *a priori* at our disposal so that a world can exist for us? And for us even to form the project of showing that a world is possible only under the category of causality, must we not already possess the essential thing, namely that category and its idea? This is the argument the section has been building to, and it is a genuine objection to Kant rather than a restatement of Henry's premises: the transcendental deduction presupposes possession of what it undertakes to justify.

It is therefore the problem of the origin of the idea of causality, not that of its reflexive implication as a universal condition of experience, that must first orient a genuine deduction. Until the internal demands of that problem are met, the worst difficulties arise. The world of experience exists in fact and is a real world; but what makes it possible must be no less real, failing which the world of experience would itself cease to be real and become a pure possible. Either condition and experience are both possible, and then there is still nothing; or both are real, and only then does a human experience and a human world exist. One cannot take the reality of the world alone as starting point and place its condition of possibility in the possible. And even if one thought oneself entitled to do so, one would have no means of determining that condition of possibility in general, whose being would float somewhere outside the real without one's being able to say quite where; one would have no means of naming it and calling it, for example, causality. The naming problem is the sharpest form of the objection. A merely posited condition cannot be identified as *this* condition rather than another.

In truth it is causality that knows causality. If we need no recourse to empirical judgments to form its idea by abstraction, and if the deduction dispenses with any guiding thread, that is because causality, like all the other categories, belongs to an order of more intimate facts constituting the thinking and acting being in a relation of immediate knowledge with itself. The problem of the categories can find a solution only in an ontology of subjectivity. In a text explicitly on causality, Biran declares that the whole mystery of *a priori* notions vanishes before the torch of interior experience, which teaches that the idea of cause has its primitive and unique type in the feeling of the self, identified with that of effort. Since Biran identifies the being of subjectivity with that of the ego, the deduction of the categories terminates at once in the ego as the absolute being from which the categories draw their ultimate origin.

Henry now forestalls an objection: does this reduction bracket the world or withdraw it from the action of the categories, since there is no room in the world for the being of the categories? No — because the world is a world lived by the ego and not separated from it, so that it is not a dead world either but has a life, the very one the ego lends it. The life of the world is the ego's life, and the world is accordingly one where causes and forces intersect, a world with zones that are centers of interest, attraction, or repulsion, strong or weak points, having a unity of action or reaction, a power I can neither ignore nor always defy. The world is penetrated by my causality, which dominates it even in its refusals, even in the resistance it opposes. Things have their category, their manner of acting — that is, of giving themselves to us, their manner of being for the ego. Because the ego is causality, force, unity, identity, freedom, things are realities and individualities and have an autonomous power proper to them that defines them in our eyes. The world is the same because I am the same.

The world's magical being is finally irreducible, because the world is a human world. The world of science, a world without causes, is only an abstract world. There is no purely scientific world, since at the limit such a world would be nothing but nothingness. Positivism's world is a surface world, a parade of images that could never affect a man or do him the least harm, a world without relation to man. If such a world subsists in its bloodless and exhausted reality, it is because it still conceals something it cannot account for, referring to powers of the ego. The truth of weight — what lets me speak of it even from a strictly scientific standpoint — is that I can fall and that contact with the ground will be a blow that strikes me. And how could positivism, or any scientific theory, account for that contact, for the presence of a world to the ego? That presence is founded on a transcendental relation, and the world it delivers is not first a scientific structure. Scientific relations are founded on other relations, and the primitive world these compose is the world of men. The *cosmos* is the ensemble of the elements, of what things originally are for us. What makes water water is that I cannot hold it between my fingers and that if I plunge in I sink and, lacking an appropriate technique, risk being lost. The liquid medium means for me the end of the reign of solidity, the absence of ground and of any fixed point of support. The pleasure of contemplating the sea does not go without a secret anguish; every thing bears at the heart of its being the image of a human destiny; the world is traversed by a life that is mine: I am the life of the world. This is the passage where Henry's own voice displaces the exposition of Biran, and the elements are not chosen for color. Water is defined by what my body cannot do with it, which anticipates the thesis that the world's articulation is the correlate of my powers — established in Chapter II and applied to resistance in Chapter III.

By giving a subjective theory of the categories and identifying subjectivity with the ego, Biran barred any interpretation of the categories as rules of reflective thought intervening only in the constitution of science, rational language, or logic. The categories are powers of the ego, the fundamental modes of life, the first determinations of existence. When one speaks of the ideas of causality, unity, force, one must therefore beware, since two very different things may be meant. First, an idea of causality in which causality is the theme of thought: we form it as we form the idea of the sea, the proletariat, or the state, and use it to establish a causal relation between two phenomena and, step by step, to constitute a science. Such an idea certainly exists, but far from being identifiable with the category of causality, it is merely founded on it. The category is not an idea at all but a way of living the world, a structure of natural life. The theory of the categories in Biranism is thus no longer a theory of reason or the understanding but a theory of existence.

At the same time, because for Biran existence is ego, because the ego is subjectivity, and because he gives an explicit theory of subjectivity, Biranism avoided the reef on which many later thoughts founder — thoughts which, on the pretext of fleeing intellectualism and lacking a theory of existence, can only resort to descriptions and mere suggestions and finally sink into literature. Sinking into existence, the Biranian deduction appeals not to an indeterminate region of being — one drawing all its prestige and powers from its very indeterminacy — but to a determinate sphere of absolute existence, the sphere of transcendental immanence.
