The jab at existentialism is methodological rather than tonal: a philosophy of existence without an ontology of subjectivity has no criterion for its descriptions and therefore cannot distinguish insight from evocation.

The deduction thus rests on a region we know, since it is the original site of all knowing, and each category's deduction leads back to that sphere of subjectivity, that is, to the ego. Henry has shown for causality how, as an idea, it is deduced from an original power coinciding with the being of our existence: to deny the feeling or interior knowledge of power, Biran says, is to deny one's entire existence. The deduction of the idea of force displays clearly what the Biranian deduction consists in, and how the idea's being is borrowed from something more original that is the ego's own being: the idea of force can originally be taken only in the consciousness of the subject making the effort, and even when wholly abstracted from the fact of consciousness, transported outside and entirely displaced from its natural base, it always keeps the imprint of its origin. Because it bears the imprint of an origin it does not contain, the world has the magical character making it a human world — and this is first what lets it exist as a world at all, as the ego's world.

The deduction of the ideas of unity and identity has the same sense: every idea of one, of the same, is essentially contained in the primitive fact or self, of which it is a form. The object's unity is derived; original unity is the ego's, whose reproduction coincides with apperceiving itself as one in that reproduction. Henry glosses that the ego's apperception of the unity of its phenomenological being is accomplished on the plane of absolute immanence, to the point of being constitutive — not in the usual sense of a transcendent constitution, but in a radically immanent sense — of the ego's very being, which is thus a transcendental phenomenological being. Biran: the self reproduces or apperceives itself constantly in effort under the same unitary form. The world's unity can only be founded on the ego's: remove the self and there is no unity anywhere. As for freedom, its idea is no less derived.

The world is thus invested by the ego's powers; the category reigning in it is genuinely deduced, brought there from another region, that of subjectivity. In that region is situated the original category, of which one can hardly say it is still an idea: as Biran says of freedom, this idea is at bottom only an immediate feeling. Biranism thus gives an immanent theory of the categories and in particular of freedom, which leads it to reject the tangle of endless discussions about the idea of freedom, discussions that cannot touch the being of the ego or the infinite and free sphere of existence that is its own. Henry quotes Biran's dismissal: to put freedom in question is to put in question the feeling of existence or of the self, from which it does not differ, and any question about this primitive fact becomes frivolous by the very fact of being made a question. This is Henry's model for how immanent theories dispose of classical problems — not by answering them but by showing that their formulation presupposes what they interrogate.

Only an immanent theory of the categories can explain that they are genuinely in our possession, that we can know and recognize them originally at the transcendental level, and consequently that we can know and recognize things. Were the category not immanent it would be of no use in knowing things, since we would not know it itself immediately. Because he grasped the necessity of the category's immanence absolutely, and gave that immanence a radical interpretation leading him to make the powers of knowledge and the conditions of experience the very being of the concrete ego, Biranism not only takes its place among the perennial philosophies but advances further, to the interpretation of original truth as existence.

Henry now registers a genuine breakdown. It is not easy, after this, to understand Biran's deduction of the categories of substance and necessity, since he assigns them a foundation that is no longer the original being of the subjective ego but transcendent being — indeed the foundation of all transcendent being in general. The idea of substance seems at first to be deduced from a concrete mode of the ego's existence, namely effort, its origin thus being the same as that of the idea of force. But we are warned that the origin of the idea of substance is mixed, that it may equally lie not in effort but in the term resisting effort, and it becomes increasingly clear that the idea of substance is finally deduced not from the ego's unity but from that substratum which is the resisting continuum. The polemic against the Cartesian *cogito* and against calling its being a substance, the rejection of substantialism in general, and the appeal to activist philosophies like Fichte's, Schelling's, and Tracy's all accentuate this movement and forbid Biran any assimilation of substance to the primitive fact of the *cogito*. Substance and *cogito* are opposed as the being of the world is opposed to that of the ego. The origin of the idea of substance is the world's alterity, or rather the foundation of that alterity, the resisting term on which everything peopling the world is built. The "resisting continuum" enters here as a term of art and will carry the argument of Chapters II and III; note that Henry introduces it as the point where Biran's own deduction breaks its pattern.

That the category of substance takes as its origin what, within transcendent being, is the foundation of all transcendent being is not, Henry says, a mark of inferiority relative to the other categories deriving from the transcendental sphere. In Biranism the existence of the resisting continuum enjoys the same absolute certainty as that of subjectivity and of effort. The world is as certain as my own existence. There is indeed a phenomenological reduction in Biranism, but unlike what some commentators on Husserl suppose, it does not put the being of the world in question; it seeks rather to circumscribe what in such being is original and enjoys genuine certainty. The term of effort, the resisting continuum, plays that role, and Henry cross-references Chapter II, p. 104, for the reason the resisting term belongs to the transcendentally reduced sphere of certainty. The modes of sensibility — colors, sounds, odors — which rest on the resisting continuum to constitute the sensible world, alone fall under the reduction, while the resisting continuum remains within certainty just as does the pure being of the ego reduced to effort. The primitive fact is in reality a primitive duality, both of whose terms enjoy an irreducible certainty; so it is understandable at first sight that Biran could think of using either term as the foundation of his original ideas, and that he rested the categories of substance and necessity on the transcendent term that founds the world. The correction to the standard reading of the reduction is worth marking: Henry's reduction is a discrimination within being, not a suspension of the world's existence.

The deduction of substance has the merit of bringing to light one of Biranism's riches, the presence within it of a kind of ontological proof revealing an element of absolute certainty at the heart of transcendent being. But the problem of that element's certainty has no bearing on the problem of the category's origin. The category could in no case find its foundation in transcendent being, for then we would be deprived of any means of intuiting the milieu where it is supposed to appear, and it would remain forever unknown to us. To read the category of substance in the element of transcendent being one must already possess it, if only to recognize it. And to say one must already possess it is to say that the category's being belongs to the sphere of transcendental immanence in general, and that a deduction of the categories can have philosophical sense only as a return to that sphere, the only region where anything like an absolute beginning is possible. Henry's correction of Biran is thus internal: Biran's own criterion of recognition refutes his derivation of substance.

The uncertainties in this otherwise rigorous deduction, when it meets substance and necessity, connect to more general difficulties concerning a problem that exceeds that of the category and that not only Biranism but nearly all philosophies have left in total ontological obscurity: the problem of passivity. Henry defers it — Chapter VI is devoted to it — and proceeds with the study of the philosophical presuppositions of the analysis of the body, taking it as established that reduction to immanence represents the essential doctrine of the *Essai* on the category. This standpoint is justified not only because it is Biran's own for nearly all the original ideas, and because Biran himself sensed the insufficiency of his deduction of necessity, and because the deduction of substance correctly interpreted has the same sense as the others; it is necessary in order to grasp the deep unity of Biranian ontology and in particular its theory of the ego, the prelude to its theory of the body. Registering here that passivity is the fault line saves the reader from surprise in Chapter VI: Henry's eventual break with Biran is announced at the exact point where Biran's deduction first failed.

### § 3. The Theory of the Ego and the Problem of the Soul

Biranism is one of the rare philosophies, perhaps the only one, undertaking to give an ontological theory of the self. What is at issue is not the ego's characters or properties, nor its relations to its biological or social milieu, its time, or whatever reality philosophers, psychologists, and scientists please to consider and most often surreptitiously erect into an absolute. This is not psychological, sociological, historical, or literary description, still less theories containing and justifying the presuppositions of such descriptions; it is an ontological analysis of the *being* of the ego — of what makes a self a self, the essence of ipseity in it. Such analysis terminates in the identification of the being of the ego with that of subjectivity.

Biran determined the being of subjectivity rigorously by its appearing, and determined that appearing in turn by its radical opposition to the being of ideas or notions, images or things — to exterior being in general, whose appearing, and so precisely whose being, resides in its very exteriority. This was the result of the problematic tracing back to the foundation of a double observation, that is, to the disclosure of two irreducible modes of manifestation. Determining the ego's being by the internal structure of a mode of manifestation has genuine ontological significance: what it posits is not "something," a being in the ordinary or philosophical sense, an entity, since this "something" is constituted rather by its "how," by the internal structure of its mode of manifestation. The reversal is exact and easy to miss: the *how* does not describe an antecedently given *what*; there is no what apart from the how.

Designating the ego's being as identical with subjectivity's therefore means that for Biran the self is not an entity. And because the self is not an entity, the opposition of self and non-self cannot be defined in ontic terms either, since an opposition necessarily holds between homogeneous elements. Like the self, the non-self must as such have an ontological significance. Henry accordingly rejects the received reading: the traditional interpretation of the Biranian opposition of self and non-self as that of effort and the real resisting it is inadmissible. Effort is still *something*, and so is the real it measures itself against. What constitutes the self's ipseity is the *being* of effort, its original mode of presence to itself; and what lets the resisting continuum give itself as other — as that which is the other — already, prior to its resistance and independently of it, is its mode of manifestation, its exteriority. The opposition of self and non-self is between the being of effort and the being of the world; it is an ontological opposition. This correction is the section's first substantive result and it forestalls the standard picture of Biran as the philosopher of a psychological struggle between will and obstacle. Otherness does not arise from resistance; resistance presupposes an already-given otherness constituted by exteriority.

The theory of subjectivity is completed in Biranism by the theory of faculties and categories; but for a philosophy identifying the being of subjectivity with that of the ego, subjective ideology is at the same time a theory of the ego. Conversely, that the subjective theory of the categories terminates in the ego as the being from which the categories draw their origin is as much a proof as a consequence of the theory of the subjective ego. The deduction of the categories offers itself, precisely because of the ego's central role within it, as a decisive confirmation of the thesis that the ego belongs to the sphere of absolute immanence.

Henry then removes an equivocation from a formula quoted earlier: the self reproduces and apperceives itself constantly in effort under the same unitary form. One might ask whether the self's being, giving itself to itself in this reproduction, is not submitted to the category of unity as *thought* by it, subsumed under it like any other being of the world. The philosophical direction of the Biranian deduction absolutely rejects such a reading. Since the category is identified in its original being with the ego's own being, the ego can no longer be an object known by means of the category, or constituted by it in any way. The deduction has the capital consequence of tearing the ego's being away from the sphere of transcendent being in general, which is always the product of a constitution. The ego is *not* constituted and cannot be, so long as it coincides in its being with the category, that is, with the power of constitution in general — so long as it is itself such a power. Registering this argument is worth the effort: the ego escapes constitution not by being exempted from it but by being identical with the constituting power, so that nothing remains to constitute it.

The assertion of a transcendence of the ego thereby appears groundless and, since it destroys the character constituting the very essence of ipseity, frivolous. What constitutes the essence of ipseity, of egoity as Biran says, is the interiority of immediate presence to self. The *Essai*: the self must have begun to exist for itself. If the ego is transcendent, it follows that it does not exist for itself but only for something other than itself — a power = x, a transcendental milieu, a purely logical subject, nothingness, or whatever name one gives it. But is interiority not a condition *sine qua non* for the concept of ego to have sense? If the ego's being did not belong to absolute immanence, nothing would let me designate as mine rather than another's this ego now belonging to transcendent being; and conversely, nothing would prevent another ego from slipping into the sphere of absolute immanence from which mine has just been expelled, so that Biran's words, evidently reserved for the immanent ego, would apply as well to the transcendent one: I could doubt whether, when I feel or apperceive my individual existence, it is not another being existing in my place. The absurdity is the point. A transcendent ego cannot secure mineness, and mineness is what the concept of ego was supposed to name. Henry is running against Sartre's *Transcendence of the Ego* here without naming it, and the argument is a preview of his mature objection that any ego constituted at a distance from itself is anonymous.

From the ego's absolute immanence it follows that it is identified in its being with life itself, instead of being an abstract generic term, the heading of a rubric under which one might file the phenomena psychology traditionally refers to the self as opposed to those it refers to the external world. Biran: the self which exists or apperceives itself interiorly as one, simple, identical, is not abstracted from sensations as what is common or general in them.

A further consequence, in Biran's eyes, is that self-knowledge must take on the characters of that sphere, which determine it as a sphere of absolute certainty. From ego to ego there is room in such a philosophy neither for bad faith nor for lying nor for deception, precisely because from ego to ego there is no distance, no possible constitution. Biran: the only modifications or operations attributable to the self are those it actually attributes to itself in the fact of consciousness. Because the ego is subjectivity, it draws its self-knowledge from a perfectly transparent source, learning in the great school of consciousness that does not deceive. The exclusion of bad faith is a real and contestable commitment, and Henry states it without softening: self-opacity, on this account, is not a possibility of the immanent sphere but a sign that one has left it.

The ego is not transcendent; it is, Biran says in the very terms Husserl will use for the transcendental ego, closest to us, or rather it is ourselves. There is indeed a transcendent ego, and a passage of the *Essai* on the idea of force indicates how such an ego might be constituted: to the notion of force isolated from the consciousness of our own force there always mingles, however abstract the notion, a confused feeling of that proper force constitutive of the self, which the mind tries to separate from it but which mingles with it despite us.

This idea of force, which Henry showed gives the human world its magical character, concerns more particularly, within that world, the ego that is now an existent. It is constitutive of that ego's being, for the transcendent ego is, amid the real or imaginary world, only an object more magical than the others, an occult force that would be more threatening to me than the powers of nature if I did not in truth hold it at my disposal — since I never entirely forget that it is constituted and that it is I, the original transcendental ego, who lends it the powers and designs by which I can only pretend to be moved. Henry notes that the transcendent ego at issue is obviously my own, not another's. But the original transcendental ego is no longer constituted, and that it escapes the jurisdiction of the categories is implied in the *Mémoire*'s critique of Locke and Descartes for positing the faculties as permanent forms under which the sensing and thinking subject subsequently apperceives its own existence or represents foreign existences — thus realizing them by separating them from the self, which could then only be represented by their means, constituted, transcendent. Henry adds a note quoting Biran's summary: if it suffices to look within ourselves to have the idea of being, substance, cause, the one, the same, then each of these ideas takes its immediate origin in the feeling of the self; and in showing that all reflexive and supposedly innate ideas are only the primitive fact of consciousness analyzed and expressed in its various characters, one shows that these ideas have an origin, since the self or individual personality has one.

Henry now draws the section's central thesis. The categories' belonging to the sphere of absolute immanence, which is also the ego's sphere, brings us to understand the fundamental relation between the ego and ontological knowledge. Experience presupposes a condition of possibility which is ontological knowledge itself, and the analysis of the categories discloses the structure of that knowledge. Philosophy begins by putting in question a knowledge without which there would be nothing for us. But philosophy deserves the name of first philosophy only when it carries that problematic as far as it can, and deliberately assumes the task of rigorously determining the *being* of ontological knowledge. Biran's answer to this fundamental problem: the ego is the being of ontological knowledge. In a text Henry calls perhaps one of the most important the philosophical tradition has handed down, Biran says first that the feeling of the self is the primitive fact of knowledge, then glosses: man properly perceives or knows nothing except insofar as he is conscious of his personal individuality, or insofar as his own existence is a fact for himself — insofar, finally, as he is a self. Note how the thesis differs from the Kantian one it superficially resembles. The claim is not that self-consciousness accompanies knowledge but that self-consciousness *is* the being of knowledge; ontological knowledge does not belong to the ego, it is the ego.

Here Henry's proposed interpretation of Biranian ipseity — which one might have thought somewhat exceeded the letter and spirit of the work — becomes inevitable. The self can be understood as the condition of all knowledge only insofar as it is not "something," not an entity, but precisely the condition and the very element of knowledge, the ontological element of pure manifestation. And this manifestation must be understood in its most original structure, as not coinciding with the manifestation of exteriority but rather excluding it from itself while founding it. Because the ego gives itself to itself in a transcendental inner experience — or rather because it *is* the very fact of so giving itself, because its structure is that experience's structure, its substance and its own phenomenality, what Henry has elsewhere called the fundamental ontological event of auto-affection — it realizes in itself the first condition of experience of the world and the effectiveness of our access to things. The relation "excludes while founding" is Henry's formula for the whole architecture: immanence is not one region alongside transcendence but what makes transcendence possible by being categorically unlike it.

This is why, immediately after positing the identity of the ego's being with that of ontological knowledge, Biran determines that being by the way it gives itself to us, and further concerns himself with defining the *sui generis* mode in which this original self-donation, the very phenomenon of the ego and of subjectivity, is accomplished. This being, he says, is neither a phenomenon nor an object that represents itself; it is an interior *sui generis* fact, doubtless very evident for every reflecting being, but requiring to be apperceived by means of its own special sense. The ego's being thus coincides with original truth itself, which is, if one likes, the self-knowledge of ontological knowledge, that is, its foundation, its true and subjective being. Hence there is no question of proving this fact, which itself serves as foundation for all proofs and all truths of fact. Henry supports the identity with a further note quoting Biran's statement of his general project: to demonstrate that there is a real mode *sui generis*, unique in its kind, wholly founded in the subject of sensation which is constituted as such by that very mode; that this mode can subsist and have of itself the character of a fact of consciousness without being actually and indivisibly united to any passive affection of sensibility or any exterior representation; and that in it is found, along with the feeling of individual personality, the special origin of all the first ideas of cause, force, unity, identity, and substance. Henry comments that ontological knowledge is here unequivocally a real being, and that being is the ego's.

Only when formulated within a problematic of subjectivity does the identification of the ego's being with that of ontological knowledge take on philosophical significance. Outside that context, determining the ego as the being of ontological knowledge has merely formal value and becomes analogous to the Kantian thesis that the "I think" must be able to accompany all our representations. That thesis constitutes neither a genuine theory of the ego nor — because it lets the original essence of presence as self-presence escape — a sufficient interpretation of the nature of ontological knowledge. On that view the ego is only a logical, purely formal subject, which the designation "nothingness" would suit better than "being." By a strange paradox, deepening ontological knowledge, which is related to or even identified with the ego, yields nothing about the ego's being. What Kantianism lacks — an ontology of subjectivity — is what constitutes the argument of Biranian thought; and within that ontology the thesis that the ego is the being of ontological knowledge no longer makes it a mere form but founds the possibility of that knowledge while determining it as the very being of life and of concrete personal existence. The paradox is stated carefully and is worth holding: the more thoroughly Kant develops the doctrine of the transcendental unity of apperception, the more completely the ego's being drops out, because the analysis operates entirely at the level of what conditions objects.

Constituting the world is not the work of an impersonal activity detached from an individual then reduced to empirical status; it coincides with apprehending the world, it is our way of living it, and only within that life do we know it. Ontological knowledge is individual knowledge; each individual's being is the light of the world and, more deeply, as original truth, the light of that light. The rejection of an impersonal transcendental subject is not incidental. It is the ground of Henry's later insistence that constitution is always someone's, and it is what makes the body's entry into the transcendental sphere possible at all.

Henry now establishes the ego's absolute immanence a second way, from Biran's analysis of the soul. This will show that if the ego's original being cannot be a transcendent being, neither may it be assimilated to a transcendent term = x — another way of affirming its immanence.

The problematic of the soul announces itself in the discussion of the Cartesian *cogito*. As a transcendental inner experience in which the ego's existence is immediately given to itself, the *cogito* is recognized by Biran as the very foundation of philosophy, and Cartesianism, having disclosed it, is the mother doctrine. The *cogito* affirms the phenomenological unity of the being of the ego and that of subjectivity, which Biran with Descartes calls thought or apperception — naturally to be understood as transcendental inner apperception. Biran: the simple proposition "I think," identical with "I exist for myself," states the primitive fact, the phenomenal link between the self and thought or apperception, such that the subject begins and continues to exist for itself only insofar as it begins and continues to apperceive or feel its existence, that is, to think. Discussion can bear only on the formulation of the primitive fact and on the deductive appearance it risks acquiring — which is precisely an appearance.

The critique begins when the soul enters Cartesianism to designate the being of the self identified with thought. Henry insists on the originality of this critique and warns against confusing it with the usual objections to the Cartesian *cogito*, particularly those of classical French philosophy. The risk is heightened because Biran seems to, and does, reproach Descartes with substantialism — admittedly the most classical and banal of critiques. But that reproach was directed at Descartes by philosophers inspired by Kant, notably the French neo-Kantians; and it would be surprising, after everything said about subjectivity and Biran's isolation, if the *Essai*'s critiques resembled the classical ones. If Henry's analyses are right, they should be not merely different but opposed. Henry's method here is worth noting: identical conclusions ("Descartes was wrong to call the ego a substance") can rest on incompatible grounds, and only the ground settles the philosophical meaning.

The *cogito*'s truth is not impaired by affirming that the ego, whose existence is identified with thought's, is a real being. On the contrary, in Biran's eyes the ontological realism of the Cartesian *cogito* is what makes its truth and depth. Where classical philosophy reproaches Cartesianism for passing from the *cogito* as pure phenomenon of thought to the affirmation of that thought's being as the self's being, Biran, following Descartes' authentic teaching, holds first that pure thought, because it has an original mode of revelation that is not the mode of manifestation of things, also has an original being which, though different from transcendent being, is no less real; and second that this phenomenologically determined being is the very being of the ego. Where then is the critique?

There is indeed an illegitimate passage in the philosophical movement of the *cogito*, from a true to a false conception. But for Biran that passage is no longer from pure thought — totally indeterminate anyway in a classical philosophy that can only repeat that mind is not the thing, that it is absent, or at best, seeking to exceed this purely negative determination, posit it as nothingness — to a real ontological determination. It now runs from the being of the ego, originally and correctly interpreted as that of subjectivity and transcendental inner experience, to positing the ego as an element of *transcendent* being. This is all Biran reproaches in Descartes' soul: its determination as a transcendent term = x rather than as absolute subjectivity, as the ego's being affirmed in its radical immanence. If Descartes was wrong to call the ego a soul and a substance, it was not because he thereby made it a being, but because that being is no longer the one Cartesianism itself had, in its infinite depth, primitively recognized — a being phenomenologically determined by an appearing whose absolute originality had necessarily to lead a phenomenological ontology to circumscribe an absolutely original being, namely the being of the ego. Hold onto the inversion: for Henry the classical objection is that Descartes claimed too much being for the *cogito*, while Biran's is that he claimed the wrong kind and thereby lost the *cogito*'s own being.

The critique intervenes when the *cogito* believes it can posit the real and absolute existence of the soul or thinking thing. But Henry has already indicated that in Biran "absolute" does not apply to the sphere of immanence Henry calls absolute; it designates what no longer belongs to that sphere, what is outside it and so takes its place within transcendent being. The syllogism Biran imputes to Descartes runs: I think, I exist for myself; but everything that thinks or knows it exists exists absolutely as substance or thinking thing, outside thought; therefore I exist substantially. Henry underlines the essential words: "absolutely" and "outside thought." He adds a note insisting Biran does not reproach Descartes with having made a syllogism at all; the *cogito*'s mode of expression is not decisive, and one asks of it only that it make us conceive what the *cogito* is in itself, which alone matters and which is neither a reasoning nor a proposition nor anything of the kind.

The critique of the Cartesian *cogito* therefore has exactly the same sense as all the Biranian analyses so far: the principle is always the demand for a return to a sphere of absolute immanence as the milieu of existence of an absolute being, the summons to perform a phenomenological reduction whose destructive work alone can disclose the fundamental structure that is at once concrete existence, ontological knowledge, and finally what makes philosophy possible, the element of the science of the human mind. Biran: Descartes, abruptly crossing the whole interval separating the fact of personal existence or the feeling of the self from the absolute notion of a thinking thing, opens the door to all doubts about the objective nature of that thing, which is not the self.

Henry italicizes the correction. Biran in no way reproaches Descartes with having considered the self a being or with having determined the ego as soul; he reproaches him, on the contrary, with having determined as soul something that *is not the self* — something absolute, transcendent, no longer certain, escaping the grasp and competence of a transcendental phenomenology, nothing more than a term = x, object of a belief, postulate of a theory. The soul can be nothing other than the ego, but the ego has a being which is precisely the soul. Either, therefore, "soul" designates a being not determined within the sphere of absolute immanence and subjectivity but situated outside and transcendent relative to it — in which case, by the very phenomenological presuppositions of Biranian ontology, it can only be considered a downright harmful hypothesis, the soul not yet self in the metaphysical hypothesis; or "soul" designates nothing but the being of the ego as subjective being, in which case it not only has standing in Biranian philosophy but constitutes its foundation, its admission amounting to the affirmation of the real being of the ego, which is no longer a pure logical and formal subject but life itself in its concrete and absolute existence. The distinction delivers us, as Biran says, from the continual equivocation arising from a common sign given to two subjects of attribution: the soul which is self, and the soul which is not self. This disjunction is the section's payoff, and the second horn is Henry's own position: soul is a legitimate term provided it names the ego's immanent being and not a substrate behind it.

The whole effort of the Biranian critique aims at founding the ego's being, and this founding proceeds by a reflection asking in which ontological region the ego's being can be rigorously and certainly determined. It then appears that the ego's being is that of a determinate region and indeed the only region where an absolutely certain determination is possible. Possessing an ontology of subjectivity is what lets Biranism solve the problem of the ego's being, as the main articulations of the critique of the Cartesian *cogito* show: if one holds to the knowledge of feeling or the immediate internal perception of the thinking subject, determination of being is not only possible but perfect; if one aspires to an exterior or objective knowledge of the thinking thing outside thought, that mode of knowledge — to which one so vainly seeks to reduce everything, and which is certainly not primitive knowledge — is inapplicable to the thinking subject itself, that is, to the ego.

By denouncing ontological monism as an absolutely improper horizon for elaborating the question of the ego's being, the Biranian problematic not only reaches a position from which modern philosophy's theses on the soul, the being of the ego, and self-knowledge must be reopened on entirely new bases; it also shows itself in absolute opposition to the Kantian critique of the paralogism of rational psychology, a critique whose heavy legacy weighs on the contemporary conceptions Henry has felt bound to oppose. What drives the Biranian critique of the soul is the presence of an ontology of subjectivity, the richness and profusion of a thought that is nothing other than a phenomenology. The principle of the Kantian critique, in the case at hand, is a destitution so complete that one must say not merely that inner experience in Kantianism is submitted to the same status as experience of the world, but that for that very reason it is far more imperfect than the latter, of which it is a pale imitation — an imitation, on Kant's own admission, impossible — without the German thinker's having thought it necessary to interrogate the strange paradox that the soul should be less easy to know than the body, and without his ever reopening the presuppositions whose consequence had to be the ruin of psychology and of every positive science of human reality. Henry notes that he insists on the opposition between Biran and Kant on the soul not only because the shared critique of "soul," "substance," and "thinking thing" can breed serious confusion, but because Biran, in his critique of the Cartesian *cogito*, explicitly appeals to Kant in terms that might suggest identity of standpoint. The "strange paradox" is a real objection to Kant and worth registering as such: on Kant's account inner sense yields a self given under the form of time and hence more remote from determination than outer objects, and Kant does not ask why the self-given should be the harder to know.

Where the Transcendental Dialectic brings to light the difficulties governing the constitution of the soul as substance and then of inner experience, Biran affirms that the soul is in no way constituted, because the inner experience in which the ego's being is given to itself is radically immanent. Biran's critique — no longer of the Cartesian *cogito* but of the concept of soul in general, and particularly of Stahl's conception — confirms this last point.

If the soul, that is the being of the ego, is not determined within a sphere of absolute immanence, it becomes either a transcendent ego or, in the case now at issue, a transcendent term = x. It is then placed at the source of the self's conscious determinations, as their hidden origin. And why should this soul situated behind us as an absolute force from which our conscious life derives not also be the cause of the organic effects we observe in our own body? This is Stahl's thesis, of which Biran gives a remarkable critical account: considered as cause of movements or unknown productive force, and consequently distinguished from the self, which exists only insofar as it perceives and perceives only insofar as it acts, the human soul could be ranged in the class of all other forces, of which there is no science properly speaking beyond the sensible effects by which alone they can manifest themselves. Thus philosophers were often led to form an objective idea of the thinking and moving principle. Stahl, having realized the soul's substance outside the self, could be led to refer to this common cause both the functions that can only be imagined or represented in material organs and the acts or operations conceived solely in the reflection of the subject who attributes them to himself. Stahl is the pivot of the argument's second half: he shows what necessarily happens to a soul placed outside the self, namely that it becomes available to explain bodily process as readily as thought, since it has no determinate nature of its own.

The identity of an unknown cause assigned to both the self's conscious determinations and organic modifications implies, when affirmed, a double hypothesis: that these conscious determinations and the physiological phenomena have an unknown but real cause, and that this cause is the same. On the second, Biran observes that reducing organic and intellectual phenomena thus juxtaposed to a systematic unity of productive force did not conform to the true method of the sciences of fact, according to which one may be led to suppose or affirm identity of cause only by analogy or complete resemblance between observed effects. The first hypothesis, infinitely graver, amounts to affirming, as applied to conscious determinations, that thought has a transcendent foundation and that something = x determines it.

Here the Biranian critique performs its destructive work in depth. The soul posited as the transcendent foundation of our thoughts, being neither circumscribed within subjectivity nor an element of the visible world, inevitably becomes a transcendent term = x. Totally indeterminate in itself, it can receive determination only indirectly, from the phenomena it is charged with explaining and of which it is in principle the foundation. But such determination is variable and frankly gratuitous, since one can assign this transcendent term = x as foundation of whatever the philosopher pleases to explain by it: one can attribute anything one likes to such a cause. The argument is a criterion, not a complaint. A posited term explaining anything indifferently has no determinate content and so explains nothing.

The indetermination of this alleged foundation's role is equally an indetermination of its essence and of the name given it. The Biranian critique here takes a dialectical turn, since the transcendent and nocturnal world against which it deploys its irony is the privileged world of dialectic, the world of false and gratuitous determinations that can be denied as soon as affirmed and preserved at the same time: being absolutely empty, their presence or absence changes nothing, and only different names are given to the same nothingness. This transcendent term = x, posited in doctrines of ontological alienation as the transcendent foundation of our conscious determinations, can receive any name at the philosopher's humor: Stahl's soul, the God of occasional causes, Descartes' God, the unconscious, the play of the organs are so many idols necessarily dissolving in their own vacuity, a dissolution expressed in the incessant passage leading us from one to another. Dialectic, never anything but a vast tautology, triumphs here, for the absolute oppositions — between God, the unconscious, or the cerebral center — prove unserious, since the different terms in fact cover the same reality, or rather the same unreality, the same phantom and illusion. It is the difference of the sign given to the unknown that makes the whole division. Henry's point is not that these doctrines are equally false but that they are the same doctrine; the interchangeability is the proof of vacuity.

Biran establishes in another text, successively, four identifications. First, the identity of Malebranche's position with Stahl's: despite the apparent distance separating the system of occasional causes from the one attributing vital or organic functions to the thinking soul as efficacious cause, they rest on like bases and spring from a common stock. That common base is the ontological status assigned to the soul as principle of actions, a principle making it no longer an immanent source of absolute certainty but an unknown term. Biran: having extinguished the torch that lights me on the interior principle of my own acts, I will go pursuing its shadow in exterior darkness, seeking a fantastic common cause of everything operating in me or in my organs, without me as by me. Then, there being no longer any foundation for a science properly speaking of my active intellectual faculties, metaphysics will have to identify itself in my eyes with theology if I am a Malebranche, with physiology if I am a Stahl. The dependence of metaphysics on the fate of the torch is the argument's heart: extinguish immanent self-knowledge and metaphysics must borrow its content from some external discipline.

Second, the identity of these two positions with Descartes': Descartes, vainly seeking to conceive how the soul could set in play and direct with such precision animal spirits and nerves of which it has no objective knowledge, resorts to a supreme efficacious force whose idea or model he could only have found in the experience or feeling of his own force. Henry notes that this last proposition contains the index of a theory of the genesis of the idea of the soul as term = x, confirming the genesis he gave earlier for the transcendent ego; and that Descartes' thesis is explicitly assimilated to Stahl's, both seeming equally to reject the authority of intimate sense and to suppose faculties or innate ideas preexisting in the soul without needing, in order to exist, to come to its knowledge.

Third, an exhaustive psychoanalysis of the idea of the unconscious, which if founded would mean for human reality an ontological alienation comparable to that implied by the conception of the soul as transcendent term = x. The new idol of modern times is only another name for an old metaphysical entity, and Biran's prophetic genius denounces this identity of essence and signification when, explaining the genesis of Stahl's theory, he says that Stahl, not knowing how to reconcile the finalistic phenomena characterizing consciousness with the laws of brute and living matter, transported into the latter the active and intelligent principle, supposing that it is not essential to this principle to know itself in order to exist and to act. Henry's use of "psychoanalysis" is not casual, and the criterion identified here — a principle that acts without knowing itself — is exactly the one his *Généalogie de la psychanalyse* will use two decades later to place Freud in the lineage of the philosophies of the unconscious that lose the immanent self-givenness of life.

Fourth, a critique of the transcendent term = x taken no longer as soul or unconscious but as "the physiological": others attribute to the play of the organs the whole ensemble of acts, functions, and results, whether organic and foreign to the self or intellectual and accompanied by consciousness. It will thus no longer be Stahl's soul but the living parts united in systems and corresponding to a common center, which will be said to feel — often without the self's knowledge — the impressions received, and to execute or even determine and will the movements following them.

Commenting on this last text would lead to the center of the Biranian problematic of the body, so Henry defers it. But he notes the change of value the transcendent principle of our thoughts undergoes in becoming a system of physiological processes. As night in its disquiet aspires irresistibly to submit to the law of day, so the transcendent term supposed to account for our conscious life is no longer merely behind us but also appears before us, so that it is no longer simply a transcendent term = x; the x seems to determine itself — the whole Biranian critique of the body will consist in showing this determination illusory — since it has now found a place in the objective world. The physiological, when made the principle of our thoughts, is thus only a coarse realization of the nocturnal transcendent, its immediate and naive incarnation in a visible universe. Biran denounces the subterfuge: the metaphysician who speaks of the soul abstracted from the feeling of the self risks being led to objectify this thing in a point of the brain, and turns into a physiologist. This diagnosis is the book's link between the critique of the soul and the critique of the objective body: localization in the brain is not a scientific advance over the metaphysical soul but the same error given a visible address.

Why must the metaphysician incur such a destiny; why is metaphysics the sister of the coarsest materialist realism? Because the soul as transcendent term = x is only a shadow. Like the souls of the dead in ancient mythologies, wandering ceaselessly in search of a place where they might take form and again taste, feel, and embrace, the soul of metaphysics is without consistency and without being, light and vaporous; and if it is everywhere and slips even into the dreams of the living, that is because in reality it is nowhere. Incapable of being anything at all, like the pseudo-subjectivity of the moderns, it aspires to existence but is only nothingness: it would need a body. Building a physiologist metaphysics, with its summary materialism, is — like the contemporary philosophy of despair — only a presentiment of this requirement. But the objective body is precisely like the universe: it is that in which the soul cannot become incarnate, what it traverses in its wandering without finding habitation or dwelling. It would need a body, but also the means of satisfying such a requirement. Building an ontology of subjectivity, whose principal phases Henry has retraced, is precisely what allowed Biran to dispose of a body that is ours and can be designated as the reality of the soul, as the authentic being of the ego.

The closing figure carries the argument of the whole chapter into the next. Materialism and the metaphysics of the soul share a single failure — both look for the soul's body among objects — and the discovery of the subjective body is what supplies the demand both were unable to meet.

## Chapter II. The Subjective Body

Biran opposed Descartes explicitly in his determination of the *cogito*, charging him with a static conception of thought that appears too often in the author of the *Meditations* as a substance closed on itself, whose whole life consists in being modified by a succession of modes that are only moments of that substance and therefore permit no passage beyond toward anything else, no action on anything else — no action properly speaking. Consciousness is an "I think," and all modifications of conscious life are determinations of thought, that is, ideas. When we deal not with conceptions properly so called but with a desire, an action, a movement, the Cartesian determination of the *cogito* obliges us to say that we still have only ideas: the idea of a desire, of an action, of a movement. Action or movement considered in themselves no longer belong to the sphere of the *cogito*; they are determinations not of thought but of extension. The normal process running from the idea of a movement to the real accomplishment of that movement therefore poses a problem that can be neither solved nor even envisaged within the sphere of pure subjectivity, and the body, as the milieu where real movements occur, can find a place only in a philosophy disposing of an ontological region other than subjectivity. Within subjectivity there is room neither for action nor for the body; and if the self reduced to pure thought, it would be only a milieu of passive modifications where our desires could arise but not be realized. Henry poses the difficulty in Descartes' own terms before offering Biran's solution, and the difficulty is real: a *cogito* whose contents are all ideas cannot contain an action, only the idea of one.

Biran, having affirmed the identity of the being of the ego with that of subjectivity, nonetheless refuses to determine the self as a substance modified by accidents. Is the self given to itself in the primitive fact as modified substance or as cause or productive force of certain effects? The effort of Biranian thought is precisely to determine the *cogito* as a power of production — a determination already implied in the affirmation of the immanence of the categories. The central place of the deduction of causality prefigured the interpretation of the ego's concrete being as cause and, in Biran's word, as effort. The Cartesian *cogito* thus had to undergo a radical change of value: it had to shed the immobility of thought-substance and become instead the very experience of an effort in its accomplishment, an effort with which the self's being begins and ends. Biran: this primitive substantial thought supposed to constitute my whole individual existence, I find identified at its source with the feeling of an action or a willed effort. The ego's being is therefore no longer determined as a pure thought whose essence is exhausted in knowledge of extension and contemplation of things; it appears identified with the action by which I ceaselessly modify the world, if only to make my own continued existence possible there, with the movements I direct toward the universe to reach it or flee it. It is the very element of those movements. The ego is a power; the *cogito* means not "I think" but "I can."

Henry immediately denies that this classical opposition is where Biran's originality or depth lies. Many other philosophers, beginning with those Biran explicitly invokes — Schelling, Fichte, Cabanis, Destutt de Tracy — had determined self-consciousness not as a representation but as effort, force, life, act. If one holds to the interpretation of consciousness as activity and effort, Biran's philosophy is only one unit among the activist philosophies, and one could cite many other worldviews where opposition to contemplative and theoretical thought yielded richer, more brilliant, and seemingly more suggestive developments.

Depth of thought cannot lie in such oppositions — in erecting an activist antithesis facing the contemplative or theoretical thesis. Philosophies built from antitheses of this kind may seduce us for a moment by the beauty of their descriptions, the romanticism of their formulas, the breadth of perspective their generality seems to afford, and the grand divisions they trace through humanity between sages and heroes; they may exalt action, glorify risk, urge commitment, and divide history in the light of their elected concepts; but they cannot make what they condemn cease to exist. The philosophical naivety of these constructions is precisely to present one conception of human nature rather than another, to make man a being who acts *or* a being who thinks. Depth of thought is rigor, and the only rigor at issue in a first philosophy is ontological rigor, which infallibly discards gratuitous affirmations and seductive or grandiose oppositions. Biran's philosophy is not a philosophy of action opposed to one of contemplation or thought; it is an ontological theory of action. Its originality and depth lie not in having determined the *cogito* as an "I can," as action and movement, but in the affirmation that the *being* of this movement, action, and power is precisely that of a *cogito*. This is the chapter's governing distinction and the criterion by which Henry separates himself from every voluntarism and philosophy of praxis: the thesis is not that man acts rather than thinks, but that acting has the mode of being of thought as Descartes discovered it.

The ontological consequence is infinite. By affirming that the being of movement belongs to the sphere of absolute immanence, Biran proposes an entirely new theory of the mode of knowledge in which movement is given to us. That mode is transcendental inner experience: movement is known immediately, with absolute certainty, and its study belongs to the project of a first philosophy. Further, if such is the original and absolute being of movement, it need not be the object of a thematic thought in order to be known; and it in no way belongs, despite everything one might have thought, to the sphere of transcendent being. The original being of movement is not constituted. Among the many consequences of this thesis, Henry cites one immediately: it lets us understand why children and human beings generally accomplish their movements without thinking about them, yet not without knowing them.

More: the difficulty of understanding how we can dispose of our powers over the world, how we can not only accomplish our movements but first be in a position to *rejoin* them, is already lifted. We rejoin our movements, we never leave them at any moment while accomplishing them, we are constantly informed of them by a knowledge whose originality and exceptional character Henry has established — because we coincide with these movements, because their being, phenomenologically determined by the mode of its appearing, which is that of transcendental inner experience, is the very being of subjectivity. At the same time we understand the concrete character of subjectivity: it is nothing abstract, nothing intellectual; it does not think the world like a philosopher surveying it in thought — it transforms the world, it is a production.

That production is not the work of a biological life, a dynamic unconscious, a will to power, a vital impulse, an indeterminate or third-person praxis, or any obscure force. This force is *knowingly productive*; it never does more than it knows. The least of our daily gestures, which habit, repression, or any other cause would have rendered unconscious, belongs in fact to the sphere of transparency and absolute certainty of transcendental subjectivity; its being is the very being of original truth. Henry is denying at a stroke the whole family of doctrines that ground action in something darker than consciousness, and the denial follows from the chapter's premise: if the being of movement is the being of a *cogito*, nothing in movement can be hidden from itself.

The Biranian *cogito* therefore does not oppose the Cartesian at all. There is no cause to set an "I can" against an "I think," since the whole Biranian analysis of effort has the single essential result of determining effort as a mode of subjectivity itself. It may seem in many respects that the thought Descartes studied is a reflexive thought, an explicit thought of objects, a thematic knowledge of the determinations of extension. But even were that so, even if the Cartesian *cogito* were reflexive, one would have to recognize that the *being* of this reflection is identical with that of movement. The fundamental structure of consciousness is always the same, always a transcendental inner experience, and whatever mode it expresses itself in, our life coincides with such experience. Reflection is an intentionality, and what makes its being is the being of every intentionality.

The distinction between a reflexive and a prereflexive *cogito* is equivocal. One should in any case see that before being a thematic knowledge of anything — of the ego itself, for instance — reflection is first a transcendental inner experience; and it is in this original understanding of reflection that we find reason to affirm that its being is identical with that of any other modality of transcendental life, with the being of movement for example. Such an identification in no way means that movement is known by a reflection — an objection that could be pressed only by someone imagining that reflection, as a determination of transcendental subjectivity, is itself known by a reflection. On the contrary, and because every determination of subjectivity is known in itself, movement, which is such a determination, is likewise never known, at least in its original being, through the mediation of a thought. Note how the objection is disarmed: the same principle that makes reflection self-known without a second reflection makes movement self-known without any reflection at all.

So the determinations of the *cogito* proposed by Biran and Descartes do not conflict. One can only say that Descartes studied the *cogito* of reflection — reflection as transcendental inner experience, or in improper language the prereflexive *cogito* immanent in all reflection and constituting its very being — while Biran grasped the *cogito* in movement or apropos of it, that is, the transcendental inner experience making up the whole being of subjective movement. If there is a lesson in this comparison, Henry wants it to be that the "I think" and the "I can" have the same ontological status, that of subjectivity and of the transcendental inner experiences of which it is the milieu. On that milieu, its ontological status, and the phenomenological mode in which it reveals itself, the two great French philosophers agree, and the foundation of their thought is a common project expressed in a vast phenomenological reduction aiming at nothing but an ontology of subjectivity.

One need only note that in realizing this project Biran was more faithful than Descartes to the first philosophical demands that motivated it — a remark taking on its full importance when one sees that it is precisely because he gave the central Cartesian intuition a radical signification, because he advanced as far as possible into the region of absolute existence that intuition had disclosed, because he never thought he could cross the circle assigned to his researches by the presuppositions of an ontology of subjectivity, that Biran, far from being limited thereby, could deepen his analysis to the discovery of subjective movement and the ontological theory of the body. The point is methodological and worth registering: the discovery was made by refusing to leave the immanent sphere, not by supplementing it.

Descartes, by contrast, forgetful of the structures of the privileged region the *cogito* had just discovered, abandons himself when he comes to speak of the body to transcendent constructions that can only fall under the reduction any authentic philosophy performs. The Cartesian theory of the body — to which Henry will return in Chapter V — has nothing in common with the one Biran was to build. Unless one is careful, assimilating Biran's ontological theories of subjectivity to the Cartesian conception of the *cogito*, however well founded that assimilation is, risks leading to a serious misreading of the ontological interpretation of subjective movement.

For in Cartesianism, determining movement as subjective movement would mean mutilating the true being of movement, bracketing its real being. What subsists of movement within the Cartesian *cogito* is only the *idea* of movement. Real movement occurs elsewhere, in extension; subjective movement is thus only the interior and in itself ineffective design of that real movement. The place where real movement is accomplished is the body, which means in Cartesianism and many other philosophies that movement belongs to transcendent being as one of its determinations. One may certainly dispute the essence of Cartesian extension as a homogeneous milieu of reciprocal exteriority, the conception of a *partes extra partes* body, and the consequences for the nature of movement occurring in such a milieu. But it is not enough to challenge mechanism: a dynamic or structural interpretation of movement would change nothing essential in this conception so long as the body is an element of transcendent being. The point is aimed past Descartes at every modern physiology of movement, and the criterion is single — the region to which the body is assigned, not the model of causation employed within it.

The Biranian determination of subjective movement is by contrast a radical critique of the Cartesian conception of the body, because in tearing not the idea but the very being and reality of movement away from transcendent being, it defines the *real* body, not the idea of the body, as a subjective and transcendental being. One must see that only a philosophy that conceived original truth as a being, and the transcendental ego as a self rather than a logical subject and a nothingness, is in a position to complete this work of determining subjectivity and not to recoil before the apparent absurdity of giving ontological and original knowledge the name of *body*.

Such, in its ontological development, is the intuition at the principle of Biranian thought: recognition of an original sphere of existence, that of subjectivity; conception, within a phenomenological ontology, of the being of that subjectivity, which proves identical with the ego's; understanding of the ego's structures as the structures and the being of ontological knowledge; determination of the being of ontological knowledge, which is the ego's, as the very being of the body; and finally, the identity of ontological knowledge with the ontological nature of that body itself. This five-term chain is the book's architecture in miniature, and each link was established in Chapter I; the last two are what Chapter II adds.

The interdependence of the theory of the body and Biranian ontology as a whole thus appears clearly, and it is disclosed in a question forming one of the major themes of the *Essai*: the origin of the knowledge we have of our own body. Henry notes the title is Naville's, given to chapter III of section II of the *Essai*'s first part. The body — that is, for Biran, movement felt in its accomplishment, the feeling of effort — is given to us in a mode of knowledge that remains to be determined; and this problem of our primordial knowledge of the body is at the same time the problem of the body's ontological nature, since in a phenomenological ontology being is determined solely by the manner in which it gives itself to us. Biran's answer to these two problems, which are one, is that the body is given in a transcendental inner experience, that our knowledge of it is thus genuinely original knowledge, and that consequently the being of the body belongs to the ontological region where such experiences are possible and occur — the sphere of subjectivity. The phenomenological, that is original, real, and absolute being of the body is thus a subjective being.

This affirms the absolute immanence of the body, and implies rejecting all analyses commanded by the presupposition that the body is in its original being something transcendent — a presupposition usually implicit, since among all the affirmations that go without saying, a place of honor doubtless belongs to the one holding that the body is a thing, a constituted reality, a part of the world. That the original body belongs to the sphere of absolute immanence means that the phenomena of the body belong to an order of facts in immediate relation of knowledge with itself. Henry then draws three decisive results.

First, movement is known by itself. It is not known by something else — not by a regard of reflection, nor by any intentionality directed on it. No phenomenological distance interposes between movement and us; movement is nothing transcendent.

Second, movement is in our possession. Our body is the ensemble of the powers we have over the world. But how are these powers first in our power? How are they genuinely ours; how can we effectively put them to work and through them reach things, whether the access is positive or negative, according as we aim to seize objects or turn from them? The problem is not new, requiring a fresh problematic; it is the same central problem of Biranian analysis, that of the original knowledge of our body.

Biran's critique of Condillac's theory of the knowledge of one's own body offers a new occasion to formulate the fundamental theses. Condillac's man is affected by multiple sensations, but these carry no character letting us localize them in a determinate seat. What pushes us, according to Condillac, beyond these sensations to determine behind them the real they indicate and in which they take place is the sensation of solidity, whose organ is the hand. Applied to the various parts of our body, our hand gradually reveals to us, through the sensation of solidity, the being of our body and its real forms. Our hand is thus the instrument of the knowledge of our own body.

But the original body is not this body whose parts are circumscribed by the displacement of our hand; it is rather that hand itself, precisely insofar as it applies itself to our body or to other things in order to delimit their contours. The problem of the nature of our original body — of the original knowledge of our hand, for instance — is passed over in complete silence by Condillac, whose theory is thus a vast circle, presupposing what it claims to explain. The hand, organ of the sensation of solidity, is the instrument letting us determine the parts of our body; but how is this instrument itself first known? The circularity charge is exactly targeted: any account that explains bodily self-knowledge by an exploratory organ has helped itself to knowledge of that organ.

The question is decisive and reduces to another Biran puts to Condillac, again about the hand: how has any mobile organ been constantly directed without being known? The hand's movement is doubtless not known in the sense of being constituted; but if we can direct it, is that not because we are informed of it, because we possess about it a primordial knowledge — precisely the kind in which no phenomenological distance intervenes and no constitution occurs? The hand's movement is known without being apprehended in a world; it gives itself immediately in the transcendental inner experience that coincides with the very being of that movement. Because it is not constituted, because it is a transcendental experience, the hand's movement has nothing to do with a displacement in objective space or in any transcendent milieu: original and real movement is subjective movement. For the same reason it is in my power — not at all in the sense in which objects are subject to a power that would be mine, but in the sense that it *is* that power, that it is itself what has purchase on things.

Third, movement is not an intermediary between the ego and the world; it is not an instrument. The body is often characterized as the instrument of my action on the world, by whose mediation I can modify it as suits me; it is also called the "vehicle" of my power over the world. About the being of this instrument, intermediary, or vehicle, no precision is offered. What these words mean doubtless goes without saying — but neither are we told why every man in fact knows where he stands on the matter, why the movement by which a human being performs any action poses him no problem at all. If I execute my movements without thinking about them, that is not because they are mechanical or unconscious but because their being belongs entirely to the sphere of absolute transparency of subjectivity. There is no intermediary between soul and movement, because there is between them neither distance nor separation. The soul therefore needs no intermediary to execute its movements. As an intermediary between the soul and the movements by which it acts in the world, the body *does not exist*; it is only a fiction of reflective thought.

Children have no consciousness of a body that would be the ensemble of means they must employ to do this or that, to reach this or that result. Our actions are accomplished without our resorting to our body as a means. We therefore have no need to reflect on this means or this body, which is never for us a problem nor an element for solving one. Our movements are accomplished spontaneously, naturally; they have no instruments serving us to execute them. Biran: the soul does not think in advance of the object of its willing or of the instruments that must execute it and that it does not know. So the ego acts directly on the world. It does not act through the mediation of a body, it resorts to no means in accomplishing its movements; it is itself that body, itself that movement, itself that means. Ego, body, movement, means are one and the same, and this is entirely real — dissolving neither into the night of the unconscious nor into the void of nothingness. It is a being, and that being is the being of everything given in a transcendental inner experience: the very being of the ego.

The body is not an instrument, because what we call an instrument is always in the service of something else that uses it. In this relation of use, that something else — the subject, thought — using the body in its service would have to have, by hypothesis, a knowledge of that body as a reality different from itself, that is, as a transcendent reality. When it becomes an instrument, the body's movement is given to us only in a transcendent experience. The theme of thought would then be this instrument rather than the goal of the action or movement to be accomplished, which is absurd; for supposing the subject could think both the means and the end of its action, it would not thereby execute the action — it would simply *represent* it to itself, representing its goal and the means of attaining it, but it would not act. This thought of end and means certainly exists, but the thought of movement is not movement; movement is an entirely new phenomenon relative to that thought, and it is precisely this phenomenon that is in question. The conception of the body as instrument of our action is therefore an element of our *representation* of movement, but it can in no way become a theory of real movement itself. The argument turns on a point easily lost: were the body an instrument, acting would require attending to the instrument, and attending to the instrument is not acting.

Henry now guards against the obvious objection. The ontological theory of subjective movement, far from reducing movement to its idea, lets us conceive the only possible foundation for the reality of movement and of our body. To affirm that the body is not an intermediary between the soul and its action on the universe is not to deny the body's reality; it is to say that the constituted body — of which Henry will speak again — is not our original body, and that the latter's being must escape all constitution in order to be identified with the power of constitution itself, with the milieu in which constitution occurs.

Only on this condition can the body really act on the universe: on condition of not being a transcendent mass — of nerves and muscles, say — which one cannot see how absolute subjectivity could set in motion so as to produce a displacement or modification in the world. To deny that an instrument or intermediate term interposes itself in action between absolute subjectivity and its world is to give its radical signification to the thesis of the absolute immanence of the body — a thesis that refuses us the right to establish any separation between the being of absolute subjectivity and that of our original body. It is to understand that only on the condition that the being of movement be *closest to us* is that movement possible as real movement rather than movement in idea; to understand how this movement can be ours, how we can join ourselves to it and come into possession of it, and how finally we have an interior knowledge of it beginning and ceasing with it.

We come into its possession because in reality we are never separated from it. We know it while it is being accomplished, and this knowledge is perfect, because the being of movement is a phenomenological effectivity whose whole being is precisely to be given to us, and given in a transcendental inner experience. This movement is ours because its being so defined is the very being of the ego, whose life is nothing but the life of transcendental subjectivity in all its modalities and determinations. And this movement is real, with an absolute reality like subjectivity's; what opposes it to a merely represented movement is precisely that it does not, like the latter, belong to transcendent being. That is the whole sense of saying that the being of movement is a subjective being. The reversal against the expected objection is the paragraph's point: it is the transcendent body, not the immanent one, that cannot secure the reality of action.

The affirmation of the absolute immanence of the original body is the principle of Biran's challenge to Hume's theses on causality. The Biranian critique is not a term-by-term refutation of Hume's propositions but a *destruction* — an elucidation of the philosophical horizons within which Hume attempts to determine and grasp the origin of our principle of causality. It is thus an ontological inquiry showing on what planes Hume poses his problems and situates the elements entering their solution or statement, and how the failure he meets and wants to present as definitive, as somehow implied in the nature of things, in fact stems from the inadequacy between those problems and the ontological planes on which they are supposedly resolved. This deficiency in ontological elucidation is what leads Hume to maintain about movement theses exactly opposed to Biran's in the *Essai*. Henry's use of "destruction" is deliberate and marks his method throughout: a philosophy is refuted by exhibiting the region of being it silently presupposes, not by contesting its inferences.

First, for Hume movement is not known by itself, a thesis expressed through the ambiguity of his terminology in the claim that the effect could not be foreseen in the energy of its cause. This terminology seems to originate in the description of external phenomena. An objective process decomposes into an initial state said to be the cause of a terminal state; but considering the latter, one does not find in it the first state supposed to determine it causally, and conversely, analyzing the cause, one does not see why it is followed by that effect. The idea of causality must therefore come from elsewhere, and Hume is led to consider another process for whose description he unfortunately lacks an ontological horizon.

That other process is bodily movement, which Hume's analysis — bearing the heavy legacy of Cartesian dualism — divides into a first phase, the will or desire to accomplish the movement, and a second, the corresponding material process. Examining the desire or will, Hume finds nothing letting us call it cause of the physical movement following it, so that here too one could never have foreseen the effect in the energy of its cause. We do have experience of the influence of volitions on bodily organs, as we have experience of all natural processes, but this influence at the principle of all our movements is mysterious to us: we are condemned to eternal ignorance of the efficacious means by which this extraordinary operation is effected, so far are we from having an immediate feeling of it.

This last sentence is particularly important, for in it appears the interdependence of the thesis that we have no immediate feeling of movement with the thesis that we know nothing of the instruments by which movement is accomplished. More: it is *because* we have no knowledge of these instruments that Hume affirms the immediate feeling of movement cannot be given us. One does not see how, in an empiricist ontology knowing only the transcendent being of nature, there could be room for a correct interpretation of this immediate feeling, that is, for subjective movement. To do it justice and recognize its fundamental role, one would need an ontology of subjectivity rather than an empiricist conception of inner life assimilating it to a transcendent milieu peopled by beings joined in external relations.

It is nonetheless significant that Hume's argument bears on the instruments of our action, that is, on the body considered as an ensemble of transcendent masses, an interior play of nerves and muscles the will is supposed to set in action in the movements of our limbs. Henry has shown that no instrument intervenes in the accomplishment of our movements, and that *this very ignorance of the anatomist's or physiologist's body is the condition of our action's taking place*. Action is accomplished as a subjective experience having no more relation to a play of nerves and muscles than our original body has with a third-person process or with the material elements supporting it. Biran: what kind of analogy is there between the representative knowledge of the position, play, and functions of our organs as an anatomist or physiologist may know them, and the intimate feeling of the existence corresponding to those functions? The inversion is the argument's sharpest move — the ignorance Hume treats as the source of the mystery is what makes action possible.

Taking inventory of the elements available to Hume for explaining or even describing the accomplishment of one of our movements, one sees that the English philosopher knows far too little — strictly, he knows none of them. The instruments of our movements he wholly ignores, and on that point one can only grant him the case, so long as "instrument" carries a transcendent signification.
