But since he has no ontology of subjectivity, he equally ignores the feeling of movement in the course of accomplishment, and so gives Biran the chance to catch him in his own nets: if it is true that we do not know the instruments of our actions, one cannot see why we would even think of seeking and knowing them, if we did not otherwise have the feeling of those actions in their very accomplishment. Of our movement, Biran writes, we can have the feeling without knowing the means in any way — a decisive text containing the essential of Biranian thought on the problem of the original body.

What then remains to Hume? How, on his view, would it be possible for man to act, how could his movements be in his possession and his power? He has no feeling of them, and if one speaks of means and instruments, he is ignorant of those. Strictly, Hume should not even speak of our desire or our impulses to accomplish a movement, since he has no theory capable of accounting for the belonging of psychic states to a self. Will it be said that on his view the agent at least knows the results of his action as natural phenomena? The reductio is complete, and its final step is what follows.

Here is revealed the complete bankruptcy, on the very terrain where they should triumph, not only of empiricism but of *every philosophy that does not make movement a transcendental inner experience* — that is, which lacks the means to determine it originally as a modality of the ego's own life. Considering the effects produced in the world by our actions, these appear as natural events belonging to the transcendent being of nature, and one cannot see what on this view differentiates them from any other spatiotemporal phenomenon. The movement of my hand as objective displacement is a third-person movement, and the dependence relation linking it to the ego, the relation that should make it appear as an "effect" of the ego's action, remains wholly mysterious.

Where could the principle of a distinction lie between such a movement — the movement of my hand tracing these characters on the paper — and that of the rain I watch falling outside? What lets me attach one rather than the other to the ego; what lets me say the first is an effect of my action while the second is not? The first condition a theory of the movement of one's own body must satisfy is to account for the feeling of a movement I myself accomplish, of a power in exercise that is mine. That demand can be met only if movement is originally and immediately known and lived as a determination of the ego's own concrete life. As soon as one leaves the sphere of absolute immanence, movement is only a foreign phenomenon assimilable to any event in the universe. And if there exists a transcendent movement nonetheless constituted as emanating from the self, as an "effect" of its action, such constitution requires a foundation found precisely in the original being of subjective movement. The hand and the rain make the criterion concrete: no inspection of the objective movement can tell which is mine, so mineness must be given before the objective description.

Where the power of my body is concerned, it remains true and necessary to say, from an original standpoint, that this power is known and sensed only insofar as we place ourselves in the position of the moving being; effort or movement is represented only insofar as we separate ourselves entirely from the being to which we attribute it; so that by the very fact that the latter is known as a foreign object or phenomenon, it cannot be felt in its cause, nor consequently can its cause be known in it as it is itself known.

The problem is therefore ontological. So long as one has not determined movement in its original being, in its belonging to transcendental subjectivity — so long as one has not rigorously dissociated the original element of movement insofar as it is *felt* from the being of constituted movement manifesting itself in the transcendent region of the world — one can only go from confusion to confusion in analyzing the movement of one's own body, as Hume does, and as do nearly all the philosophical systems that have approached this problem without the ontological horizons that alone permit posing it correctly.

Hume's analysis is exact within philosophical presuppositions never made explicit and bathed in total ontological obscurity. Biran: attending only to this representation, and considering exterior movement as an effect of which the will would be supposed the cause, it is quite true that the power cannot be known in the effect and vice versa, for these two conceptions are heterogeneous, one founded solely on an interior sense and the other on an exterior sense. It is by reestablishing homogeneity between the two terms of the primitive relation of causality, by reentering the fact of consciousness, that the subject of effort apperceives itself interiorly as the cause of a movement. And if Hume did not recognize the reality of effort in its immediate self-revelation as power of production, that is because he had no ontology of the sphere of existence of subjectivity in which the being of movement is originally given: if he refuses to admit the existence of this cause, it is because he wants to conceive it under an idea that is not its own, or by some faculty foreign to the proper and immediate sense in which it resides. Henry's concession that Hume's analysis is *exact* within its presuppositions is characteristic and important: the critique never disputes the reasoning, only the region within which it is conducted.

The affirmation that the original being of movement is given in a transcendental inner experience and known by itself gives Biranian thought its original character, equally distant from empiricism and intellectualism. In both of these, *movement is known by something other than itself* — by muscular sensation in one case, by intelligence in the other. Intellectualism does not, however, dispense with muscular sensation; its psychology is an empirical psychology, and it is content to show that this psychology is not complete and that muscular sensation can take on its signification — making movement known to us — only if submitted to the action of the category. The movement intellectualism thus tries to reconstruct is in reality only a *representation* of movement, while movement's being and the problem of its original knowledge escape it entirely.

Deepening the Biranian interpretation of movement in its opposition to classical psychology requires first giving the latter a genuinely philosophical significance. Henry turns to Jules Lagneau, the most profound of the French neo-Kantians, who moreover long meditated the Biranian theses Henry is himself attempting to rethink with infinitely lesser means. The *Leçon sur la perception*, with its explicit discussion of muscular sensation and movement, will show how far Lagneau's genius pushed his understanding of the Biranian philosophy of effort, but also the limits of that understanding, how such limits were necessarily traced within the French philosophical horizon of the late nineteenth century, and what consequences they entailed for psychology generally and for the theory of body and movement. Lagneau is the ideal test case because he is both sympathetic and rigorous: what defeats him is the ontology available to him, not carelessness.

The discussion in the *Leçon* concerning the "feeling of action" turns entirely on whether movement is or is not what Henry has called a transcendental inner experience, and develops in two directions. In a first moment Lagneau grasps the necessity of answering his own question affirmatively: can one feel one's action? Despite hesitations appearing at once — is there not a contradiction in feeling oneself acting; what is feeling if not undergoing a modification suffered? — he immediately affirms that we feel our action very well.

This position results from a Biranian argument whose admirable rigor Lagneau grasped better than anyone, and which definitively excludes the thesis that movement could be known by muscular sensation, known by something other than itself: the muscular sensations informing us of modifications in our muscles are the same whatever the origin of those modifications, whether they are the effect of our willing or of an external cause. For these modifications to appear as an effect of our action, they must be referred to the idea of that action, an idea that must come to us from elsewhere. Lagneau: for the idea that the action produces an effect to be linked to the sensation of that effect, the action must be revealed to us otherwise than by its effect — revealed in itself. The argument is genuinely decisive and belongs to Biran: identical muscular sensations under different causal provenance show that provenance is not read off the sensations.

An admirable commentary on Biranian thought, Henry grants. But how can Lagneau affirm that movement, action, or effort are known by themselves when he has no ontology of subjectivity? By what intellectual motivation would he be led to make psychology a transcendental phenomenology, while philosophizing in a Kantian perspective newly introduced in France, in which there is room only for an empirical psychology?

Hence the second moment of the analysis, in which Lagneau's thought departs completely from the theory of movement as transcendental inner experience while trying to preserve the points only that theory could have let him make. Having affirmed that we have the feeling of our muscular action, Lagneau declares that we do not: we do not feel ourselves active, we judge ourselves such. If muscular sensation cannot account for the feeling of producing a movement of which we are the cause, that is not because we have the revelation of this movement in an immediate experience, but because a judgment is added to muscular sensation joining to it the idea that we are its cause and that it is indeed the effect of our action. Lagneau: to feel oneself active is to undergo certain modifications while judging that they result from thought, that they result necessarily, that they are its effects, that between thought and these modifications exists a relation of causality, a necessary relation whose conception and affirmation impose themselves on thought. The idea of action implies that of causality; the idea of causality implies that of necessity; and necessity cannot be felt, it is affirmed as what must be.

Henry shows first that, however remarkable the text formulating it, this is no solution. For Lagneau as for Biran, the task was to understand why, when we accomplish a movement, the sensations supposedly translating modifications in our muscles are determined as effects of our action, and how they can be distinguished from analogous muscular sensations that are not the consequences of a subject's willing. Lagneau's answer is that in the first case the idea that it is an effect is joined to the muscular sensation, an idea expressing that this sensation is linked by a causal relation to our willing and to the determinations of our thought.

But why is this idea of causality applied to muscular sensation, making it appear an effect of my willing, in the case where it is effectively I who act, and not in the other, when an external cause has produced in me the same muscular sensations a voluntary movement would have determined? Why, when the two elements at Lagneau's disposal — muscular sensation and the idea of causality — are the same in both cases, the first by hypothesis and the second because it is a universal idea always identical with itself, are we able to differentiate the two cases, to say now that it is we who act and now that it is not and that the effect was produced in us by something else? Henry's objection is exact and fatal: Lagneau's two ingredients are invariant across the very cases he must distinguish, so the distinction cannot be built from them.

There are two different judgments here, but what is the truth of these judgments, what is their foundation? If, when I act, I judge that the modification produced in me by this action is the result of an external cause, where is the principle of my error, in what does it consist, how can I recognize or avoid it? And if, when a foreign cause produces a certain impression in me, I attach that impression by a judgment to an action supposedly emanating from me, making the latter the cause and the former the effect, how here again could I notice that I am mistaken? How, if I have no original knowledge of my action itself, if that action is not first given to me in a transcendental inner experience, so that this primordial and absolutely certain knowledge can become the foundation of all judgments I bear on the dependence relation uniting my muscular sensations to my movements?

I do not act because I judge that I act; I judge that I act because I effectively act. What is at issue is my effective movement, and only an ontology of subjectivity, and within it a theory of subjective movement, can explain how this movement is at the same time a knowledge, how consequently it is known neither by muscular sensation nor by judgment, but is on the contrary what makes possible the intervention of a judgment by which muscular sensation will be posited as an effect. The order of foundation stated in that inversion is the chapter's central claim, and the reason Lagneau's account inverts it is that his ontology offers nothing beneath judgment.

Lagneau did not reject the theory of subjective movement; the ontological horizon within which he philosophizes did not permit such a theory even to occur to him. The ontological deficiency of Kantian presuppositions regarding subjectivity in fact prevented him from grasping the depth of Biran's thought and even led him to commit a historical and technical error about it as significant as it is serious.

Commenting on effort, whose central role in building the ontological theory of the body and elucidating the being of the ego Henry has shown, Lagneau says that it is in what Biran calls the sensation of muscular effort that the self's revelation to itself is produced, and adds that what Biran did not see is that this knowledge cannot be called sensation — we do not feel ourselves active, we judge ourselves such.

A serious historical error, since Biran *never* called the feeling of muscular action a sensation. His whole philosophy consists precisely in affirming that the feeling of action does not result from a sensation, that action is known in itself insofar as it belongs to subjectivity, insofar as it is a fact in immediate relation of knowledge with itself. That this mode of knowledge cannot be designated by "sensation" is exactly what Biran expressly declared in the text Henry cited earlier, whose essential proposition he recalls: a name is needed for this interior knowledge, since "sensation" cannot say everything.

A significant error nonetheless, for Lagneau was led to commit it not through ignorance or want of probity, but because in a Kantian perspective there exist only two sources of knowledge, sensation and judgment. And just as Lagneau, understanding that our feeling of action cannot be reduced to a muscular sensation, was constrained to make it the product of a judgment, so he had necessarily to think that Biran, departing radically from a philosophy seeing effort as a composite feeling one of whose elements would be an intellectual judgment, could then only reduce the being of that feeling to a sensation. The diagnosis is the chapter's most instructive moment: the misreading is not an accident of scholarship but a projection of an exhaustive two-source epistemology onto a philosophy that denies exactly that exhaustiveness.

Lagneau's analysis thereupon sinks into a deep contradiction. Having said that one must not confuse muscular sensation with the feeling of action — precisely what Biran taught him — he comes to reproach Biran with having committed that confusion. Yet it is Lagneau who commits it, for in his perspective muscular sensation remains, if not the sole element, at least a determining element of our feeling of action, indispensable to its birth: this feeling, he says, is a modification of ourselves — that is, for the Kantian he is, a passive modification of sensibility, a sensation. It remains only true that this modification does not suffice, that it is linked to the affirmation that we are its cause. And so muscular sensation, which Henry affirms with Biran plays absolutely no role in the original knowledge we have of our body's movement, in fact regains standing in the *Leçon*'s explanation of the feeling of action.

How could it have been otherwise, if in Kantianism knowledge is always the product of a collaboration between two terms, the sensible empirical given and the category? But such knowledge is a thematic knowledge of the universe; what it constitutes is the transcendent being of nature. Where in such an ontology is there room for the being of the ego, where is its concrete life, its action, its movement? Where is the origin of the knowledge I have of my body, if such knowledge can find no support in the world and cannot arise from an idea either? How can I know that it is I who act; whence come the feeling and immediate knowledge of my effort? How could I live if my own life is not given to me myself, if I am not my own life? The last question states the book's positive thesis in its most compressed form and marks the point where Henry's own philosophy of life becomes audible beneath the exposition of Biran.

Answering such questions, the prerogative of first philosophy, required rejecting Kantian ontology and possessing an ontology that is first an ontology of life, of subjectivity, and of the ego. Doubtless I judge that it is I who act, and such a judgment supposes the idea of causality in my mind; but the transcendental deduction of the categories showed that the categories do not float in the air and do not occupy our minds by chance — they have a foundation, precisely the concrete life of the ego, its action and movement, in a word its body.

One must not merely reverse Lagneau's deduction and say that the idea of necessity supposes the idea of causality and that the idea of causality supposes the idea of action; one must further see that the idea of action supposes *action itself*, that we can speak of an idea of action only if, as Lagneau momentarily understood, action is revealed in itself, and that consequently only a theory of the absolute immanence of movement in its original belonging to subjectivity can account for the ideas under which we may afterward think it. In other terms, the self's causality, which lets us feel muscular sensations as effects of our actions, is not first known through the mediation of an idea, an idea of causality; that causality, before being an idea, is a *power*, and this power is revealed to us in the same way as the being of the ego with which it coincides.

Henry has thus determined the original being of the body as belonging to the region where intentionality's self-revelation is accomplished within transcendental inner experience. Because it belongs to this region of original truth, the being of subjective movement is an immediate revelation of self to self, without appearing to itself in that revelation, through the mediation of a phenomenological distance, in the element of transcendent being. For that reason Henry affirmed that movement is known immediately and denied that muscular sensation or any other form of mediation plays the least role in this primordial knowledge, which is less a knowledge *of* our body than the phenomenological being of that body itself. That last formulation deserves attention: the knowledge is not about the body, it *is* the body, which is what the identification of ego, knowledge, and body in Chapter I required.

But all consciousness is consciousness of something; transcendental inner experience is always also a transcendent experience. If movement is genuinely an intentionality, that is because it is the very site where original truth is accomplished, and because through that accomplishment something is also announced to us in the truth of transcendent being. Only then will Henry have been right to affirm that the being of the body is genuinely that of ontological knowledge, because in its own self-revelation the being of the world too will be manifested to it. This is the turn from the chapter's negative results to its positive one: immanence would be idle if it disclosed nothing beyond itself, and the remainder of the chapter establishes what movement discloses.

Movement, however, is a *sui generis* intentionality, and when the transcendent term given to us is the strict correlate of such an intentionality, it gives itself with specific characters whose originality must be marked. What manifests itself to us when we have access to it by a movement is in no way comparable to any other transcendent content reached by an intentionality carrying a doxic thesis. Because movement is not a knowledge in the ordinary sense — because it does not carry an intentionality like the one making up the being of our theoretical life — what manifests itself to it is in no way represented; the transcendent element is here lived quite otherwise than in a representation, and the manner in which it is lived is precisely the ego's own life in the mode it now takes on, that is, movement.

If, however, one showed that movement is not merely one intentionality among others but the *deepest* intentionality of the ego's life, and consequently one found again in all other determinations of transcendental subjectivity, then one would have to say that the transcendent being in whose presence we live bears in itself the principle of a unity of all the forms it can take for us. This unity, resulting from the presence in subjectivity's life of a fundamental mode of our power of constitution — or, if one prefers, from the determination of our deepest intentionality as movement — is not only the common aspect of all aspects the world can take for us; it becomes their foundation. This foundation, the essence of transcendent being as Biran understands it, is what all other intentionalities will come to determine so as to confer on our world the totality of its human predicates.

In itself, however, and independently of the intervention of the other modes of our power of constitution, this foundation, the correlate of motor intentionality, is known to us — known not as an object reached by the intentionality of theoretical knowledge, but as what manifests itself to us well before we think of knowing it in that manner, as what on the contrary will make possible the birth of a new intentionality which, the real being of the world being already given to it, can form the project of knowing it in a thematic and intellectual knowledge of another order. The ordering is important for the argument that follows: theoretical intentionality does not disclose the world's reality but presupposes it, having received it from movement.

If the transcendent term our movement experiences, given without the mediation of any representation, is nevertheless not a mere phenomenological nothing — if it is effectively discovered in the truth of transcendent being — that is for the sole reason that the movement of our body is not an unconscious or physiological process either, but belongs in its original being and as real movement to the sphere of absolute immanence. It is the most original truth in which the being of subjective movement is bathed that makes the transcendent content it experiences posited for us in the truth of being as a determination that will be the foundation of all determinations of the world, and that is determined by nothing other than being the correlate of our movement. Our body can know the world only because it is a subjective body, a transcendental body; and reciprocally, this world of the body is a world originally known only by the body, that is, only by our movement.

To say now that the deepest intentionality of the ego's life is movement is to say that the world originally given to us is precisely this world of the body, a world whose being is at the origin only the transcendent term of movement. The determination of the being of the world as transcendent term of movement, as term resisting effort, is an essential determination; it presupposes nothing else, and makes no implicit and unavowed use of notions or categories whose effective intervention at this stage of the constitution of the real would render illusory the allegedly essential character of such a determination.

Is the resisting element not, however, in reality a *something* that resists, so that we should have to acknowledge the role of the category of substance in determining this element supposed to constitute the foundation of the real world? The idea of substance is not without relation to the resisting continuum; but far from the latter being constituted by the former, it is on the contrary its foundation, according to the very terms of the Biranian deduction of the categories. Henry is redeeming here the promise made at the end of Chapter I, § 2: the anomaly in Biran's deduction of substance now receives its correct interpretation, with the resisting continuum as ground rather than product of the category.

For this reason Lagneau's objection cannot be admitted: it seems we immediately feel resistance, but this is an illusion, since the idea of resistance supposes the idea of an exterior body resisting us and the representation of two bodies in contact in extension. Resistance is certainly not felt in the sense of being known by a sensation; but all the ideas by which Lagneau claims to account for our knowledge of the relation of movement to a resisting term in fact rest on that real relation of which I have experience.

Doubtless an idea cannot be born from a fact. But when Henry says the resisting continuum, though escaping representation and theoretical knowledge, is nonetheless manifested to us in the milieu of transcendent being, that is because the transcendence of movement toward it is a transcendental inner experience, and thus the given from which the categories draw their origin is in no way a brute fact — it is a fact in immediate relation of knowledge with itself; and if it is the foundation of theoretical knowledge, that is precisely because it is original truth itself in its immanent accomplishment. This meets the standing empiricist objection directly: the given here is not brute, so nothing is derived from a fact that is merely undergone.

The transcendental deduction of the categories thus takes on an ever deeper sense: if the categories are founded in our life, that is because what we think depends on what we are. The idea is not the foundation of the real; the contrary is true, and could be affirmed only by a philosophy having the means to make us conceive a real capable of being effectively the origin of our ideas, because it is the site where truth is originally realized.

In its determination as pure and simple correlate of a motor intentionality, the transcendent real received the name "resisting continuum." Henry has tried to show what is meant by this resisting term, and how Biran's originality consisted in accounting for the fact that it could exist for man within his most concrete experience without being the theme of a theoretical or intellectual knowledge. He closes with a remark on "continuous."

By "continuous" one should not understand a spatial continuity. Space in Biranism is not a constitutive form of my experience of the real; it is itself constituted by the development of that experience. It is in and through the unfolding of movement that the transcendent correlate resisting it acquires the extension that is thus the product rather than the condition of my first experience. The resisting element opposes my effort continually; it is the term effort always finds as the limit and also as the point of support of its own accomplishment. Registering that space is a product rather than a form is essential: it removes the last route by which the body might be reinserted into a pre-given extension.

One might then think to interpret "continuous" as expressing the temporal form in which my experience of the primitive duality is accomplished. But the temporal character of that experience does not seem to have a privilege over the constitution of space; it seems rather to coincide with the very mode in which that constitution is effected.

Calling the resisting term continuous has in fact a more original signification: it refers to the fact that this resisting term constitutes the foundation of the real, the essence of things, and does so in principle, because there is a question of right here — because the determination of the real as what resists is an *a priori* determination, and we are sure such a determination will never be absent from our experience of the real but will always constitute its foundation. This last certainty rests in turn not on a demand of our reason but on the very nature of our experience of the real, on the fact that movement is the original intentionality, in a sense a permanent intentionality of the ego's life, so that what is given us in experience ineluctably presents this essential character of being given to our movement. Our concrete life, which has transcendental inner experience of itself as subjective movement, by that very fact and at the same time has experience of the world as the transcendent term of that movement, as resisting continuum. The *a priori* here is exactly the immanent *a priori* defended in Chapter I, § 2 — a permanent feature of experience grounded in the permanence of a power, not in a requirement of reason.

It is in the eidetic determination of the correlate of the transcendental inner experience of movement that the reason lies why the certainty of that experience is shared by the term resisting it. This is why this transcendent term escapes the phenomenological reduction: because the certainty inherent in the sphere of absolute immanence where our original movement is accomplished *is* the certainty of the resisting term it reaches; and it is this signification, carrying the rejection of all problematic idealism, that is included in calling "continuous" that of which our movement is the experience. Henry is here settling the debt incurred in Chapter I, § 2, where he promised the reason at this page.

Bringing this Biranian thesis together with the Kantian critique — a rapprochement doubtless at the origin of Lagneau's fine analysis of the relation between the unity of sensing and the unity of the universe — must not, however, obscure that the premises of the common conclusion, that the existence of the external world is as certain as that of our inner life, are very different in the two cases. The Kantian proof is after all indirect: it confines itself to showing that the constitution of our inner life can claim no privilege over that of the external world but on the contrary presupposes the latter as its condition. The Biranian thesis is quite different. It cannot begin by arguing from the conditions necessary for constituting our inner life, since for it that life is in no way constituted, at least so long as one considers it in its original nature — something Kantianism scarcely troubles about, being incapable of determining the being of subjectivity at the level of absolute immanence.

The Biranian argument, phenomenological rather than reflexive in inspiration, runs: it is because our inner life as subjectivity is a sphere of absolute certainty that what it is certain of is also absolutely certain. Thus the character by which the resisting term gives itself as irreducible, as escaping the reduction, is founded precisely on the absolute certainty we have of it, that is, on the subjective being of the movement that experiences it. Our certainty is the origin of the truth of being; and it is for this reason that from the start Henry was led to determine this certainty as original truth and to elaborate an ontology showing how only in radical subjectivity can truth find a foundation. It is within man that truth dwells; and in the case at hand, it is the subjective being of movement that carries in itself the certainty we have of the reality of the world. The contrast with Kant's Refutation of Idealism is precise and is the chapter's closing result: both philosophies secure the external world, but Kant secures it by showing inner experience to be no better off, while Biran secures it by transmitting to it a certainty inner experience possesses absolutely.

## Chapter III. Movement and Sensing

The ontological theory of movement coincides with the ontological theory of the body. The body is not only movement; it is also sensing. But the decomposition of thought — here the decomposition of the faculty of sensing — shows precisely that the essence of sensing is constituted by movement. The act of sensing, first, is not known by sensation; it is on the contrary what knows sensation. Biran affirms at once the transcendental reality of sensing and the transcendent being of sensation. The body, as subjective body, coincides with the act of sensing; it is in no way a compound of sensations, whatever unity one may discover among the latter (in their correlative variations, for instance). Such a unity is a constituted unity, the unity of a transcendent mass, and so in no way the unity of the original being of our body. Biran on the visual impression and the self: whether the visual impression, confused or distinct, uniform or varied, is in the organ or outside it in space, it remains true that once the subject perceives it, that impression is not the self, the self is not identified with it. The chapter extends Chapter II's result from movement to sensing, and the extension turns on a single reversal — sensing is not built from sensations but is the power that constitutes them.

Torn from immanence and cast into transcendent being, sensation is not thereby the object of a theoretical representation. For if it is not that by which we know our body, nor that body itself, sensation is *known* by the body — not represented, precisely, but given to movement along with the unfolding of the subjective process of its effort in sensing.

Because the faculty of sensing, taken in itself, is independent of sensation, a problematic of its being must begin with a kind of reduction abstracting our power of sensing, grasping it in the pure state by separating it from everything belonging to sensation. This is properly a decomposition of thought, whose process of abstraction, however, must not be understood as delivering a term that would not suffice to itself and would still need a foundation. What the decomposition abstracts is on the contrary the essential — the body *without* sensation, the body *before* sensation, that is, the act of sensing considered in itself and as a pure power. We may therefore suppose this "pure body" applies itself to sensing in the absence of any object and so of any sensible property. What we find then, what remains when we have removed everything transcendent in our sensible life, is that life itself, which Biran determines at the root of each sense as what constitutes its own being. Biran: set aside the object; the same voluntary determination can occur anew apart from any external force, the acts reproduce and are apperceived in their own unchanged power — the only thing absolutely lacking is the sensation. The methodological point deserves marking against the standard reading: the abstraction does not isolate a dependent residue but the load-bearing thing, the power itself.

At the moment this abstraction of a pure power — the root of each of our senses — is performed, Biran immediately and unequivocally determines the nature of that power as subjectivity. It is the subjective determination of this origin of our power of sensing that makes the movements by which we are conscious of exercising such a power not physiological determinations of our organs but original movements immediately in our possession. Whatever sensations we set ourselves to procure — visual, olfactory, auditory, tactile — it is always through one and the same power that we go toward them and obtain them. Visual sensations, for example, are under the immediate dependence of the movement of our gaze. It is this movement that delivers the yellow of the wall facing me, then, through the window it crosses, the tints of winter's dead woods in the park, and above them the pale blue of the sky. The unity of our visual sensations is a constituted unity, but that unity has a foundation lying precisely in the power that constitutes it, which is nothing but the subjective movement of the gaze. Henry's own descriptive register enters here, and the concrete example is doing argumentative work: the sensations do not organize themselves; the gaze organizes them by moving.

The determination of visual sensations in visual space — the determination constituting the structure of the visual world — in no way results from an immediate relation between a given sensation and a given determination of space. Space by itself is totally indeterminate; a determination cannot be conferred on it, as was long believed, by the insertion of a visual sensation, since the relation of the sensation to its place is a universal and empty relation, always the same whatever the sensation. The sensation is always where it is, and that "where" ceases to be totally indeterminate and becomes a spatial determination properly speaking only under the condition of a real or possible movement of my gaze that situates it, that determines it spatially in relation to me. Only then does it become possible to say that a given visual sensation is here or there, that the pale blue of the sky is above the blacks, blues, and violets of the winter forest. The unity of our visual sensations is therefore not established directly among them; it is not an immediate unity but results from the mediation of our power over them, that is, from the unity of the subjective movement of our gaze. It is in our power over it that the unity of the world originally resides; this unity is accomplished at the very stage of the sensible constitution of the universe, in a manner immanent to the exercise of each of our senses taken separately. It is by the movements of my eyes that I seize all things, and it is first by opening them that a visual spectacle manifests itself to me. The critique of the "local sign" doctrine is exact: a place-relation identical across all sensations cannot individuate places, so space must be a product of motor exploration rather than a container of sensations.

What Henry says of sight holds for the other senses. The decomposition of smell brings to light a subjective act — that by which we smell odors — and those odors themselves as transcendent terms of our movements of inhalation. Such movements are in reality always one and the same movement, and on its identity rests the unity of the sensory world considered. Likewise in hearing, a motor action is accomplished interiorly, and it is to this that sounds are given. Above all in the analysis of the active vocal organ Biran brought to light the motor function that helps render perceptible the impression made by a foreign cause. The voice repeats the sound heard, and the subjective movement in which the sonorous element is apprehended is thus itself repeated, reinforced; it becomes a voluntary act whose ontological status is nonetheless similar to that of the first subjective act of apprehension. It is this identity within a single status — that of the original being of subjective movement — that lets us speak of a second act repeating the first. Biran: at the instant the sonorous disturbance is communicated to hearing, besides the simultaneous motor reaction completing the sensation, there is a further determination of the same order setting the vocal instrument in play; the latter repeats the exterior sound and echoes it; hearing is struck by two impressions, one direct, the other reflected and interior — two imprints added, or rather the same one doubled. Both sonorous impressions are constituted, the power of constitution is the same in both cases — a motor reaction or action, the original being of subjective movement, the body.

The determination of the original being of the body as subjective movement furnishes the principle of a phenomenology of memory whose possibility thus rests entirely on the ontological theory of the body. When a sound is heard, the sonorous impression is constituted; but the subjective movement in which the operative power of constitution consists knows itself originally as such, since it is given in a transcendental inner experience. It is precisely possessing the interior law of constitution of the sonorous impression that lets me repeat that impression, reproduce it myself anew as often as I please, and recognize it throughout that reproduction — because the knowledge of the power of constitution is immanent to its exercise and coincides with it. What repeats the sonorous impression is the body, hence the ego, which is to say that the power of constitution of the sonorous impression is the ego itself. While I repeat the impression, I know that I have already had experience of it, that I am now repeating it, that it is I who repeat it, and that it is the same impression I already experienced that I now repeat. This is the chapter's most productive result: memory is not a faculty added to the body but a structural feature of the body's mode of being, since a power that knows itself in its exercise thereby knows its exercise as repeatable.

The reminiscence implied in this phenomenon splits into a reminiscence of the power of constitution — the repetition properly speaking — and a reminiscence of the sonorous impression, the reminiscence of the repeated or reproduced term. The first is accomplished on the plane of transcendental immanence, produced without any constitution, knowing itself as such interiorly and immediately. The second concerns the transcendent plane on which the sonorous impression is constituted before being recognized and repeated there. Biran calls the first "personal reminiscence," the second "modal reminiscence."

This distinction serves only the clarity of the analysis, for modal reminiscence in fact rests on personal reminiscence, or rather coincides with it. If the sonorous impression constituted in transcendent being is recognized while repeated, and recognized precisely as a repeated impression, that is because the power operating its constitution recognizes itself originally in its repetition; so repetition and recognition of the impression are nothing but the unity and permanence of the ego across the deployment of its powers of constitution and the repetition of their exercise. In the vocal repetition of a sonorous impression first heard, we face a relation of unity to diversity — the unity of the ego that hears and utters the sound, and the diversity of the sonorous impressions, which, even identical in their being, are individually differentiated by the place they occupy in time, making one the first, another the second. This relation, precisely because memory is a corporeal phenomenon, is found in any sensory activity as a constitutive element of it. As the sensations vary and succeed one another while effort remains the same, there is plurality sensed or perceived within unity; and thus the body, as the subjective unity of movement, is the principle of the unity of the power to which the infinite diversity of sensible impressions is given. Biran: setting out from the same principle of motility, I followed it in the exercise of each sense in particular. So it is the exercise of motility — the faculty of making movements and being conscious of them — that constitutes the root of our power of sensing, as the decomposition of the motor function of touch shows.

This decomposition immediately brings to light the subjective movement of touch as the principle of all our tactile sensations. In touch, better than in any other sense, we see how this fundamental mode, movement, can concur with the exercise of various senses; for it is by directing our movements on and against things that we make tactile sensations arise in us, sensations that come to cover over the very substance of the real — since, according to Biran, it is that substance that in the exercise of touch we reach immediately as the transcendent term of our effort. For this reason motor touch delivers something quite other than an indeterminate sensible layer that would still need to receive the transcendent signification of being the immediate manifestation of the real. Motor touch being a movement, what manifests itself to it is things themselves in the resistance they offer us, and the tactile sensations inserted as it were into this resisting continuum belong to it and are the sensible determinations of the real being of the world. The signification of the transcendent content of our experience of motor touch — a signification transcendent relative to the sensible given properly so called — is nonetheless included in that given, insofar as it gives itself precisely to our movement.

But, on Biran's own account, movement is immanent to the exercise of each of our senses, and consequently the privilege of touch must be shared by all the other senses; it belongs in principle to sensory activity in general. There is not one sense making the real world known to us and others delivering only sensations to which the signification of manifesting real being would have to be added — by their constant association with our tactile perceptions, for instance. Having given a radical signification to the thesis of the immanence of subjective movement to sensory activity in general, Henry rejects the Biranian privilege of touch, or rather extends it to the life of all the senses, and by the same stroke affirms that the sensible world in general is the real world. This correction of Biran is one of the chapter's genuine advances, and it is internal: Biran's own premise (movement immanent to every sense) undercuts the privilege Biran grants touch.

If it is true that the feeling of causality associates itself in various ways with different impressions — by a relation of derivation if they issue from the will, by a simple relation of coexistence or simultaneity if they are passive by nature — it is also true and necessary to affirm that our impressions are all constituted by the original being of subjective movement, which, in the movement of constitution projecting them as it were onto the ground of the resisting continuum, confers on them this transcendent signification (immanent therefore to the signification of the sensible in general) of being an immediate manifestation of real being itself. The diversity or even the contingency of our sensations is only the expression of the infinitely diverse manner in which being manifests itself to us; but everywhere and always, whatever the mode, this manifestation is genuinely a manifestation, the very unveiling of being disclosing itself to us in its truth.

Sight, then, does not offer us mute images floating somewhere in the air, in an interval stretching between the real and us. Sounds and colors do not compose a sensible film that in its indigence would demand, like the matter of the Ancients, an intellectual form to think and determine it, while the world of touch alone would have consistency and self-sufficiency. Each sensory world is a real world and an autonomous world. But the reason for this self-sufficiency and autonomy is precisely the reason this world does not form an isolated world but is in reality one with all the other sensory worlds. The visual world is not real because I can also touch the things it manifests. By itself already it manifested *things* to me, not colored images, and that is precisely why I can also touch those things — for one touches only things, not phantoms. So what makes the visual world real is also what makes it accessible to all my other senses. What I see is also what I can touch, hear, or smell. The foundation of this "also," which is the foundation of the reality of each sensory world, is the resisting continuum, immanent to each of these worlds because subjective movement is immanent to the exercise of each sense, because it is the very being of the body. The argument closes a potential regress: reality is not conferred on one sense-world by another, since each already reaches the real; their unity is the single resisting continuum reached by the single subjective movement running through them all.

It is this immanence to the various senses of the power constituting the resisting continuum that explains why the continuum is not transcendent to the sensible given but can form the common site of all our impressions. This is why we need no recourse to an idea — the idea of substance or the idea of the real — to found the reality of the world of our daily experience: that real is already implied in sensible experience itself. How, besides, could one found the real on an idea? Is it not obviously the contrary that is true?

Henry can now formulate conclusions letting us understand the principle of the unity of our senses, how that unity is a knowing, and finally what the individuality of human reality consists in as a sensible individuality — the three §§.

### § 1. The Unity of the Senses and the Problem of the Relation Between Our Images and Our Movements

Our sensations have by themselves no unity; they are heterogeneous, furnishing only a pure diversity. But our sensations considered in themselves are only abstractions, since in fact they are always constituted by a power to which subjective movement is immanent. This is to say that this power is our body's and that what it constitutes is the real itself, reached in its being as resisting term. This term, whose constitution is effected with the exercise of our senses, is in a sense appresented by the specifically sensible content of our experience. In the phenomenon of this appresentation lie: the reason the sensible gives itself as an immediate manifestation of being itself rather than an unreal efflorescence covering over the existence of a something = x; and the principle of the transcendent unity of the sensible world. Because such unity is that of the resisting continuum running through the various sensory worlds and founding in each its reality along with its openness to all the others, it rests on the unity of the power constituting this real and single continuum, on the unity of our subjective body. That in turn rests on the internal structure of subjectivity itself, where the ultimate origin and essence of all possible unity lies.

Outside this original ontological sphere there exists only a constituted unity, and the commonest philosophical illusion consists in building the unity of the world on a unity that, not being original, cannot play the role expected of it but itself demands a foundation. Lagneau was dupe of such an illusion when he thought to rest the unity of the external sensible world on the unity of our internal sensations, on what he calls a fixed order of the sentient. Doubtless it is on a fixed order of sensing that the unity of our sensible experience rests, but that fixed order cannot consist in the unity of the concomitant variations of our internal sensations. These are transcendent like all sensations; the unity they manifest is a constituted unity, which only the unity of the power constituting the sensible world in general can account for. Only by thinking of the subjective sphere to which this power of constitution belongs can we understand its unity along with the origin of the transcendent unities it grounds.

Henry illustrates on a precise example: the problem of the relation between our images and our movements, which has always preoccupied psychologists who tried in vain to solve it. In *L'imaginaire* Sartre takes up the problem with the example of the movement by which I trace a curve and a circle in space with my finger — the relation of the visual image of that curve to the movements by which I draw it. Sartre first criticizes the psychologist Dwelshauvers, who decomposes the process into: the idea of a movement to be accomplished; the incarnation of that idea in an unconscious motor attitude; and an image provoked in consciousness as a registration of the motor reaction, qualitatively different from the elements of that reaction. This, a variant of the classical thesis, is plainly inadmissible. In our natural life we do not form the idea of our movements before executing them: taking a matchbox from my pocket or striking the match, these very simple movements are preceded in my consciousness by no representation, nor by anything at all. The incarnation of an idea we do not have in a third-person motor attitude we do not know is a first mystery. The appearance, as a consequence of this unconscious motor process, of the visual image I am conscious of, is a new mystery. The affirmation of a relation between this image and such a motor process is only the expression, in the language of the reflexive mythology of the unconscious, of the bare fact of the image's appearance. That appearance is in no way explained, and its relation to the so-called physiological motor process is wholly unintelligible — whereas the whole problem is precisely to explain that relation.

What then is Sartre's solution? It appeals to Husserl's analyses of the temporal mode of the constitution of our impressions, but this recourse to theses valid in themselves only masks the absence of any real answer. The visual image of the trajectory described by my index finger is constituted from the visual impressions by a series of protentions and retentions separated by the concrete present, which for Sartre is a kinesthetic present. But the kinesthetic impressions are themselves constituted and unified by retentional and protentional acts, though this last constitution — which would culminate in awareness of a transcendent motor form actually existing, that is, in a kinesthetic perception — is in reality accomplished only in the shadow of the visual perception of my finger's trajectory. Otherwise, the appearance in the field of consciousness of this motor form would be accompanied, as Sartre notes, by the disappearance of the image. So in the ordinary visual perception of the curve, as long as it is not interrupted by kinesthetic perception of our movements, retention and protention retain and anticipate the vanished and future phases of the movement under the aspect they would have had if I had perceived them by sight; every retention is thus a conversional retention, a conversion of the kinesthetic into the visual. Sartre attaches his analysis to protention alone, which should be simpler since the future impression need not be converted.

Consider the case where, from the present sensible content corresponding to the actual position of my hand and finger, consciousness awaits a visual sensation. The sensible impression supporting these intentions is kinesthetic, since it is from the movement of my arm that I await the accomplishment of the visual form of the curve. This impression cannot give itself as visual; yet it is the extreme point of a past that gives itself as visual. On one hand it is the sole concrete element of the intended form, conferring on it its character of presence and furnishing the degraded knowing the something it aims at; on the other it draws its sense, scope, and value from intentions aiming at visual impressions — it was itself awaited and received as a visual impression. This does not suffice to make it a sensation of sight, but it suffices to give it a visual *sense*; this kinesthetic impression endowed with a visual sense will thus function as *analogon* of a visual form. Henry's reductio is exact and devastating: as well say that when I expect an airplane and a goat arrives in its place, I take the goat for the airplane, or at least, while perceiving it as goat, confer on it the signification of being an airplane. The critique Sartre directs at Dwelshauvers strikes his own theory full on.

The convergence is no surprise if the two authors share a fundamental postulate — as Sartre explicitly declares, that we are informed directly of the movements of our body by a special type of sensation, kinesthetic sensations. This leads him to pose the problem in the same terms as Dwelshauvers and the classics: how can kinesthetic sensations serve as matter for an imaging consciousness aiming at an object furnished by visual perceptions? So posed, the problem is insoluble, because it requires finding a passage from the kinesthetic to the visual, and this passage must occur on the very plane of the sensible. But the being of the sensible is an opaque and irreducible totality.

When one speaks of the signification of the sensible, one must watch what one says. The sensible can perfectly well bear a signification, and that signification can even be sensible — as when a blue refers us to a red. But a blue is a blue and a red is a red. The signification of a blue cannot make that blue itself, in its own sensible being, a red — a proposition that is, phenomenologically, the very type of absurdity. All the more, passing from one sensory field to another, is it true that a kinesthetic sensation cannot by any subterfuge transform itself into a visual sensation. The kinesthetic is wholly kinesthetic and only kinesthetic, and the same holds for the sensible character proper to each sense. Precisely because the sensible world is a real world, one has no leisure to make it become anything other than what it is. One does not sense what one wishes. Our power of intentionality is not exercised gratuitously; the intentionality aiming at a given sensible content is rigorously determined — I cannot direct on a goat the intentionality constitutive of an airplane. The constitution of a visual image, of the curve my finger traces, cannot rest on a kinesthetic sensation. The sensible data are rigorously heterogeneous; each sensible content refers to a specific mode of constitution and cannot receive the signification of being anything other than what it is. The intervention of time has no power to lift the radical and irreducible heterogeneity between the kinesthetic and the visual as such, and the relation of our images and our movements is absolutely unintelligible and indeed impossible — *if* it is true that the movement of our body is known through kinesthetic sensations and that the visual image of the curve is in some way second relative to the plurality of visual impressions. The whole reductio hangs on that double conditional, which the ontological theory is about to reject.

Before showing how the ontological theory of the body — substituting subjective movement and the act of vision for kinesthetic impressions and visual sensations — furnishes the foundation of the relation between our senses, Henry exposes the inconsistencies of the classical theories regarding the role assigned to kinesthetic sensation. In Sartre's analysis that role is considerable: kinesthetic sensation is the origin of our knowledge of our body's movement, so that only through it can the relation of our movements to our images be established; and this same sensation serves to define the present, a present strangely resembling the coenesthetic present of the empiricists or Bergson's sensorimotor present. The present is not a sensible present but an ontological present; but what matters here is that the importance of the role assigned to kinesthetic sensation accords ill with the total uncertainty otherwise reigning about its exact nature. In a note of *L'imaginaire* Sartre says he explained the "motor basis of the image" by holding to James's thesis on the peripheral origin of the feeling of effort, without taking account of the hypothesis — made by certain contemporaries, notably Mourgue — that there might exist sketched, outlined, retained movements, motor impressions not originating in muscular contractions; and should that hypothesis be confirmed, it will suffice to conceive that the imaging intention applies itself to these non-peripheral motor impressions.

This is singular in a phenomenological perspective: the kinesthetic impression, supposed to constitute the being of the present and the foundation of the relation of our images and movements, is a something = x, totally indeterminate and frankly hypothetical, to the point of becoming the object of the hypotheses of psychologists and scientists. More: this uncertainty about the being of kinesthetic sensation as "motor basis of the image" must receive its true name — it is in reality an absolute unconsciousness. The unconsciousness of the kinesthetic sensation as alleged element of the knowledge of the movement by which I draw a circle results directly from the ontological thesis that movement, immediately present to us as transcendental inner experience, is otherwise totally ignorant of the alleged instruments by which it would be accomplished. This unconsciousness is recognized by Sartre himself, who declared in his critique of Dwelshauvers that the appearance of the kinesthetic sensation would be accompanied by the disappearance of the image. How then could this sensation — whose being is handed over to the speculations of theories, which does not make movement known to us, and whose mere presence to consciousness suffices to exclude that of the image — furnish the principle of the relation of our movements and images and of our knowledge of them? Henry turns Sartre's own admission against him: a datum whose appearance abolishes the image cannot be what grounds the image.

There certainly exist kinesthetic impressions, and their constitution can be the explicit theme of thought. I then have thematic knowledge of one of my movements — but precisely, I have ceased to trace a figure in space with my finger. In the case Henry is studying there is likewise room for kinesthetic impressions, which do not escape the overall constitution of the phenomenon. That is no reason to make them play a role they do not play; and if he calls them unconscious, that is because he holds to an attitude of reduction aiming to bring to light the essential of the phenomenon and thereby deliver the foundation of the relation of our movements and images.

That foundation is this: in the original phenomenon of tracing a curve in space with my index finger, the movement of my gaze constituting the spatial figure of the curve is *the same* as the movement of my hand tracing the curve, and the unity of these two movements that are one is an ontological unity, a unity in the absolute immanence of subjectivity. The theme of thought here is the visual image of the curve, the transcendent object of my gaze but also of the subjective movement of my hand that traces such a curve, that touches it as it were, and that creates it. The theme, or more simply the term, of my hand's movement is in no way a kinesthetic impression; that impression is *absent* from the central and original phenomenon constituted by the subjective movement and its transcendent correlate, and it is this absence Henry meant in declaring the impression unconscious. The dissolution of the pseudo-problem is complete: there is no passage from kinesthetic to visual because there are not two sensations to be bridged, but one subjective movement with one transcendent correlate.

The constitution of kinesthetic impressions is a marginal, secondary phenomenon, whence their character of profiling themselves in shadow, within our body as it were, in a part of the spectacle that does not appear in full light and in the most manifest truth of transcendent being. What occupies that place in the light is the curve I trace.

The marginal phenomenon of the constitution of kinesthetic impressions is nonetheless, in its way, decisive, for with it begins the being of *constituted* movement and our own body announces itself to us in transcendent being, appearing there as the wake of the original being of the subjective body. One must beware of confusing this first constituted layer of our body — the layer giving our constituted body, in this first phase of its constitution, the character of being always short of the world, in the margin of the spectacle we relate to — with the original being of the body, that is, absolute subjectivity. This distinction between the subjective body and the first constituted layer of the body is a permanent acquisition for the rest of the work and its terminology should be fixed now. The character by which kinesthetic impressions give themselves as "in us," as opposed to the curve my hand traces in the world giving itself as exterior to us, composes only a wholly relative interiority having nothing to do with the interiority of subjective movement, which is an absolute interiority. To the latter Henry gives the name *ontological interiority*, to mark that it belongs to an original region of being characterized by an immediate relation to self, whereas the interiority of the constituted body — the interiority making us speak of an "in us" as opposed to a "outside us" about it too — is itself constituted, has no ontological character, and cannot serve to define a region of being, since it too, like what is "outside us," is situated in transcendent being.

If the marginal phenomenon of the constitution of our kinesthetic impressions signifies a first manifestation of our body in transcendent being, and must on that account be set aside from the description of the central phenomenon where the relation of our images and movements is accomplished, taking it into account nonetheless helps us better understand what that original relation consists in. The constitution of our kinesthetic impressions does not accompany only the subjective movement by which I trace the curve in space; such a constitution can also accompany the exercise of vision, and it is by the operation of this marginal constitution that we come to speak of the movements of our eyeballs. Hence the existence of kinesthetic sensations accompanying vision is not only a striking confirmation of the thesis that subjective movement is immanent to the existence of our different powers of sensing; it further lets us understand that the relation of our movements and images in no way reduces to the relation of our kinesthetic sensations and our visual sensations, since both types of sensation are present in the sole exercise of vision. This is the argument's clinching move: kinesthesis attends even pure seeing, so it cannot be the bridge between seeing and moving.

A correct phenomenological description of the phenomenon of tracing the curve with my index finger must therefore distinguish, first, a secondary and marginal act of constitution, splitting in reality into an act constituting the kinesthetic impressions accompanying my hand's movement and a wholly similar act constituting the kinesthetic impressions accompanying my gaze's movement. This double act culminates in constituting a double movement — the "movement of my hand" and the "movement of my eyes." These two constituted movements are like the double wake the original act of the subjective body — movement properly speaking — makes spring up immediately behind it. But they are accomplished in penumbra; when they form the theme of thought, the central phenomenon vanishes.

Second, that central phenomenon, consisting precisely in the act of tracing the curve with the tip of my index finger (abstracting from the wake this act leaves in the "interior" body through the constitution of kinesthetic impressions as movements of our organs), decomposes into two sorts of elements: those belonging to the immanence of absolute subjectivity and those that are constituted. The theory of subjective movement lets us grasp the deep unity running through all these elements, found in some as original unity, in others as founded unity. The movement grasping the curve traced in space decomposes into a movement of my hand and a movement of my gaze; but this decomposition occurs within the transcendental sphere and issues in no real division. It expresses rather the unity of the ego's concrete life, immanent to the deployment of all its powers, because the root of these is the original being of subjective movement, whose unity we conceive as soon as we understand its nature — to be given to us in a transcendental inner experience.

What this unity of the movements of gaze and hand is, the nature of the transcendent term they reach lets us specify, though this unity is itself founded on the first, subjective and original unity. It is one and the same curve I trace, see, and could equally sense — if I drew it, say, in a cool draft affecting my fingertip with a determinate sensation. The theory of the transcendent term of movement already furnished the principle of this curve's unity: there is not one curve traced, another seen, another sensed, because each sensory world is a real world and consequently all sensible worlds form one and the same world; and the foundation of the unity and reality of the sensible world is precisely the original being of subjective movement.

When Henry speaks of the unity of the ego's absolute life, he in no way means that life is monotonous; it is in reality infinitely diverse. The ego is not a pure logical subject enclosed in its tautology; it is the very being of infinite life, which nonetheless remains one in this diversity and in the activity by which it traces figures, sees them, and senses them — because this diversity belongs to it precisely insofar as it is given to it in a transcendental inner experience. Again, it is the ontological status of this life that is the principle of its unity and hence of the unity of the world. The unity of the senses, more deeply the unity of corporeal life, finds its foundation in the ontological structure of subjectivity, and consequently the theory of that unity is contained in principle in the ontological theory of the body. Henry notes in passing that developing these views alone could lead to a satisfactory theory of symbolism.

### § 2. The Unity of the Body Interpreted as That of a Knowing. Habit and Memory

Having grasped the principle of the unity of the senses, Henry can show how that unity is genuinely a knowing and what that knowing consists in — under three heads.

First, as a transcendental inner experience, our body is an immediate self-knowing. One must see that the immediate experience of our body delivers nothing but itself; the body is not first a being and then an experience we have of such a being — a being that would preexist that experience or exist independently of it. Our body does announce itself as overflowing the experience we have of it, but that body is not the original body now in question. For the latter, its being is that of the immediate experience we have of it; it is a being that is an appearing — but, as Henry has shown, an appearing of a determinate type, an appearance delivering itself in the absence of all phenomenological distance and coinciding with this manner of giving itself. It is by this that our body is originally a knowing. Our body is a power, but this power is an immediate self-knowing, a knowing that does not presuppose the horizon of the truth of being already open to us but is on the contrary the foundation and origin of that truth. The order of foundation is the section's first claim: bodily self-knowledge does not occur within a prior openness to being; it opens it.

Second, at the same time as a transcendental inner experience, our body is a transcendent experience. Precisely because the self-knowing of the original body is not a thematic knowing — because the "self" and the ipseity of the body are not the term but the *condition* of this knowing — it is not closed on itself, is not knowledge of self, but knowledge of transcendent being in general. Here we find again the being of absolute subjectivity in its root and deepest structure. Because such a being is not constituted, it is a power of constitution; because it gives itself to itself without appearing at any moment, in that act of self-giving, in transcendent being, that region of transcendent being remains free for it, and something can be given to it in that region. In this original ontological structure of the body as absolute subjectivity lies the reason our body knows the world without knowing the "instruments" with which it was supposed, on classical views, to know it, and the reason its knowledge of the world is accomplished without recourse to any "means." This restates the second result of Chapter II in the register of sensing, and the reason is the same: only a self-giving that does not become an object leaves the field of objects free to be given.

Our body has, certainly, the power to know its members and the various sense organs said to compose its being; but what it then knows is still an element of the world, its knowledge is still a knowledge of the world, in no way a knowledge of the *instrument* of its knowledge of the world. If the "instruments" with which I know the world, if the powers of my body rose before me in transcendent being, I would no longer know the world — or rather I would know a new sector of it, that of the transcendent body, while the other sectors disappeared into penumbra. Thus when I attended to my kinesthetic sensations, the curve my hand traced in space was only the uncertain, vague object of a marginal consciousness. The powers of my body deliver the being of the world to me only on condition of belonging to absolute immanence, of being known in a knowledge where the concept of world plays no role. The knowledge of the world by the body and the original knowledge of the body by itself are nonetheless not two different knowledges, since the second is the very substance of the first. Transcendent experience is in itself a transcendental inner experience; original experience is one in which both the being of the world and the being of the body are present to us, though the mode of that presence differs radically: the body is present in the absolute immanence of subjectivity, the world in transcendent being.

Turning to this knowledge of the world that is the body's prerogative, it is, Henry has said, neither intellectual nor even representative. When I consider the act by which I take the matchbox in my pocket, the knowledge I have of that box is only the one the movement seizing and using it has of it. The relations intervening in such a knowledge — which we may less equivocally call a *process*, since it is wholly immanent to that process whose very being it constitutes — are not objective spatial relations but relations that are the exact replica, the continuous correlate as it were, of the movements I effect. Objects are not originally, nor even usually, contemplated objects; they are the objects of our movements. It is in this sense that the original being of things is not a *Vorhanden* but a *Zuhanden*. Henry's use of the Heideggerian pair is deliberate and marks a convergence: readiness-to-hand names exactly the mode in which the body's world is given, prior to and founding the present-at-hand of theoretical contemplation.

But in affirming the original relation of the being of things to our movements — saying that objects are not first represented but immediately lived by the powers through which we relate to them — Henry claims no primacy of the hand over sight. Vision is a knowledge of the same type as manual prehension or motor touch: a knowledge that is not intellectual or theoretical, not a representation, because it operates through the body, because it is a corporeal knowledge, because it is the deed of subjective movement, as the decomposition of the faculty of sensing showed. We must place ourselves within the powers the body deploys to understand the nature of the world our body knows — and it is within those powers, in truth, that we *are* placed. This is why corporeal knowledge is not a provisional knowledge, primitive perhaps but quickly surpassed by the intelligent man; it is on the contrary a primordial and irreducible ontological knowledge, the foundation and ground of all our knowledges, in particular our intellectual and theoretical ones.

Third, this corporeal knowledge of the world is not an *actual* knowledge. Our body is not exactly a knowledge; it is rather a power of knowledge, the principle of infinitely varied, multiple, yet coordinated knowledges of which it is genuinely the proprietor. Biran identified the being of ontological knowledge with the ego's; but the ego is the body. This is why ontological knowledge is not an empty possibility, why its existence is not a virtual existence needing the concourse of a foreign reality to pass into act, why it is a real being — because it is the very being of our body, its concrete and infinite life. But how can a pure possibility be a concrete being? What is the being of the body if it is precisely the concrete being of pure ontological possibility? The paradox restated here — a possibility that is nonetheless actual and concrete — was announced in Chapter I, § 2 about the category; habit is its resolution.

A Biran text illuminates the point: all the movements executed by the hand, all the positions it took traversing the solid, can be repeated voluntarily in the absence of that solid. These movements are the signs of various elementary perceptions relative to the primary qualities; they can therefore serve to recall their ideas, and this recall, executed by means of available signs, constitutes memory properly speaking — so there will be a genuine memory of tangible forms. The hypothesis of the solid's absence during the reproduction of the hand's movement belongs to the general project of the decomposition of the faculty of sensing; it is again only a fiction meant to bring out the essence of the phenomenon.

As in the case of hearing and the voluntary reproduction of a sonorous impression, Henry distinguishes in this phenomenon of reproducing the prehensile movement four knowledges: the original knowledge of the movement by itself; the recognition of this movement as the same as one already effected; the knowledge of the transcendent term of the movement, namely the solid; and the recognition of this transcendent term as one already reached by the same movement. Specifying the nature of these knowledges, understanding why they are identified within a single primordial knowing, is to give the Biranian phenomenology of memory its full development and at the same time to trace back to its foundation, the being of ontological knowledge, identical with the body's.

Reflecting first on the knowledge and recognition of the solid, the mode in which it is given to us has an absolutely general signification: things are never present to the body in an experience bearing the character of having to be unique; always, on the contrary, they give themselves as what one will see twice. The being of an object is what I can reach under the condition of a certain movement.
