Since that movement is a proper, irreducible, inalienable, in a word ontological possibility of my body, it follows that the being of the world is what I can always reach, what is accessible to me in principle. Each time an object is given to my body, it gives itself not so much as the object of a present experience as something my body can reach, something subject to a power the body has over it. When it seems otherwise — when we see a landscape or a face we will not see again — this new signification attaching to our experience is only a *negative* determination of the general signification under which the world gives itself to our body, and far from excluding that general signification, it finds in it its foundation and is only a determination of it. The point matters for what follows about death: even irrecoverability is a mode of the general accessibility, not its cancellation.

The same holds for the actual disappearance of any one of my body's powers — vision, say — or their global disappearance, represented in the idea of death. That idea is only a determination of the general signification of our experience of the world. The world is the totality of the contents of all my body's experiences, the term of all my real or possible movements. This term is indefinitely evocable, these contents are in principle always accessible to me, because my movement is not a present and as it were empirical state of my body but its being is the very being of ontological knowledge. It is this identity of the original being of movement with that of ontological knowledge itself that Henry expresses by saying the body is a power, that its knowledge is not limited to the instant but is a possibility of knowledge in general, the real and concrete possibility that a world be given to me. Henry calls *habit* the real and concrete being of ontological possibility, and expresses the idea that the body is a power by saying it is a habit, the ensemble of our habits. As for the world, it is the term of all our habits, and it is in this sense that we are genuinely its *inhabitants*. To inhabit, to frequent the world, is the deed of human reality, and this character of habitation is an ontological character serving to define the world as much as the body that inhabits it.

Returning to the Biranian example and its four fundamental knowledges (knowledge and recognition of the hand's movement and its transcendent term), we see why there is in reality no ground to distinguish between knowledge and recognition. If all knowledge is also a recognition, that is because it is not the deed of an isolated act but of subjectivity itself, of a power — because it is not an empirical but an ontological knowledge. What the solid is present to in my knowledge of it is my hand — not a singular act of taking but a general possibility of prehension which, in its present (the ontological present), bears in it all past and future prehensions of this solid and of all solids in general. This is the sense of saying the being of my body is habit, a general and indefinite possibility of knowledges. This possibility is the real being of the ego, its ontological actuality, the identity of the body; it is also, as Biran says, the very durability of our personal individuality. The body is not an instantaneous knowing; it is that permanent knowing which is my existence itself; it is memory.

The mnemic character inherent in the power to sense and act — the memory of touch and tactile forms is only an example — forms a constant theme of Biran's thought. The self alone, he says, remembers what it has perceived or operated by its constitutive force. It is the immanence of this constitutive force to all the self's apperceptions or operations that makes the knowing they carry always a memory, always a possibility of knowing in general. The body that remembers does not separate from the first pathways that led it to things; it keeps in itself the secret of access to all the objects of its surroundings; it is the key to the universe, extending its power over all that exists. What remains beyond its reach receives that signification of being refused to it only within a more primitive power of access and openness to the world. Thus, because movement belongs to absolute immanence, our knowledge of the universe — which is the body's — takes on the character of never being a *new* knowledge; it is, if not in its empirical content then at least in its ontological structure and first possibility, a knowledge as old as our own existence.

Henry has said the body is habit; he now says it is memory. How to understand these two affirmations, and what relation do memory and habit have? Are they two names for one phenomenon? What is that phenomenon; is it truly a foundation, and how do memory and habit stand relative to it?

Returning to the example of the hand apprehending a solid, Henry has shown that its four immanent modes of knowledge reduce to two: on one hand, knowledge and recognition of the movement (which were one and coincided with the very being of subjective movement); on the other, knowledge and recognition of the transcendent term, likewise identified within a transcendent unity of signification founded on the originally subjective unity of the movement. It is in this reduction of recognition to knowledge — a reduction giving knowledge its character of no longer being individuated and instantaneous but properly ontological — that Henry defined the phenomenon of habit.

In the phenomenon described (the act by which I take my matchbox from my pocket each day, several times a day, before smoking), no signification of past appears. Such a phenomenon does not, properly speaking, belong to memory. Yet the act of taking my matchbox is not an act individuated in time, an act present at the moment I accomplish it and then falling into the past. What is past is irremediably lost, taken forever from our grasp and power; at most we can keep the memory of it, but the memory is the consciousness of something as past — that is, far from restoring in person what has sunk back in time, it furnishes only an image whose sense is precisely to deliver its object as what we have lost. Such a description in no way suits the act of prehending a solid. That act is not past; it is by essence a permanent possibility offered to me, a power dominating past, present, and future, whose ontological structure defined as habit lets us conceive what the real being of the pure possibility of an ontological knowledge is. The distinction between habit and memory turns on this: memory delivers its object *as lost*, habit as *available*, and the two cannot be the same phenomenon.

At the same time another no less essential truth announces itself. The knowledge in which subjective movement apprehends the solid, being an ontological knowledge, a general possibility of knowing that solid, does not know it as a *this* individualized in time; it knows it as what it can always know within this same movement, knows and recognizes it in a knowledge the movement produces and can always reproduce. The theory of habit reduced recognition to knowledge, but it is more exact to say it understood this knowledge as what bears in itself a recognition. This recognition, immanent to the knowledge of the solid by the movement and making knowledge by movement an ontological knowledge, can at any moment become the explicit theme of thought. A new intentionality then arises, in which the recognition of the solid is the theme of an explicit thought: the solid is recognized as what has already been known, or the prehensile movement is explicitly posited as a movement already produced. I then have, in the first case, the memory of the solid; in the second, the memory of the knowledge I formerly had of it, the memory of the prehensile movement accomplished by me in the past. Habit is the foundation of memory, and since it defines the ontological structure of the body, it is right to see in the being of the body the principle of our acts of remembering and recall. It is because the original being of the subjective body is the real being of ontological knowledge, because it is a possibility of knowledge in general, a knowledge of the world in its absence, that it is also and for that reason a memory of the world, a memory of its forms, an *a priori* knowledge of its being and determinations. Biran, in a text of infinite depth: the memory of an act contains the feeling of the power to repeat it.

To this feeling of the power to repeat an act — immanent to the memory and its foundation — Henry gives its true name: it is the transcendental inner experience of the original being of our subjective body. The unity of our body is the feeling of the immanence, in all the modes of our concrete life, of this power to produce and repeat, the immediate experience of this ontological power. This unity is therefore again that of ontological knowledge; in it and by it the unity of the world is constituted; it is itself the transcendental unity founding that unity and, on the transcendental plane, conferring on each of our movements and acts the character by which they are lived by us as one and the same power whose exercise does not reduce to the determination of this act, this movement, to the individuation of a knowledge whose being would be doomed to be carried off by time. In its original being our body escapes time, just like absolute subjectivity; it has no other relation to time than to constitute it.

One cannot therefore think to rest the unity of our original being, the unity of the body or of the ego, on memory. It is not by memory — by the mediation of time — that the ensemble of acts and movements accomplished by the fundamental powers of my own being is gathered into a unity. This unity of my being through time constituted by memory requires a foundation. That foundation is habit, or the very being of my body, which makes the act of remembering possible and is nothing but the being of ontological knowledge understood as the real being of pure possibility. To explain the unity of the ego or the body by memory is to commit a paralogism, to explain the original unity by a faculty that on the contrary finds its foundation in such unity. In other terms, the unity of our being is not constituted, not wrested from time by the power of our protentions and retentions; it is on the contrary immanent to the being of our ontological knowledge, coincides with it, and is precisely what makes that being the being of an ontological knowledge. This is a direct verdict on the constitution of inner time-consciousness: the temporal synthesis presupposes an atemporal unity of the ego and cannot found it.

Biran said very similar things. Having explicitly reproached Locke with the paralogism Henry has denounced concerning the foundation of the ego's unity, he referred to a more original unity whose ontological status is, from the whole context of Biranism, that of absolute subjectivity: the subject of effort immediately recognizes its identity, its continued duration; it feels it is the same as before sleep, without any accidental impression motivating distinct memories or any determinate relation between a present and a past time. From this simple experience of intimate sense it follows that personal identity has its own sense, and that this identity or the durability of our personal existence being the base of memory, Locke falls into a vicious circle when he says our identity is on the contrary founded on the memory or reminiscence of our varied or successive manners of being.

A terminological remark is necessary to penetrate Biran's thought on this capital point. Biran affirms that the original unity of the being of the ego — of the original being of the subjective body — does not rest on memory, on reminiscence in his own terms, but constitutes the foundation of these psychological faculties. Yet when it comes to naming this original unity, he characterizes it as reminiscence: personal reminiscence does not differ from the feeling of the same continued existence. The ambiguity is only verbal. Biran perfectly distinguished memory properly speaking, as explicit thought of the past, from the infra-temporal unity of the ego, which is the foundation of psychological memory. As his text shows: there could not be even the simplest perception (which always supposes a successive plurality of impressions or acts) if there were not continuity of the self or personal reminiscence conserved in the succession of elementary terms or modes; but this reminiscence conserved in the sensation of a same continuous movement must be well distinguished from memory properly speaking. Thus it is because the body is memory — a memory, admittedly, where the idea of past does not yet appear — that it can also be a memory remembering the past by making it the theme of its thought. The original memory of our body is habit; our body is the ensemble of all our habits. Registering the two senses of "reminiscence" is what saves Biran from the apparent contradiction: infra-temporal self-identity (personal reminiscence) grounds explicit recollection (modal reminiscence).

These habits are no more unconscious than our memories. As soon as the concept of the unconscious appears, it is the sign that we are approaching an original region, for the unconscious is often only a name given to absolute subjectivity by philosophies incapable of grasping the essence of the foundation otherwise than by projecting it into the night of a backworld — of which Henry has given the psychoanalysis. Habits are not ready-made mechanisms waiting somewhere in a region = x for a movement of our will or desire to give them occasion to trigger and unwind the melodic play of their processes. These little psychological beings, suitably structured, could remain long in the dust of the out-of-the-way place they occupy, for the good reason that the idea of using them and setting them in motion never comes to mind. Perhaps this idea or desire is in turn unconscious? Probably so — for it is the property of mythologies to enlarge ceaselessly, each myth in its indigence demanding another myth to account for it, until the whole of our psychological life leaves the plane where we evidently see it produced, to be transported into another region where it lives a new life that coarse and simplistic imaginations lend it, a life among fantastic beings and absurd concepts. The polemic against the mechanism of habit is continuous with Chapter I's psychoanalysis of the transcendent term = x: the unconscious mechanism is one more idol, generating an infinite regress of further idols.

We have a chance of grasping the original being of habit only at the term of an ontological inquiry letting us surpass the concept of individuated empirical being, like that of its milieu and transcendental horizon, to rise to the concept of the original being of subjectivity. The reality of the being at issue then no longer resides in its individuation, nor in the milieu of all possible individuation, but in the reality of its very possibility — because such a being is that of ontological knowledge. By understanding the body as absolute subjectivity we can also understand the ontological phenomenon of habit, in which the being of the body encloses in its ontological present all possible knowledges of the world, without these ever rising outside the sphere of absolute immanence to be lost in the scattering and lacunary diversity of transcendent terms or in the night of the unconscious. The body bears in it the depth of its past. That depth, however, is equally the absence of all depth, for it is an absolute transparency. The closing paradox is the section's summary: the body's past is total (nothing is lost) yet has no depth (nothing is hidden), because immanence has no interior recesses.

### § 3. The Individuality of Human Reality as Sensible Individuality

The subjective unity of the body — unity of our senses and movements, unity of their knowing — lets us in turn understand the individuality of human reality insofar as it is a sensible individuality. Here Henry rejects every empirical conception of individuality, a conception dominating philosophical thought from Antiquity to our day. It was precisely by recourse to the body and to the corporeal condition of human reality that one thought to find the means of reducing the individuality of the ego's being to the empirical conception of spatiotemporal individuation. Man having a body, and that body conceived as an empirical object individuated in time, the existence of the body naturally became the principle for applying to human reality the general conditions of spatiotemporal individuation. More: the body was not only the point of application of the general form of individuality; not only its means and foundation but genuinely its origin and cause. In other terms, it was because man had a body that he could be understood as an individual. Abstract the body, and there remained to man only the being of a pure mind, a being that was not properly a being since no longer individuated — a kind of impersonal *nous*, a homogeneous, undifferentiated substance. Man became genuinely a double man: on one hand a pure, universal consciousness, on the other an empirical individuality — the latter conferring the property of being a man, an individual. It was thanks to his body that man came into the world; empirical individuation was genuinely the principle of his birth.

This strange philosophical alchemy made and still makes the theme of half-theoretical, half-moral dissertations, for the dualism of mind and empirical characters immediately takes on an axiological signification. It becomes the frame of an effort proposed to man to rise above these determinations and rejoin his true self — no longer a self but a Self, the Spirit, or whatever one likes. The empirical element is the element to be vanquished: the pathological content duty surmounts, something temporal, doomed to corruption, a mortal and contingent husk. It is strange to found the individuality of the human being on an element with a pejorative signification, for all of man's activity could then consist only in a struggle against this element — a struggle that, if it succeeded, would annihilate the principle of our individuality, so that man's moral effort would be turned toward the destruction of his own being. But the consequences of these fanciful constructions do not matter here. Henry wants to understand how individuality, whose theory he gave elsewhere, is a sensible individuality. The reductio is worth marking: the tradition makes the body both the principle of individuation and the thing to be overcome, so its ethics aims at self-destruction.

The ontological theory of the body forbids seeing in it a principle of empirical individuation. If individuality is not met on the plane of absolute subjectivity, if it is not a *transcendental* individuality, then relating such subjectivity to the body cannot bring it the principle of individuation it needs, since the body is itself, in its essence, absolute subjectivity and carries in it no principle of empirical individuation. The problem of individuality is posed on the plane of absolute subjectivity; it is posed for the being of the body as for that of the ego — in truth it is one and the same problem, which considering the body only lets us push further. The individuality of absolute subjectivity found its foundation in the theory that the being of that subjectivity is the very being of the ego. When the ego's life is the concrete life of the body, this individuality becomes a sensible individuality. Sensible individuality is not an empirical individuality, because it is not an individuality of *sensation* but an individuality of *sensing*. Sensation is individuated in time, but the power to sense and accomplish movements escapes empirical individuation in principle as it escapes time; it is the absolute being of habit, and in this it is genuinely an Individual. The distinction between sensation and sensing does the whole work here, and it is the same distinction the chapter opened with, now applied to individuation.

In Biranism the individuality of the being of the subjective body is not the object of a particular thesis; it is rather the foundation of the whole theory. To speak of the individuality of effort is a kind of tautology, for there are two words for a single reality, the self. But one must see the ontological foundation of this individuality, which is implied in the ontological theory of the body on the same footing as its unity. Biran, summarizing: thus personality begins with the first complete action of a hyperorganic force, which is for itself or as self only insofar as it knows itself, and begins to know itself only insofar as it begins to act freely. So it is insofar as the original being of movement is subjectivity that it is also and thereby the being of the ego — individuality, or ipseity, being nothing but the milieu where a *sui generis* revelation is accomplished in immanence, an ontological milieu of existence that is precisely subjectivity itself. Because the being of movement immanent to all our powers of sensing is a subjective being, the life of our power of sensing is an individual life, the very life of individuality.

It is Lagneau who advanced furthest in understanding the individuality that makes the essence of our power of sensing. After the criticisms Henry directed at his theory of movement and action and the reproach of intellectualism against his conception of the body, this appeal to the author of the *Célèbres leçons* may seem surprising. No less surprising is the movement of thought at the end of the *Leçon sur la perception*, when the problem of the individual becomes the theme of a few rapid but decisive reflections. It is the moment of genius, when, while deepening the philosophical tradition that nourished him, the philosopher escapes it and surpasses it toward the future. Henry's return to Lagneau is not inconsistency: the same limits that defeated Lagneau on movement press him, at the end, past those limits on individuality.

Having given an intellectualist theory of perception, having founded the nature of the sensible world on the ensemble of significations our judgments confer on it, Lagneau comes to interrogate the truth of this constituted world and finds truth precisely absent from it. Truth is the work of judgment, but the judgment by which we determine our representations has only a practical and utilitarian signification; it defines only a normal way of perceiving that lets us find our way in our habitual world. Its spring is not properly an idea but a general image, a schema, a means of orienting ourselves in our surroundings. There is no truth of sensible knowledge, which is not properly a knowledge — for a knowledge always implies an objective truth and founds the agreement of all minds. Each individual, on the contrary, senses in his own way. To perceive is to return to the standpoint of the sensing individual and hold to it. Perception therefore cannot be called true or false; it is what it is, and what we sense is something irreducible and proper to each of us.

The being of sensing, essentially individual, seems devalued relative to intellectual knowledge, source and domain of truth. Does Lagneau not follow the classical line rigorously insofar as he reserves truth for intellectual knowledge and the idea, wresting sensible life from error only to confine it in the domain of illusion — every perception being in sum an illusion, a subjective way of seeing things and ideas? At least the originality of sensible life, its irreducibility to theoretical life, is loudly affirmed. Thus the being of our sensible life is no longer constituted by judgment; there is in us a primitive life, an original life that is not intellectual and that will further appear as the foundation of theoretical knowledge and abstract truth. What announces itself here, in a singular foreshortening, are already the Husserlian theses of *Erfahrung und Urteil*: theoretical life is founded. There is no purely abstract truth, says Lagneau, adding: if we conceive that there is a truth, we conceive it as the truth of what we sense. Thus intellectual truth, the truth of judgment, develops on a ground already given to it, which is our sensible life itself, the concrete life of naive perception. Henry's reference to *Erfahrung und Urteil* is precise: Lagneau anticipates the founding of predicative judgment on antepredicative experience, the very structure Husserl's genetic phenomenology will make central.

But this life, whose fundamental role is thus affirmed, is an individual life, the very life of the individual. Hence the individual can no longer be confused with the empirical; it is not a contingent, synthetic adjunction to a pure consciousness — it defines the *primitive mode of the very life* of that consciousness. It is on this life that predication in turn will be founded, and thus even our judgments will be nothing but the expression of what is individual in us. Original life is an individual life, but this life is precisely sensible life, the life of the body. Sensible individuality therefore refers to what is most primitive in us — not to an empirical element but to the concrete and original life of absolute subjectivity, to its deepest determination, which will be the foundation of all others.

These words of Lagneau receive their decisive sense: one could not conceive a manner of sensing that should be considered the true one for us in given circumstances. That would suppose either that our sensible nature does not change, or that its development is subject to a rigorous law — that this nature results completely in us from its relation with the external world, of which it would be only an effect, a resultant. But then there would be in us no spontaneity, no sensible nature. Now, it is the same thing to say that we are individuals and to say that in these individuals there is a sensible nature in which something does not result from the action of the milieu. If everything in sensible nature were subject to necessity, if there were for us a manner of sensing that would be the true one, if at each instant our manner of sensing resulted from the external world, we would not sense. The devaluation of sensibility that still seems to appear here, insofar as sensibility is deprived of truth, signifies in reality a devaluation of intellectual truth, which is not adequate to the original region where our concrete existence moves and is only a derived and secondary truth. What is affirmed as essential, as genuinely a foundation, is something individual and sensible: the life of the body. It is indeed the body that is the foundation of our individuality — not the empirical body but the originally subjective being of the transcendental body. The argument Henry extracts from Lagneau is that individuality and non-derivability from the milieu are the same thing: if sensing were wholly determined by the world, there would be no sensing at all, only the world's effect passing through.

Moreover, Lagneau's explicitly designating sensible nature as a *spontaneity* shows how his analysis breaks abruptly with the Kantian presuppositions that first framed his inquiry. The spontaneity of our sensible life indicates its properly subjective nature, for only absolute subjectivity is a spontaneity. What is immanent to our sensible life and constitutes its being is the subjective being of movement, which defines at once the power of our body and the proper quality of our individuality. To be an individual is to have with the world an absolutely original relation — and this not by an ethical decision, at the term of a deliberately undertaken effort, but everywhere and always, in romantic exaltation as in daily banality. The originality of the mode in which I relate to the universe is an ontological necessity, inherent in the ontological structure of habit. It is because my manner of sensing the world is the very experience I have of my subjectivity that it is given to me alone, in the transcendental inner experience of the originally subjective being of my body.

I am the unique — not because I decided to be, not because in my aestheticism I taste only the exceptional in rare sensations and, like Keats, the scent of faded violets, but quite simply because I sense. "One" does not sense. Sensibility is a proper possibility of the being of the ego, its most eminent possibility, for it is nothing but ontological possibility itself. To sense is to undergo, in the individuality of one's unique life, the universal life of the universe; it is to be already "the most irreplaceable of beings." The chapter closes by wresting individuality from ethics and aesthetics alike: uniqueness is not achieved or cultivated but is the ontological structure of sensing as such, since no two subjective lives are the same and the impersonal "one" cannot sense at all.

## Chapter IV. The Double Use of Signs and the Problem of the Constitution of One's Own Body

The problem of the constitution of the body falls outside the field of Henry's inquiry, since it is not the theme of a reflection concentrating on the being of absolute subjectivity and the original ego. But attending to the being of the constituted body imposes itself insofar as a question can no longer be deferred. If the being of the body is originally subjective, if the life of our body is only a modality of the life of absolute subjectivity, how is it that this body of ours has never been considered — by the philosophical systems, by psychology, by scientific reflection or by lay thought — otherwise than as an element of transcendent being, whatever characters were assigned to distinguish it within that region from the other beings peopling it, among which it appears with determinations that do not prevent but rather seem imperiously to require a system of relations of inherence and reciprocal action? Only as a constituted reality is the body capable of sustaining with the other beings of nature the relations the sciences make us conceive, relations whose validity is universally recognized. The chapter is thus a debt paid: having exiled the objective body from the original sphere, Henry must explain why everyone has always found it there.

Our body certainly manifests itself in the truth of transcendent being; any consciousness can discover it there. It appears as a spatial configuration that is also the milieu where numerous objective displacements and movements occur, by which this body enters into contact with exterior bodies, collides with them, attracts or repels them. How could what we see be charged with illusion? How could such an illusion be so universally shared? What is astonishing is not the common opinion making us consider our body an object — an opinion Henry will show to be founded — but rather the omission implied in it, concerning the original being of our body. Everything happens, on such a conception, as if our body were nothing but this object we see, and as if the original being of the body were a chimera whose trace one would vainly seek in our real experience.

There is as it were an absorption of the originally subjective being of the body into the body manifesting itself among things; the first becomes interior to the second; the whole being of our body reduces to its constituted being, and outside this transcendent phenomenon there is nothing but the consciousness thinking it, the mind or soul surveying it. What may be subjective in our body, the element Henry characterized as immanent, is indeed such — but it is an element immanent to a *transcendent* body belonging to nature. If I consider the immanent element of my body as the heart and kernel of that body, this element appears precisely as the heart and kernel of the body-object I see or can touch. What we call immanence has thus become the very essence of the transcendent. Diagnosing this displacement is the chapter's first task, and the formula is exact: the error is not denying immanence but relocating it inside a transcendent thing as its interior.

Before clarifying the fundamental ontological ambiguity presiding over such a transformation, Henry must show how it is at the origin of the perception or knowledge we have of our body — the knowledge common sense expresses in ordinary language. The philosophical presuppositions of that language will then be the theme of a radical ontological elucidation.

This language says: the eye sees the landscape, the hand moves toward the table and touches it, the ear hears the melody. Eye, hand, ear are elements of the transcendent body; they manifest themselves to consciousness in the truth of being, have place there, spatial configuration, perceived or scientifically determined relations with all the objects of nature. And it is precisely such transcendent elements that are said to bear in them the kernel of the body, the ensemble of powers by which the body sees, moves, touches, hears. These powers, however, Henry characterized as belonging to a sphere of radical immanence, as constituting the being of a subjective body. What then does this degradation mean, by which immanence ceases to define the sphere of our absolute existence in order to exteriorize itself in a being on which it confers a kind of intimacy and depth?

There is no question of revisiting the results of the ontological analysis of the original being of our body, results that are absolutely certain and part of the absolute knowledge by which phenomenological ontology is built. How, besides, could one maintain the absurdity that what we see and touch would also be what sees and touches? This body we see and also call ours presupposes, as Biran showed, *another body that sees and touches* — that sees and touches all things and, among them, this body that is seen and touched. That other body is the original body, whose being was determined as belonging to absolute subjectivity, outside which it could not arise without immediately losing everything that makes it what it is. This ontological power cannot pass into transcendent being; it cannot be identified with or incorporated into an element of nature. That identification is a naive representation and in truth an illusion. Biran's general theory of this illusion is his analysis of what he calls the *double use of signs*. The one-line refutation is worth marking: the seen body cannot be the seeing body, since seeing it already requires the seeing body.

Consider the experience of vision: it is a transcendental inner experience. This experience transcends toward a world, but it is accomplished wholly in a sphere of radical immanence. If we now express this experience in language, we employ the word "see," which is, in Biran's terms, its *sign*. How this sign relates to the inner experience of vision, how in general language is founded on the life of absolute subjectivity it expresses, cannot be elucidated here. Henry says simply that this founding is accomplished in two very different ways, giving rise on one hand to natural language, which immediately expresses the life of subjectivity, and on the other to the language of reflection, which rests on a mediate operation. In the latter case — examined here because it can give rise to grave confusions — the expression "I see" is founded not on my transcendental inner experience of vision but on a reflection directed on that experience. The experience then ceases to be an immanent content and becomes the object of a new experience, my reflection. The term of this reflection, my primitive experience of vision, has now become a transcendent reality.

From the moment vision offers itself as the term of an intentionality, it is ready to be circumscribed within an element of transcendent being — in the body-object I see, belonging to nature. The ontological paralogism implied in admitting that vision could be designated, following this slide into transcendent being, as the property of an element of nature, is this: the vision that in reflection has become the simple transcendent correlate of the life of absolute subjectivity *is no longer the vision that sees* but a simple representation of it; it is no longer the ontological knowledge disclosing the transcendence of a visual world but the mere exterior manifestation of that knowledge. The representation of vision nonetheless presupposes real vision as its foundation. Reflection never creates its object, only the manner in which it gives it to itself. It is because I see that I can reflect on vision; it is because vision is originally mine, in a sphere of absolute immanence, that I can represent it to myself. So if the word "sees" refers, in reflexive language, to the vision I reflect on — an intentional correlate — its transcendent character cannot mislead us. The theme of my reflection is indeed a transcendent manifestation, but the content of that manifestation, the substance of what it represents, is borrowed from the subjective life of our absolute body. The founding of reflexive language reduces in the end to that of natural language. Granting that the words "I see" designate the representation of my vision rather than my vision itself, it is on the latter, on its radically immanent experience and it alone, that their signification ultimately rests. The principle stated here — reflection never creates its object, only the manner of giving it — is the chapter's chief tool and will be used repeatedly.

The whole ontological ambiguity of the phenomenon Biran calls the double use of signs lies in the fact that a relation is also established between the words "I see" and a physiological organ, so that the sign "see" has a double use and designates the eye, or a property of it, as much as the transcendental inner experience of vision. One says: it is the eye that sees. The eye is a being of nature, first an extended being. Vision will therefore be a natural phenomenon, and moreover one endowed with spatial extension — the greatest absurdity imaginable. Should one try to pass over this ontological nonsense, one would meet it again in another form: if vision is a phenomenon localized in space, one cannot see how it can leave the place where it is and go off over there, to the hillside, to that house I see, higher and farther, to the forest's edge, higher still into the sky, and to the stars.

Such a vision, moreover, would see nothing at all, not even what is at the spot where it is alleged to be. It is a natural phenomenon, an element of transcendent being. And transcendent being can neither leave itself nor know itself. It could leave itself only if it could first know itself; it could be present to things only if it were first present to self. To be present to things within one's original presence to self is precisely the phenomenon of ontological knowledge, the very being of absolute subjectivity. It is as a transcendental inner experience, a modality of absolute life, that vision is possible at all. It is to this alone possible vision that the word "see" refers; it cannot be applied to anything else except by favor of a confusion and a total ontological obscurity. The argument is the same one deployed against the instrumental body in Chapter II, now applied to the sense organ: a thing in the world cannot reach beyond itself, because reaching beyond presupposes self-presence.

Each of our senses, says Biran, defines itself by its exercise. The original being of our body is, as he also says, a willing whose proper idea is wholly in reflection — meaning, in the *Mémoire*'s terminology, that it is a modality of absolute life whose being is one with the knowledge we have of it. Henry notes that the term "willing," which Biran uses too often, can no longer mislead us: it is not a willing preceding action, a mere idea of action, a subjective impulse or desire, but the being of movement in its very accomplishment. It is within this sensible and motor life knowing itself originally, and not in the representation of our organs or their properties, that the signs by which we express its various modalities find content and sense.

Because such a life manifests itself in diverse modalities, one risks again confusing that diversity with the diversity of our physiological organization. It seems that if we can distinguish among our powers of sensing, it is by relying on the material differentiation and separation of our sense organs. The contrary is true: if nature has prepared a kind of decomposition of our exterior faculty of sensing, this division of our senses insofar as it is a division of sense organs rests in reality on a transcendental view of our powers of sensing, on the division existing between vision, hearing, touch, which is originally given us in the transcendental inner experience we have of the subjective being of our body. It is because we know, with an original and primitive knowledge, what vision, hearing, or touch is that we can subsequently represent the eye, the hand, or the ear as organs endowed with proper capacities irreducible to one another, and so arrive at the idea of an exterior decomposition of our faculty of sensing. This inverts the received explanatory order and is one of the chapter's strongest results: anatomy does not explain the plurality of senses; the transcendentally given plurality of powers is what lets anatomy be read as a plurality of organs.

Biran marks this in an important text: a distinction of seats attributed to the exercise of each faculty must necessarily refer to *another division of the faculties*, pre-established either logically or reflexively, independently of all physiological observation or hypothesis. The logical division of our powers of sensing, and consequently of the sensations toward which they transcend, is a nominal division, a division into classes offering a nomenclature of the possibilities of our sensible life. This logical division, expressed by reflexive language, is evidently founded on the "reflected division," that is, on what Henry calls a transcendental division. If one makes a supposed and demonstrated diversity of organic seats correspond to this logical division, that diversity can add nothing to the reality of the ideological distinctions — the transcendental distinctions offering us this diversity and its content in the original state, since it is in transcendental experience that we draw the idea of that content and of its diversity. If the physiological division corresponds to the one metaphysical analysis (that is, transcendental phenomenology) offers, that is precisely because it is traced on the division the latter originally draws; and so the physiological division is fit only to realize or represent in an organic seat ideas of modes or operations that cannot exist outside the thinking subject nor be conceived outside its most intimate reflection.

Henry can now say what the double use consists in, in a text of Biran's concerning sensibility: the physiologist, having first employed the term *sensibility* in its proper acceptation as the sign of a faculty or intimate property of his individual being, then transports, perhaps without noticing, the expression of an order of facts that can exist or be conceived only from the interior standpoint of a unique sentient subject, to a parallel but wholly different order of composite phenomena represented or imagined outside in the play of organic instruments — thus enveloping under the same signs two sorts of different conceptions, and then judging the real identity of the ideas or facts by the conventional identity of the logical forms applied to them. Hence an often illusory rapprochement between physiologist and metaphysician, who, employing the same terms, may believe they occupy themselves with the same things or embrace the same system of ideas. The mechanism is precisely stated and generalizes far beyond Biran's example: a shared vocabulary is mistaken for a shared subject matter, and the identity of terms is taken as evidence of identity of things.

The relation of the original being of the body to the system of organs physiology studies can only be, on Biran's view, a symbolic relation, at whose term the physiological division appears as a symbol or sign of the transcendental division. Considering movement, physiology thinks to account for it by imagining in the brain a center of action from which movement will deploy. But is that anything other than a symbol? Can the individual self identify with any organic center? Is the action we objectively refer to such a center the same as the one we attribute to ourselves in the intimate consciousness of an effort? Are these not two ideas, two facts of a wholly different order? How could the mind pass from one to the other?

The relation between these two "facts" — the physiological body and the original being of our body — being analogous to the relation of sign to thing signified, its philosophical signification is double. On one hand the sign aids our understanding of the thing signified: all metaphysical analysis, leaning confidently on a physiological division of the organs, their functions and play, will receive from it that clarity, that apparent ease that images communicate to reflexive notions by uniting with them, as symbols fit to explain what is obscure in itself. On the other hand this aid is illusory: it makes us believe one can deduce from the combination of certain organic movements psychological facts that can be established only by intimate sense, so that these alleged explanations teach nothing about the subject and serve only to obscure it, substituting confused images for the simple and perfectly clear ideas of reflection.

This last text, reaffirming the absolute character of the evidence inherent in transcendental immanence, calls into question many of Biran's analyses in which physiological research appeared as a necessary and useful complement to properly psychological research. In showing that this collaboration issues rather in confusion and obscuring of the sphere of absolute subjectivity, in underlining how dangerous it is to bind transcendental inner experiences to signs that are elements of transcendent being having no relation to such experiences — so that one risks taking the sign for the thing signified and comes to hold the eye for the center of vision — Biran radically separates, after having seemed to bring them together, the subjective determinations composing the original being of our body and the physiological divisions forming the structure of our exterior and extended body. Henry is careful to note that Biran's own text is used here against Biran's other texts; the separation is what the theory requires, not everything Biran wrote.

A grave difficulty now arises. If there is no relation between the subjective life in which we experience the original powers of our body and the physiological structure of our body-object, how is it that a relation is nonetheless instituted between what Biran would call two absolutely heterogeneous sorts of conception? One will say that establishing such a relation belongs to an illusion the theory of the double use has just denounced. But philosophy's task is not to denounce illusions but rather to *justify* them, by bringing to light the foundation that makes them possible, the ontological structure from which they develop. This is the chapter's methodological principle and the reason it does not stop at the critique: an illusion this universal must have a structural ground.

What Henry seeks is a foundation letting us understand three things. First, why we do not have a single body but in a sense two — why the being of our body splits into an originally subjective being and a transcendent being manifesting itself in the truth of the world. Second, why these two bodies nonetheless form only one — that is, why and how the double use of signs is possible, why and how the same sign can be applied so generally and universally to two absolutely heterogeneous elements, or rather to two phenomena differing in their very phenomenality. Third, why these two phenomena receive the character of being precisely *mine* — why and how such a character, an essential determination of absolute subjectivity, seems here to extend its reign into transcendent being, since a constituted reality appears to me as mine, and it is just this belonging to the ego that lets me designate it as "my body" and so distinguish it, within that transcendent region, from the other beings of nature.

Some of these difficulties exceed the framework of the problem, bearing on the most general structures of being and calling into question the very foundations of phenomenological ontology. Such is the case with the question of the *two bodies*. It appears immediately that the duality incomprehensibly splitting the unity of the being of my body, giving that being to me twice over, finds its foundation in the ontological structure of truth — the structure by which something manifests itself in the truth of transcendent being only under the condition of a more original revelation in a milieu of absolute immanence. *Ontological dualism is the foundation of the double use of signs.* It is because there exist, as Biran says, two sources of evidence that our body gives itself in such a way that each of its original powers — immediately known in the subjective experience of the movement constituting its essence — also manifests itself in the form of an organ or some physiological or spatial determination. The difference between the original being of this power and the organ that seems its instrument is in no way situated on an ontic plane; it is not a difference between something and something else but an ontological difference, a difference *not in individuality but in manner of being*, relative to the region within which being manifests itself and exists. The formula deserves marking, since it is what prevents the two bodies from being two things.

Biran strongly marked the properly ontological character of the distinction between power and organ: it does not rest on individual properties determining, in their reciprocal opposition, two beings otherwise submitted to the same ontological conditions that would be conditions of possibility of being in general; it concerns on the contrary those conditions themselves, referring to the regions where the two beings of power and organ find their condition of possibility along with their internal structure. The ontological character constituting the being of the organ is transcendence, the fact that our senses give themselves by *setting themselves in a kind of relief outside us*. Belonging to a sphere of absolute immanence is by contrast the essential ontological determination of our senses insofar as they define our proper and immediate powers, insofar as they compose the original being of our body. We may, with Biran, call each of these subjective powers a "reflected concept," whose property is, according to the *Essai*, to have no direct sign of manifestation. But what cannot manifest itself directly by any sign is not the night of the unconscious or the opacity of brute matter; what is deprived of all transcendence is not the thing closed on itself, prisoner of its own thickness, ignorant of itself and the universe — it is on the contrary that by which all transcendence becomes possible, a region of absolute immanence where the original revelation of the truth of self and world is accomplished. The guard here is one Henry has posted before and it is essential: absence of manifestation-at-a-distance is not obscurity but the condition of all light.

Henry has shown elsewhere how these two truths make only one, how ontological dualism does not institute a rending within being in the form of a separation of self and things, of subjectivity and universe, but is on the contrary what makes possible the presence of being to an original presence to self, what gives rise to the true proximity of things within an absolute proximity. The idea of duality has a wholly special value when it characterizes the ultimate structures of being: it no longer signifies, as we usually understand it, a duality of two terms within one ontological region, but rather the *absence* of all duality, for it is what makes experience possible, and experience is always a unity. The unity of experience, the unity of life and transcendent being, finds its foundation in the existence of an absolute subjectivity transcending toward a world because it is in itself the milieu where the self-revelation of that act of transcendence is originally accomplished. What is meant by ontological dualism is only the necessity of the existence of this sphere of absolute subjectivity, without which our experience of the world would not be possible. Ontological dualism cannot therefore be confused with duality properly speaking, with an ontic duality presiding over the oppositions we establish within the world. To speak of ontological dualism is precisely to exclude the idea of such a duality. The duality manifesting itself in the difficulty of the two bodies evidently refers to ontological dualism; it does not place us before a genuine duality splitting our own being into two opposed parts. It satisfies rather the general conditions constituting the ontological possibility of the unity of experience. The originally subjective being of our body and the transcendent body, power and organ, are not, in Biran's terms, "two facts" but "two orders of facts."

This answers the first question. There remain questions two and three, concerning the unity of the two bodies. At this point we understand the duality of the revelation and the manifestation, but we do not see why what originally reveals itself and the transcendent being that otherwise manifests itself appear to us as one and the same thing, the very being of our body. The general unity of experience — the unity in presence of the being of the world and of the ego — cannot found the unity of the two bodies. What presents itself in the unity of experience to the life of absolute subjectivity is precisely what that life is not by itself: the other, the non-ego, the general signification of transcendent being.

The link between questions two and three is now visible, and the difficulty is the same, though it appears in full amplitude in the third, where it takes on an apparently insoluble character. If the ontological difference is the difference of self and non-self, one cannot see how an element of transcendent being — the being of our objective body — can receive the signification of being *ours*, of belonging to the ego, whose being is identified on the contrary with absolute subjectivity. It is the same problem to understand why transcendent body and subjective body are one and the same body and to understand why this transcendent body can be designated by me as a body that is mine. For if the transcendent body is the same as the subjective body, then it must be mine, must be the absolute ego itself, since it is precisely this belonging to the ego that makes the whole being of the original and subjective body. But how could the absolute ego, a life in the absolute immanence of subjectivity, be otherwise a transcendent being? How could it tear itself from immanence to appear somewhere in the world?

Henry explains why this difficulty is radical for him. We do not have the leisure to say that our body (or our self) is in itself one and the same body known to us in two different ways, from outside and from inside. Similarly, it is sometimes claimed that our own being is constituted by an ensemble of conducts or behaviors, but that two means of acceding to the being of these behaviors are given us, a knowledge from within and a knowledge from without. On such a view there is only one behavior we reach by two different routes. The being of the behavior, or of the body, is then *beyond* the two phenomena by which it offers itself to us, and it is in this beyond that the unity of the two phenomena resides, the foundation on which we lean when we say we possess two modes of manifestation of one and the same body.

Phenomenology cannot advance so illusory a solution, because it reduces being to its appearing, because for it there is nothing beyond the phenomena, nothing behind the various manifestations given to us, which all reduce to two essential types whose ontological structure Henry has studied. Phenomenology is not a theory of appearances leaving behind it the real being of things. That real being, it shows us, is wholly in the manner in which it gives itself to us, in the appearances; it shows that *being is its own revelation*, so that where two phenomena are opposed we must also say we face two beings — and the task of surmounting their duality cannot be left to a mysterious term situated behind them, to an alleged absolute whose magical power is only the projection into the metaphysical heaven of the requirements an unity must satisfy when one cannot account for it on the concrete and real plane of our experience. This magical term can take many forms, more easily detected when it is the absolute of Spinoza or Schelling, or Malebranche's God, than when it takes a humbler, apparently "scientific" aspect and becomes, for instance, the concept of "behavior." The target is contemporary and unmistakable: the behavioral monism that dissolves the mind–body problem by positing behavior as a single reality accessible from two sides is, structurally, Malebranche's God in scientific dress.

To say that our behavior is given to us in two different ways is to give the statement of the problem for a solution; the notion of behavior founding the unity of the two phenomena is in reality only a beyond of those phenomena, an empty term reducing to the role one claims to make it play and for which it was invented outright. The subjective body is not a phenomenon leaving behind it the real being of the body — a being then free to manifest itself by other phenomena, by an objective body; *it is the real being of the body itself*, its absolute being, the whole being of that body, a being that is an absolute transparency in which no element escapes the revelation of original truth. My body is not like a mountain I see now from one side, now from another; nor an object I would always see from the same side; nor something I would reach now from outside and now from inside. *I never see my body from outside because I am never outside my body* — this is what must be affirmed if the theory that the being of my body belongs to absolute immanence is to have any sense. That last sentence is the chapter's most compressed statement of its position and the reason the "two aspects of one thing" solution is unavailable to Henry.

It seems then that the principal ontological theses framing the analysis distance us from any solution. The double use of signs seemed to find its foundation in ontological dualism; but does the latter not now appear as what forbids us to understand the unity of this body, whose very *being* — not merely the appearances under which it gives itself — we have divided, since we affirmed on one hand the identity of the original revelation of subjective movement with the being of our body, and on the other the identity of the transcendent manifestation of that body with a being that must be the body-object's? We do face two beings; and ontological dualism, if it does not issue in a duality analogous to that between two elements of the world, nonetheless posits a real, indeed radical duality, since the two beings do not even stand out against a common structure but differ in their very essence, in their ontological origin referring to two absolutely heterogeneous regions.

At the moment we seem to meet insurmountable difficulties, Henry brings to light the positive elements ontological dualism and the theory of the subjective body have established, bearing precisely on the problems of the unity of the body and its belonging to the ego. We understood that only within an ontology of subjectivity did the body receive an original status on which its unity and the identity of its being with the ego's could rest. It is not at the moment of completing the theory of that unity and identity that we can think of calling into question the ontological presuppositions that alone can found such a theory. The theory will be completed when we have brought to light the conditions letting the unity of the original being of the body and its identity with the ego extend their reign to the being of the transcendent body, whose identity with the subjective body would thereby receive a solid foundation.

We have in hand all the elements of a solution: duality of two regions of being; unity and belonging to the ego of the being of the body — a unity and belonging instituted, however, within absolute subjectivity and originally concerning only the subjective being of the body. If the problem is how these ontological determinations can be extended to the being of the transcendent body, we already understand that such an extension will operate only by taking support on the original being of the body: *the unity and belonging to the ego of the transcendent body are constituted on the foundation of the original being of the subjective body*, on the foundation of its unity and its belonging to the ego — that is, of ontological determinations originally the exclusive privilege of a determinate ontological region, a region of absolute immanence. This is what the long analyses that might have seemed sterile have taught; they aimed at justifying the ontological presuppositions within which the analysis of the body operates, by showing that these presuppositions do not lead the theory of the constitution of one's own body to inextricable difficulties but rather furnish the elements it needs, without which the problem of the unity of our body could not even be posed. The strategy is now explicit and is worth holding onto: the objective body's unity and mineness are not discovered in it but *lent* to it by the subjective body.

It remains to describe this constitution. Until now the analysis bore mainly on elucidating the subjective being of movement and was content to reject from absolute immanence everything not belonging to it. But even while holding within absolute subjectivity in an attitude of reduction, we were led to consider the term toward which movement immediately transcends: the term resisting effort. This manifested itself as a resisting continuum escaping the phenomenological reduction, to be considered the foundation of all transcendent being, offering the solution of the "great problem of existences." Considering this resisting continuum in itself, we see that its ontological homogeneity nonetheless admits an essential differentiation, since it must let us distinguish, within the region of the transcendent body in general, a body that is ours among foreign bodies. Failing such differentiation we would be led, as Biran notes, to a position analogous to that of the Stoics, who saw in the soul the principle of the universe and made it the "soul of the world." The ego's immediate power extends in reality only over a particular body that is its own, and only mediately does it act on the universe — which is to say that within the world our transcendent body is distinguished from and opposed to other bodies by distinctive properties needing to be accounted for. It is understood that this is not a represented difference, or a difference between the representation of our own body and that of exterior bodies, but a difference between these bodies *as they are originally lived by us*, as they give themselves to the subjective movement experiencing them.

This differentiation rests in Biranism on the fact that movement meets in one case an *absolute* resistance — the phenomenological foundation of the being of the foreign body — while this resistance *yields* to effort in the case of the transcendent being of one's own body. Biran calls "interior extension" the transcendent milieu thus yielding to the effort of our movement. In this *sui generis* manner of giving itself resides, for a phenomenological ontology, the whole being of our transcendent body, whose immediate solidarity with the original being of our body is only the expression of the fundamental relation of transcendence of movement. Thus to the original being of our body is bound a kind of *organic body*, from which the soul, in a phrase of Leibniz's that Biran cites, is never separated. The criterion is elegant and does real work: what distinguishes my body from all others within the transcendent region is not appearance but the *modality of resistance* — yielding rather than absolute.

This organic body, the point of application of effort and bound to it, is an organic space primitively "vague and unlimited," whose ontological homogeneity is founded entirely on its original mode of manifestation. Because it is not represented, because subjective movement and it alone undergoes and experiences it, this organic or interior space has nothing in common with exterior space. Nor is it an empty continuum without depth, a kind of monotonous and lifeless space like the one pure intuition of Cartesian extension or Kantian space delivers. It is on the contrary a resisting term, a real being, a mass moved by effort and which, even at rest, is always as it were lifted and held out of nothingness by a kind of latent tension that is the very life of absolute subjectivity, insofar as this life is here that of the original body. The phenomenological description here is precise and is not merely evocative: even the body at rest is not inert, since holding it is itself an exercise of the power that constitutes it.

This mass our life sustains and holds (as we say we hold our breath) does not remain undifferentiated and amorphous; it lets appear in itself structures to which we will make correspond the names of the different parts of our body — our limbs, torso, neck, muscles — but which originally are nothing of the sort and give themselves only as phenomenological systems expressing *the different manners in which our effort is yielded to*. In this structuration the original mass of our transcendent body splits into different parts obeying our movements, over which these have an immediate power. So our organic body is indeed the ensemble of our organs, but: these organs are not parts of extension and so are not juxtaposed in space, their original phenomenological being having nothing to do with the anatomical or physiological determinations science takes as object; and since they are nothing but the terms immediately obeying the original modes of subjective movement, we understand that they give themselves as an empire over which we have power and authority, as a region we know precisely within the power we exercise over it. That power traverses it entirely and goes to its very depths, since this region is nothing but that depth, that limit of our power — a limit not to be interpreted as a negative determination, as a term up to which our movement's influence would extend while still leaving something behind it unreached. The limit of our power here signifies on the contrary its *completion*, its effect, the result in which the subjective being of movement manifests that it is not the impulse of a power but a real power.

The being of our organic body not reducing to that of any one of our organs, but having been determined as the ensemble into which all these organs integrate, Henry must now give an ontological interpretation of this "ensemble" — an interpretation of great importance, since it bears on the problem commanding all these analyses, the unity of our body. *The interpretation of the organic body as the ensemble of all our organs will show that the unity of our transcendent body is nothing but the transcendental unity of the original being of the subjective body.* The unity of the organic body is essentially a *practical* unity, a unity by which and in which all our organs are equally at our disposal. The unity of these organs — the internal coherence of the phenomenological systems bending to our movements while opposing them that relative resistance characterizing the being of our transcendent body — is only the unity of our movements, of which these organs are the moving terms; a unity Henry has shown to be originally subjective and to belong to absolute immanence. Our organs are thus integrated into a totality and in that sense have lateral relations among themselves. But this totality and the ensemble of relations manifesting and defining it have by themselves no original character; they in no way found the unity of our transcendent body but rest on it. We dispose immediately of each of our organs, and it is in their common reference to this power of the subjective body that they find their unity. *The unity of the transcendent body is not a transcendent unity*; it is the unity of the power that moves the different parts of organic space, conferring on them their unity and letting them appear in the coherence of a structure containing them all — what we may consider the true *schema* of our body.

This schema is evidently not an image, nothing represented or theoretical. Thinking its ontological status rigorously, we must say it implies a double relation: the relation our organs sustain among themselves, and the immediate relation each of them maintains with the original being of subjective movement. The latter is a modality of the transcendental relation of being-in-the-world, a specification of the general act of transcendence, in which movement surpasses itself each time toward one or another of the resisting structures yielding to its effort and so determining the originally homogeneous mass of the organic body. These two relations are not on the same plane: the relation of our organs among themselves, which seems to found the internal coherence of the organic body, rests in reality on the relation of each organ to the power that moves them. *If all our organs form one and the same organic body, it is because the power to which they are subject is one and the same power*, because it is a subjective unity that is the very unity of our original body. Thus the unity of the organic body is nothing but the transcendental unity of absolute subjectivity; it is in the unity of the transcendental inner experience of the power making the being of our original body that the principle of the integration of all our organs into an overall structure resides — the principle of that schema by which our organic body gives itself as a coherent and practical totality. Henry's treatment of the body schema is a pointed intervention: the schema is not a representation the ego consults but the correlate-structure of a single power, and treating it as an image reinstates the intellectualism of Chapter II.

One should not even say that the unity of our organs is a transcendent unity that must merely be recognized as founded and so implying a more original subjective unity; one must affirm that the unity of the organic body *is not different* from the original unity of our subjective body, since it is in the transcendental inner experience of movement that the unity of the organic structures this movement deploys resides. There does exist a transcendent unity of our body, but it is the unity of the *image* or representation of that body. The phenomenon of the schema of our organic body has no relation to an image of that sort. To say that the ego's power over its organic body is exercised through the mediation of a corporeal schema conceived as an image or any mediation between originally subjective movement and its term is to fall back into the intellectualist thesis that a representation of movement or of its instruments always precedes its real accomplishment. To affirm on the contrary that the unity of our transcendent body resides in the unity of the power transcending toward the different systems of resistance of the organic body, conferring on them the coherence of an overall structure where they become as it were the cleavage lines along which it is yielded to this single power of the subjective body — that is to give full sense to the theses maintained about the reality of subjective movement, the immediacy with which its term gives itself, and the fact that all ontological determinations of that transcendent term are the strict correlate of the subjective act aiming at it. In other terms, it is subjective life that holds the organic body in unity, in a unity originally its own belonging to a sphere of absolute immanence.

But then, if the unity of the transcendent body is the very unity of the absolute life of the subjective body, *the unity of the two bodies is thereby affirmed at the same time as the foundation appears of the organic body's belonging to the ego* — a foundation residing precisely in its unity, which is the unity of absolute subjectivity, of the life of the ego itself. Questions two and three thus find their solution, a solution displaying the solidarity uniting them. That solidarity is in reality a unity and can be expressed thus: it is because the unity of the transcendent body is the subjective unity of the original body that these two bodies form one and are traversed by one and the same life.

Doubtless the transcendent body does not belong to absolute immanence, and it seems abusive to identify its being with that of the original body, the subjective being of the ego. But it is not such an identification Henry has performed; he has rather shown that the being of the organic body is an *abstract* being, having by itself neither autonomy nor ontological sufficiency. It is in reality the unity of this being that founds it and lets it exist, and this unity is that of the absolute life of the ego. It is the ego's being that makes the whole being of the organic body, though it does not coincide with it, any more than vision coincides with the term seen. It is as *term of movement* that the organic body exists and gives itself as a coherent totality of parts, each the term of a movement, whose ensemble refers to the virtual totality of all possible movements of our body. It is therefore movement that sustains the being of the organic body while conferring on it its unity and its belonging to the ego. And it is precisely because it is not separable from this concrete subjective reality of movement animating and bearing it that the organic body, like the transcendent term of movement in general, escapes the phenomenological reduction. I am the life of my body; the ego is the substance of its organism, the matter and principle of its movements; and it is because it would be nothing without this foundation that our transcendent body, which is only the frontier of that life, finds in it its unity and the principle of the ontological determinations making it the body of the ego. The vision analogy carries the point: to say the ego's being makes the whole being of the organic body is not to say they are the same, just as seeing makes the whole being of the seen-as-seen without being the thing seen.

One might contest that the being of the organic body becomes concrete only by and in the life of the ego straining toward it to sustain and bear it, and be tempted to reverse the proposition or at least establish a symmetry between the two beings, making their *relation* alone something concrete and absolute and seeing in each term only an element abstract by itself, becoming real only in reference to the other. If there is no being of the organic body without an act of transcendence of subjective movement, must we not conversely recognize that the being of the original body could not subsist by itself and exists only in the transcendental relation uniting it to the transcendent being of the organic body? Is it not in this solidarity of ontological interiority and manifested being that the reason lies why both terms escape the reduction and give themselves as absolute terms, or rather as the two terms of an absolute relation? The objection is a serious one and Henry states it fairly: it would make the relation itself ultimate and demote immanence to one pole of a correlation.

That the organic body does not fall under the reduction in no way signifies, however, that it has the same ontological dignity as the original being of the subjective body, nor that the ontological sufficiency of absolute subjectivity is usurped and must shift to a zone of exchange between subjectivity and being, of which it would then constitute the essence and foundation. Such a zone of exchange, which Henry has called phenomenological distance, certainly exists; but we know it requires a foundation, and that this foundation resides precisely in the essence of the original truth of subjectivity. Subjectivity is therefore not a term remaining abstract by itself; and far from finding its reality and completion in the transcendent being of the organic body to which it is immediately bound by a transcendental relation, it is on the contrary the *foundation* of that being, which, as the term toward which it transcends, appears as its limit — but a limit still belonging to it.

This character by which subjectivity plays the ontological role of a genuine foundation did not escape Biran, who, after showing that the primitive fact is a duality — consisting in the original relation transcendence institutes between subjectivity and world, here between the subjective being of the original body and the organic body — nonetheless affirms that *there is a simpler relation anterior to that one*. What can such a relation be, the most original of all, if not one instituted within subjectivity itself and by which subjectivity reveals itself immediately to itself in transcendental inner experience — a relation that in truth is no longer a relation, since it is the very negation (an immediate, not a dialectical negation) of all mediation, but which is the very being of absolute life? It is this life, the life of the original body, that deploys the different parts of the organic body and holds them in unity; in it resides the principle of the organic body's belonging to the ego, a belonging that is nothing but its belonging to a life that is the ego's. The unity and ipseity of the life of the original body are the unity and ipseity of the absolute life of the organic body, because the life of the latter, the movement inhabiting and animating it, are precisely the life and movement of the original body. The reply to the symmetry objection turns entirely on this Biranian text: the correlation is not ultimate, because subjectivity has a self-relation prior to any relation to a term.

Completing the theory of the constitution of one's own body would require, alongside the theory of the constitution of the organic body, a theory of the constitution of our represented and objective body. The being of our transcendent body does not reduce to that of our organic body. Henry has shown the latter is not the object of a representation or theoretical knowledge.
