Yet we can represent our body and make it the correlate of a knowledge analogous to that of other objects, or even of the explicit conceptual knowledge we sometimes direct on determinate regions of the world when we want to build their science. Biran carefully distinguished these two modes of knowledge within which we can reach our transcendent body, calling the original knowledge of our organic body "immediate knowledge of one's own body" and the objective or representative knowledge "secondary knowledge of one's own body." The exterior experience I have of my body attaches exclusively to the secondary knowledge of one's own body as object of intuition or external representation, and leaves entirely aside that intimate sense on which is founded the knowledge of the body as term of the immediate internal apperception of the self. There is an immediate knowledge of one's own body founded solely on the reply of a willed effort and an organic resistance yielding or obeying the will. Thus, independently of the exterior knowledge of the form or figure of the parts of our body as object relative to touch and sight, there is an internal apperception of the presence or consistency of this body, wholly relative to a special muscular sense that can act and know itself only *within*, without being able to represent itself outside.

It is the immediate knowledge of our organic body, and it alone, that lets us move it — because it is precisely in the possibility of moving this body and successively developing its different parts that all this immediate knowledge consists, the knowledge by subjective movement of the transcendent term it deploys in its concrete exercise. There is thus a primacy of the immediate knowledge of one's own body over its representative or objective knowledge, and the paralogism of all the classical theories is to have forgotten this immediate knowledge in favor of the objective knowledge alone, supposed to furnish the totality of the knowing we have of our body. Thus Condillac did not seek how the self could directly acquire the interior knowledge of the organs; he occupies himself only with an objective and secondary knowledge of their exterior forms. To oppose to the "objective" knowledge of our body a "personal" knowledge delivering its being and each of its organs interiorly lived in movement is to denounce the conceptions reducing our body to a represented object, when not to a scientific object: the body-object-of-the-world of objective perception or of science implies, as we have seen, another body that knows it; and in general the sensible reality of objective appearance is always grasped within a power of knowing that is nothing but the original being of the subjective body.

If objective knowledge of the transcendent body thus presupposes a more original knowledge, a primordial knowing of our body, it exists nonetheless, and our body is also an objective being manifesting itself among all the other objects of the world. It is therefore not two bodies we must distinguish but three. Registering this tripartite division is essential, since Chapter V's critique of Descartes turns on the middle term the tradition lacks.

First, the original being of the subjective body, the absolute body revealed in the transcendental inner experience of movement. The life of this original body is the absolute life of subjectivity; it is in it that we live and move and sense; it is the alpha and omega of our experience of the world; it is by it that being comes to the world; it is in the resistance it experiences that the essence of the real manifests itself and every thing acquires consistency, form, and value. This resistance, however, is not homogeneous: sometimes it is only a *relative* resistance yielding to subjective movement, yielding along permanent lines drawing fixed structures — these structures are our organs, and the general milieu of this relative resistance is the organic body.

Second, the organic body, the immediate and moving term of the absolute movement of the subjective body, or rather the ensemble of terms on which movement has purchase. Because there is a structuration of this organic body, it is divided into different transcendent masses whose diversity is nonetheless held in the unity of the absolute life of the original body. The existence of such structures is of great importance for the problem of *internal sensations*, of which Henry has not yet spoken. If the mass of the organic body remained undifferentiated, our internal sensations would float within it without being localizable in any way and hence without being clearly distinguishable from one another. Internal sensibility would be a unity in confusion, a unity without diversity, and so there would be no sensations properly speaking but only the general, vague, confused feeling of an indeterminate sensible existence. In many respects coenesthesia answers such a description, offering an overall sensible and affective tonality much more than a diversity of rigorously determined and localized sensations. Yet a differentiation of our internal sensations is always realized. Affective zones spring from an undifferentiated and homogeneous ground, and these are also sensible zones manifesting themselves with proper characters letting us distinguish and oppose them to other ensembles of sensations likewise belonging to internal sensibility but radically different from those actualized in the affective and sensible present.

The principle of this differentiation doubtless resides in the irreducibly heterogeneous character of our different sensations; but these would still form only a dust of elements mixing inextricably in a compound where consciousness would risk losing and dissolving itself, if this sensible diversity did not rest on *another* diversity on which the ego has purchase and which it disposes of within a unity that is its own life's. This diversity founding internal sensible diversity is precisely that of the structures of the organic body, which Henry has shown are maintained in unity by the absolute life of the subjective body. Our internal sensations then organize themselves around these structures, which lose their primitively pure character to take on a sensible character that can mask — not to the living being but to the philosopher and psychologist — their true origin. The parenthesis is characteristic and important: the confusion is theoretical, not lived; the person moving has no difficulty here.

Biran gave an admirable description of this sensible structuration, bringing to light the organic foundation (itself founded on the absolute life of our body) such structuration requires: for impressions to be localized in the different parts of the interior space of one's own body, the parts must have been distinguished, put as it were outside one another, by the repeated exercise of their proper and immediate sense. But the general muscular system finds itself naturally divided into several partial systems offering as many distinct terms to the motor will. The more these points of division multiply, the more immediate internal apperception is illuminated and distinguished, the more the individuality or unity of the permanent subject of effort manifests itself by its very opposition to the plurality and variety of mobile terms. In setting itself outside each, the self learns to set them outside one another, to know their common limits and to refer impressions to them.

The important problem of the relation of our impressions to their seat, that is, to a place, receives here a solution provided one understands: that this place is not originally exterior space but the interior extension of the organic body, the transcendent milieu subjective movement deploys and which is its term; that this place is thus known *before* the impressions that will fill it, the knowledge of it being neither representative nor theoretical but immanent to movement and belonging to it (which is to say that this space is precisely not exterior space but "interior extension"); and that the insertion of impressions into the transcendent milieu that subjective movement deploys and opens for them is made possible by the fact that these impressions are not blind irreducible masses — of which one would not see why they come to take place in that milieu — but are constituted, that is, *sensed*, and that the power constituting them, our transcendental power of sensing, is not different from the original being of subjective movement. In other terms, it is by one and the same act that I deploy the transcendent milieu of the organic body that is the term of my movement, and perceive internal sensations as situated in and belonging to that milieu. That the place is known before what fills it is the key to the localization problem and follows directly from Chapter III's account of space as the product of motor exploration rather than its condition.

Fully understanding this last thesis would require an ontological theory of *passivity*, indispensable to a general theory of sensibility. Both theories are lacking in Biranism. For now it suffices to understand that internal sensible experience is inseparable from the original constitution of the organic body, that is, from the immediate knowledge of one's own body. This knowledge is complete by itself; it has autonomy and sufficiency; the whole primordial knowing of our body is contained in it and, naturally, in the original knowledge of the subjective body it implies and from which it is inseparable. The system formed by subjective movement and the organic body is a *closed* system, completing and closing on itself, which would be what it is in the absence of all representative knowledge of the transcendent body as objective being belonging to exterior space. Biran in a note: the self cannot exist for itself without having the feeling or immediate internal apperception of the coexistence of the body — that is the primitive fact; but it could well exist or have this apperception without yet knowing its body as object of representation or intuition through the exercise of touch. Henry again flags the missing theory of passivity, and it is the third such flag; Chapter VI will make it the axis of the break with Biran.

Third, the objective body, object of an exterior perception and possible theme of scientific research, is the only body the philosophical tradition knows; and it is this exclusively objective conception that is at the origin of so many false problems — notably the famous problem of the union of soul and body — and of so many theories vainly striving to solve them. Biran is the first philosopher to have understood the radical insufficiency of this standpoint and the derived, secondary character of every objective conception of the body, as one sees in a remarkable text: when philosophy raised the question how the sentient and moving being first learns to know its own body, it has in view only a mode of exterior and objective knowledge; it has again taken a secondary phenomenon for the primitive and simple fact. In making the origin of the representative knowledge of the body a problem, one already supposes the problem of existence solved, or rather one does not believe there is occasion to raise a question. Yet this mobile hand, which travels successively over the different parts and becomes the unit of measure of a sensible surface, does not palpate itself, any more than the eye sees itself, and yet it can and must be known before being employed as instrument or measure. Note that the diagnosis of the mind–body problem is genetic rather than dialectical: the problem arises from an omitted term, not from an antinomy.

Henry has tried, following Biran, to account for this original knowledge of our body presupposed by the representative knowledge of the objective body. He now gives some indications on the constitution of the objective body, for which the theory of the subjective and organic bodies furnishes the essential elements.

Insofar as it manifests itself in the world as an object, our transcendent body is only a spatial configuration endowed with the secondary or primary qualities belonging to all objects. But the being of our objective body cannot be reduced to this ensemble of spatial or sensible determinations, for it would then be only an anonymous object among all others, and one could not see where the foundation could lie of that particular right, as Descartes says, by which I call this body mine. Something therefore intervenes in the constitution of our objective body that did not intervene in the constitution of ordinary objects, something conferring on the objective body the character by which it gives itself as having an *interior*, and an interior of a determinate nature. What does this "interior" consist in, making our objective body appear as a body that is one, that belongs to us, and that is the same as the body we experience interiorly and immediately?

Clearly the interiority of the objective body rests on ontological interiority, and it is thus by a *borrowing* from the transcendental body and the originally subjective being of the ego that the objective body is what it is and gives itself with distinctive characters. That a natural being (the eye or the ear) which, as a determination and part of nature, is evidently deprived of all possibility of transcending and knowing, should nonetheless be said to see or hear, is possible only if we are otherwise in possession of what is signified by these words "see," "hear," that is, if the transcendental inner experience of the subjective body in general is given us. It is therefore in the content of the original subjective experience of our body that the objective body draws the signification inherent in it, determining it precisely as a body that is ours, that sees, that accomplishes movements. Furnishing an explicit answer to how this objective signification is founded each time on the corresponding subjective experience is to treat the problem of the constitution of one's own body for itself; Henry confines himself to a remark on the unity of the objective body and its belonging to the ego.

The unity of the transcendent objective body is a *transcendent* unity, a founded unity. As such it must not be confused with the unity of the organic body, which was nothing but the original subjective unity of the absolute body. It is on this latter unity that the unity of the transcendent body rests, in the sense that it is its simple *representation*, its projection into the part of extension the objective body occupies. As for the belonging of this objective body to the ego, it must be understood in the same way as its unity. In other terms, the life of the objective body is not absolute life but a representation of it; and consequently we must recognize that there is no absolute identity between our objective body and our original body, but a genuine duality between them. Because our objective body is only a representation of our original body, the problems posed by the duality of these two bodies and the unity of signification uniting them are wholly analogous to the problems concerning the relations of the transcendent ego and the absolute ego. To the *real* identity of the original body and our organic body — or rather to the identity of the absolute life that is the being of the original body and holds in its unity the organic body, whose life it therefore also is — is opposed the *represented* identity of our transcendent objective body with our absolute body, an identity resting naturally on the original identity of the being of the subjective body, that is, of the ego. The distinction between real and represented identity is the chapter's final refinement, and it prevents the solution from collapsing into the identification Henry has been at pains to refuse: organic body and subjective body are genuinely one life; objective body and subjective body are not.

Many other questions arise about the constitution of our objective body, which cannot be examined here. It mattered only to bring to light the ontological horizon within which such a constitution operates. It is on this constitution of the objective body that the double use of signs rests — and it is not totally illegitimate, since the constituted body effectively borrows the essential of its being from the original body and since, if not the absolute life of the latter, at least the *representation* of that life is immanent to it. The sign drawing all its signification from a transcendental inner experience of our original body can nonetheless refer equally to the natural being of the objective body, or even to one of its parts, since the representation of that transcendental inner experience intervenes in the constitution of our body-object, in the elaboration of the general signification conferred on it. The chapter's promise is thus kept: the illusion is justified rather than merely denounced, because the sign's transferred application tracks a real transfer of signification in the constitution.

With the three phenomena of our body studied successively (subjective, organic, objective) one should not confuse the image or images we may form of this body. Such images certainly exist, and describing them is a task for a phenomenology of the body. All Henry says is that these images require a foundation constituted by the real being of the objective body but also by the being of our organic body. The nature of the latter explains that we can have an image of certain parts of our body that our objective perception does not let us reach, at least directly. To our organic body belong all our organs, including those we do not perceive objectively; so an image founded on the organic body offers a representation of our body infinitely richer and more complete than one relating only to the being of our objective body. Henry notes that insofar as the image of our body rests on the being of our organic body, it is an image of the *total* being of our body and in no way a lacunary image — which it would be if it had no other foundation than the representative knowledge of our objective body. That the body image is non-lacunary is a testable consequence and a genuine payoff of the tripartite division: the organic body reaches organs the objective body never sees.

A phenomenological description of one's own body, of the organic body for instance, must not be duped by the attitude it has instituted, because such an attitude is not a natural attitude but its modification. The transcendent body that becomes the theme of thought in the phenomenologist's philosophical interrogation is, in our daily life, only a marginal phenomenon whose constitution operates in shadow. To occupy oneself with one's body is not an immediate or habitual attitude: in the latter, the body occupies itself with the *world*, which is quite different. Certainly the surging of our body into the phenomenological field occurs not only in philosophical interrogation but on many other occasions of daily life, particularly in certain essential modalities of our affective or corporeal life, phenomena in which we take an acute consciousness of our body and its particularities. On pain of a paralogism whose repercussions on descriptions and on the general theory of the body risk being grave, one must recognize that such phenomena can be understood only from the natural attitude, of which they are never anything but a variation, however important. The methodological warning is aimed at descriptive phenomenologies of illness, fatigue, and shame that take the salient body as the paradigm; for Henry these are modifications of a prior state in which the body is not thematic at all.

If we make the effort to plunge back into this natural attitude, the problems reputed insoluble concerning the relations of the self and its body, the "union of soul and body," grow clear; and the inextricable difficulties in which philosophical reflection sank when approaching such problems seem imputable to a manner of posing them that has nothing to do with our original experience or the spontaneous life of man. The dualism of soul and body — that is, of the original being of the subjective body and the transcendent body — is only a particular case of ontological dualism. The act by which subjective movement deploys the hand as an organic mass known interiorly, as the term toward which its not intellectual but motor knowledge transcends, is neither more nor less mysterious than the act by which my gaze aims at and reaches the tree standing over there on the hill. The dualism these descriptions bring to light is not an ontic dualism; it is a dualism no different from the one recognized between original truth and the truth of transcendent being, expressing the founding relation of the unity of these two truths, of the unity of experience — a dualism that has nothing to do with Cartesian dualism. The final sentence is the chapter's bridge to what follows: having built a dualism that dissolves the union problem, Henry turns in Chapter V to the dualism that created it.

## Chapter V. Cartesian Dualism

The ontological analysis of the body is one of the master pieces of Cartesianism. It is an essential analysis, issuing in extension as the essence of body. There is no ground to distinguish several forms of extension: it is the same extension we conceive or imagine, the difference lying in the act of mind apprehending it, not in its nature. Extension is the milieu where movements occur, movements purely mechanical and reducing in every case to a displacement of the parts of extension, one pushing another, this operation being accomplished in an instant. The ontological determination of the essence of body as extension has in Cartesianism an absolutely general signification: it holds not only for the inert body of physical nature but also for the living body and the human body. Hence in one case the famous theory of animal-machines, and in the case of the human body its conception as an assemblage in extension of parts themselves extended and mechanically linked. In truth there is no difference between the human body and the animal's, any more than between the latter and any physical body.

Comparing the results of the essential analysis of body in general with the particular body that is ours, what relation can exist between the latter, as it delivers itself in the most manifest experience, and the essence Cartesian philosophy confers on it *a priori*? None. So true is this that when he comes to consider the phenomenological body of man, Descartes no longer attributes extension to it as essential determination, but thinks he can account for the exact nature of this body — no longer identifiable in its essence with any other body — only by recognizing a new simple nature, no less fundamental than the two primitive natures of extension and thought on which the whole Cartesian edifice seems in many respects to rest. This new fundamental simple nature is the union of soul and body. Cartesianism is then no longer a dualism: the three primitive simple natures are equal in dignity and autonomy, equal too with respect to the bond of dependence uniting them to absolute substance, that is, to God. Note the structure of the objection Henry is preparing: the *permixtio* is introduced precisely where the essential analysis fails, which already suggests it is a repair rather than a discovery.

The way to understand the primitive nature of the union has plunged commentators into embarrassment. On one hand the union of thought with an extended substance is unintelligible; on the other, if this union is a fact, philosophical reflection must admit it, unintelligible though it be. Descartes misrecognized neither the unintelligible character of the union nor its character as undeniable fact, before which the understanding and its requirements had to bow. Against those who, following Spinoza, denounce the theory's absurdity, many commentators have insisted on the profoundly human character of Cartesianism, on the philosophical humility it attests before the human "nature" that the substantial union seems to define.

But has a philosophical interpretation of the Cartesian simple nature of the union ever been furnished? Have the conditions making such an interpretation possible ever been posed? Should one not first recognize, from a purely philosophical standpoint, the fundamental ambiguity presiding over the constitution of the doctrine? This ambiguity must not be confused with the intrinsically obscure or incomprehensible character of the theory; it is revealed rather in the fact that the theory is sometimes declared by Descartes himself extremely difficult to understand and sometimes presented as so clear and evident it cannot be denied — Henry cites the *Conversation with Burman* for both formulations side by side. Is it then the same thing at issue? Or does the task not impose itself of establishing a division between what is effectively so clear and distinct that it cannot be denied, and what is on the contrary so difficult to explain and so obscure that we must perhaps consider it impossible and false? The wedge Henry drives here is the chapter's method in miniature, and the textual evidence for it is Descartes's own oscillation.

And how could we effect such a division except by disposing of a philosophical horizon letting us distinguish with certainty the certain from the hypothetical — that is, by operating here again a phenomenological reduction that will welcome what is delivered in the absolute certainty of subjective experience but will refuse what, in the dogmatic content of Cartesianism, must be considered a pure transcendent hypothesis whose absurdity could then no longer claim the alleged character of fact? It is by one and the same movement that phenomenological reflection will make the absolute evidence of the *fact* arise and abandon to the reduction the hypothetical, indeed absurd, element of the *theory*. The ambiguity of the Cartesian position will then appear in full light: it consists in inextricably mixing the two standpoints, in giving for a fact what is only a theory, but above all, in an infinitely perfidious manner, in surreptitiously including the theory in the very statement and definition of the fact. Thereupon, in recognizing the fact as one must, one is led without noticing to admit the implausible theory. This is the charge, and it should be registered precisely: the theory is smuggled into the description of the datum, so that acknowledging the datum looks like conceding the theory.

What then is the indubitable "fact"? Will one say it is the union of soul and body? How not to see, on the contrary, that into the definition of this fact as *primitive nature of the union of soul and body* the theory already enters — the affirmation of the mixture of extended and thinking substance? Is this mixture (*permixtio*) really a fact? What is its phenomenological status? In formulating this question Henry in no way aims to drag the problem out of its Cartesian context, to pose it within his own philosophical horizon. It is indeed within a phenomenological ontology that Cartesianism itself elaborated the essences of thought and extension, and it is evidently in the same manner that one should proceed to determine the essence of the third primitive nature. What phenomenological content lets us affirm the existence of such an essence? Is the mixture of soul and body given to us in any manner? One will not fail to remark that not only would Descartes admit the legitimacy of this question, but he has in fact answered it strikingly: did he not declare repeatedly that to conceive the union correctly it was necessary no longer to reflect on this phenomenon but to abandon oneself to it and *live* it? Can one wish a more explicit appeal to experience? Henry's fairness here is real and strategic: he grants Descartes the best evidence available, the lived experience of union, and will show it proves something other than what it is called on to prove.

The essential remark is that the experience in question is a *transcendental inner experience* whose content is itself transcendental: the facts constituting it belong to the sphere of the *cogito*; they are *Erlebnisse*. Whether they concern sensible knowledge, imaginative life, our sentiments and passions, or the experience of our action in daily life, we have to do with facts, and indubitable ones, which are so many determinations of the *cogito* and participate in the absolute certainty that is the privilege of that region of existence. But how then elude this question: *in what way is the substantial union of soul and body implied in such transcendental inner experiences?* Why did Descartes invent, alongside the sphere of the *cogito*, another region of being, that of the *permixtio*, destined to receive facts that are *Erlebnisse* and consequently belong to absolute subjectivity and to it alone? The question is the chapter's hinge, and its force is that the evidence Descartes appeals to lies wholly inside the *cogito*, so it cannot testify to a region outside it.

Doubtless the facts Descartes refers to the third primitive nature are *Erlebnisse* of a particular character: they define within the *cogito* a *sui generis* zone of existence that seems the reign of affectivity and certainly differs from the pure thought of the mathematician resolving an equation. This specific character Descartes thinks to account for by making it the *effect* of the union, a disturbance resulting from the intervention of the body in the domain of pure thought — an intervention that is nothing but an action of body on soul, which in turn presupposes the union as its condition of possibility. But in this reasoning have we not passed insensibly from a fact to a theory aiming to explain it, a theory that is thenceforth only a transcendent construction whose status must be carefully distinguished from that of the *Erlebnisse* it claims to account for? Will one say the theory may be true? Had it not been admitted, despite its internal and specifically theoretical difficulties, on the sole pretext that a fact, even unintelligible, cannot be denied? But if the fact is not the theory, if it has in truth nothing to do with it, what credit can the theory's implausibility still claim? The argument closes cleanly: the theory's immunity derived entirely from its supposed factual status, and once that is withdrawn nothing shields it.

Or else one would have to show that it is in the internal structure, the very essence, of certain *Erlebnisse* — which one might call corporeal *Erlebnisse* — that the substantial union is somehow included. There certainly exist corporeal *Erlebnisse*; but when Henry speaks of a subjective body he means that the body at issue is *wholly subjectivity* and coincides in its being with the being of absolute subjectivity. It is otherwise in Cartesianism — or rather, and this is its whole ambiguity, it is sometimes a matter of a subjective body of which Descartes certainly had a brilliant presentiment, and sometimes, precisely in the theory of substantial union, of the union of the soul with a body that is very much the third-person body of physical and mechanical nature, a body whose essence is extension and which is always submitted, if not actually then at least virtually, to the category of *partes extra partes* that renders unintelligible its alleged mixture with the essence of thought.

Examine the *Erlebnisse* in which we experience our practical action or our passions. Considering them leads Descartes to a genuine scission of the *cogito*, in fact situating these *Erlebnisse* outside absolute subjectivity, insofar as their essence ceases to be thought and becomes the substantial union itself. Only on that condition could such *Erlebnisse* prove the union — on condition that the very substance they are made of be no longer thought but precisely the third primitive simple nature. But whence comes to corporeal *Erlebnisse* the character making them what they are, the character by which they give themselves phenomenologically as determinations of transcendental subjectivity, from which they draw their nature as absolute, certain, irrecusable facts? It is always the same ambiguity: Descartes, having taken these *Erlebnisse* where they effectively exist, in the absolute sphere of the *cogito*, then transports them into a region no longer having any authentic ontological character, deprived of all phenomenological footing, a transcendent construction whose unintelligible character renders it inadmissible even for a philosophy nourished on theories and hypotheses. The circularity is exact and worth marking: the union is proved by *Erlebnisse* whose evidential force depends on their belonging to the *cogito*, and the proof requires that they not belong to it.

Insofar as Descartes does not content himself with affirming the union as a hypothesis explaining the particular character of certain *Erlebnisse* (whose belonging to thinking substance as its modes would then not be denied), but sometimes seems to read this union in the phenomenological structure of these *Erlebnisse*, in their very fabric, a deeper *destruction* of his thesis is required. This destruction will bring to light a number of specifically Cartesian prejudices.

Why affirm the existence of a primitive nature other than thought as milieu destined to receive corporeal *Erlebnisse*, when these find their natural place in subjectivity and it is even impossible to conceive their being as different from the *cogito*'s? Because Descartes forms an idea of thought that had finally to exclude such *Erlebnisse*, at least so long as one considers thought in the pure state. Descartes's ideal is that of theoretical and intellectual knowledge, an impassive apprehension of mathematical being in which there is room neither for sentiments nor passions. Hence the idea, proper besides to every intellectualism, that affectivity in general is something inferior and cannot as such belong to the pure essence of thought. Hence, finally, the hypothesis that the degradation of pure thought into affectivity, unable to have its principle in the essence of thought, necessarily stems from the interference in it of a foreign element, the body. This body, however, is no longer the subjective body, nor does it coincide with the affective tonality proper to corporeal *Erlebnisse*; it is the body-extension delivered by the essential analysis of the piece of wax. Here the division between the phenomenological and the explanatory aspect of the theory is made clearly, and the ambiguity commanding all these Cartesian analyses dissolves: we have on one hand the *Erlebnis* with its proper psychological character — and it alone can be said to be so evident it cannot be denied — and on the other the pure conception of a problematic intervention of body-extension in the essence of pure thought. The genealogy Henry offers is a strong claim about motive: the *permixtio* exists because affectivity had to be got out of thought, not because experience displays a mixture.

Descartes's thesis at least affirms that the *Erlebnis* delivered on the plane of the *cogito* would not be what it is if an action of extended on thinking substance did not occur elsewhere. But this is the moment to say with Hume that in the effect — supposing the corporeal *Erlebnis* to be an effect — we no longer read the energy of its cause. The *Erlebnis* is what it is; it is a perfect transparency and as such has absolute ontological sufficiency. The essence of thought is a substance. Henry disclaims taking up Arnauld's objections: one will always be free to affirm that these two complete substances can nevertheless unite, provided one recognizes their union is not necessary but only accidental — and does human "nature" not give itself precisely as the product of an incomprehensible accident? But it is not a question of discussing the nature and properties of substances; what is at stake, and cannot be put in question, is the absolute and irreducible value of the *cogito*. The being of the *Erlebnis* coincides with its subjective and transcendental appearance. Neither the extended body nor its alleged action on the soul is enclosed in that appearance. The union of soul and body is therefore not a fact, nor a primitive nature — if by that one means an ontological region founded on an irrecusable phenomenological given; it is a pure affirmation resulting from Descartes's inaptitude, and that of many other philosophers, to understand that affectivity can belong to the essence of pure thought. Turning Hume against Descartes is a deft move and consistent with Chapter II: Henry earlier granted Hume's principle for the world, and it applies exactly here, where the *Erlebnis* is being treated as an effect of an extended cause.

Now precisely, there is here nothing to understand, only facts to establish. If affective *Erlebnisse* exist, then thought can be, in its very essence, affectivity. And the Cartesian positions reverse and destroy themselves: one denies that in its own pure essence thought can be affectivity or sentiment; one then imagines the action of an exterior agent producing this affectivity in it; one constructs the theory of substantial union. What then is this theory's point of departure, its real foundation, if not *the very fact of the affectivity of thought*? And what does it aim to verify, if not Descartes's prejudice that thought is not in itself affectivity? The truth is that thought is affective. The Cartesian and intellectualist prejudice is that it could not be. The *cogito* evidently gives the truth its due, showing that there are affective thoughts. Far from resting on a fact, the theory of substantial union is thus nothing but a roundabout means of *denying* the fact, of denying the truth revealed by the *cogito* — the affectivity of thought — and of affirming that it is by an accident, of which one gives a fantastic theory, that thought discovered itself in the *cogito* as affective. But when the moment comes to prove this theory, one appeals to the *cogito*, that is, to the affectivity of thought. The unavowed goal of the Cartesian theory of substantial union is thus to deny the fact it nonetheless invokes in its support and of which it claims to be a faithful translation. This is the chapter's central demonstration and its most exportable result: the *permixtio* is a device for denying the datum that motivates it.

What characterizes Cartesianism on this problem is the *absence of a transcendental theory of affectivity*. This essential lacuna is doubtless detectable in every intellectualist philosophy. But seeking its origin within Cartesianism itself is to advance further into understanding its general signification, that is, the conception of thought it implies. Considering the content of the *cogito*, one notes considerable differences among our *Erlebnisse*. These differences are themselves *transcendental* differences; it is on the plane of the *cogito* that they appear, in its immanent subjective content that a hatred, for example, differs from a mathematical conception. The theory of substantial union, far from founding such a distinction, is only a speculative variation resting wholly on this prior subjective difference. Yet while all our *Erlebnisse* are equal in phenomenological and ontological status — their psychological differentiation expressing only the modalization of absolute subjectivity — Cartesianism establishes among them a hierarchy that can rest only on considerations of another order, namely axiological. Mathematical conception is deemed superior to passion despite the identity of their phenomenological status. Pure thought, the essence of thought, is then identified with mathematical-type thought, with intellectual and theoretical knowledge, all affective or sensible thought being declared inferior and in a certain way heterogeneous to pure thought. The diagnosis separates two things Descartes runs together: a value ranking among *Erlebnisse*, which is legitimate, and an ontological division, which is not.

What is the origin of this devaluation of affectivity, or this overestimation of pure theoretical knowledge? *That origin resides in the affective tonality of intellectual knowledge, in the particular pathos of its own certainty.* The reason a certain form of thought (and not at all the pure essence of thought, definable only from the phenomenological status common to all *Erlebnisse*) is preferred to affective thought lies in the *affectivity of that privileged form*. What is this to say, if not that all thought is affective thought, that every *Erlebnis* has its own tonality? This is just what the transcendental theory of affectivity shows, a theory resting on the ontological structure of absolute subjectivity. The reversal is the chapter's most elegant stroke: the very preference that devalues affectivity is itself an affective preference, so the devaluation refutes itself.

The different forms of affective life are certainly neither similar nor equivalent. Establishing a hierarchy among them is perfectly legitimate, provided one recognizes its purely axiological or existential character. When we say quite validly that love is superior to hatred, we do not thereby affirm that the essence of thought differs in the two cases. One may likewise place the doxic life of consciousness above its passional life. That is no reason to claim that the essence of the passional *Erlebnis* is not that of thought in general. If Descartes affirms the superiority of intellectual knowledge, it is because he found in it a particular mode of existence, an affective experience that was precisely the one he sought: the experience of a certainty raised above all doubts. And if mathematical experience is unduly privileged in him — if in general triangles play in the history of philosophical thought a role certainly disproportionate to the interest we accord them in our concrete life — that is because the kind of necessity with which their properties impose themselves signifies, for the consciousness abandoning itself to the mathematical object, a repose, an assurance, a kind of ecstasy within that assurance Descartes had so much sought. The remark about triangles is not merely witty; it identifies the affective motive behind an entire philosophical style.

These remarks would hold too for the other Cartesians, Spinoza for instance: reflecting on the existential signification of the progress of consciousness through the three kinds of knowledge, one may notice that knowledge of the third kind proceeds from the abstraction of the affective content of rational knowledge and the positing of that content for itself, independently this time of knowledge of the second kind. The existential signification of Spinozist experience shows constantly through the dogmatic exposition of the *Ethics*, and is explicitly recognized by Spinoza himself, notably at the beginning of the *Treatise on the Emendation of the Intellect*. What Descartes and Spinoza asked of intellectual knowledge and the eternal rational truths it is supposed to deliver is, in the face of the vicissitudes and contingency of our historical existence, the experience of a certainty that events cannot ravish from us and — to borrow an image from Alain — of a happiness that clings to us more closely than a coat.

So it is the secret intentions of Cartesianism that themselves dispose of the intellectualist prejudice devaluing pure affectivity. Because each *Erlebnis* has a tonality consubstantial with it, affectivity, far from resulting from a contribution exterior to thought, is on the contrary an *eidetic determination* of it. There is no ground to distinguish certain affective *Erlebnisse* from others that would not be so; all our experiences, insofar as they are our different ways of living, bear in them what is precisely the first character of all life and all experience, which Henry calls, for want of better, an affective tonality. The impassibility of the *Erlebnisse* of theoretical knowledge is only one determination among others of this tonality — a subtler one perhaps in its apparently privative character, but so little deprived of what makes the essence of affectivity that it is rather by virtue of a proper affective character that theoretical life has received in many systems an often exclusive privilege. It was not, all told, affective life in general that was depreciated, but only certain of its modes, while others, those we live in our theoretical life, were invested with positive value for reasons precisely of an affective order. Did Descartes himself not recognize the transcendental nature of affectivity when he said there are purely intellectual joys and pleasures? Affectivity as such cannot therefore result from the substantial union, since there exist affective states in which this alleged union plays, by Descartes's own admission, no role. The intellectual joys are decisive counter-evidence from Descartes's own text: an affectivity with no bodily involvement shows affectivity is not the body's contribution.

It appears then that if other affective states are devalued and conceived as resulting from the body's interference in the sphere of thought, this can no longer be by virtue of their *affective* character. What then leads Descartes to split not the *cogito* in general but the affective *cogito* itself into affective *Erlebnisse* that are pure, as it were, and affective *Erlebnisse* stemming from the union? It is the feeling, borrowed from the proper affective content of these *Erlebnisse*, that our affective life is sometimes like the expansion and flowering of our own existence and sometimes, on the contrary, like the experience of our dependence, our finitude, and our misery. *The Cartesian theory of the passions concerns the problem of existential alienation.* It is not difficult then to see that the solution of this problem obeys in Cartesianism a general schema consisting in the attempt to found existential alienation on an *ontological* alienation supposed to be its cause. But the difference between these two sorts of alienation is precisely the one separating pure theory from fact — the fact here being the inner experience of passion as we live it in our very existence. It is to such experience that we owe all the knowledge we have of our sentiments; that experience alone delivers their signification along with the principle of their distinction. Henry notes in passing that "ontological alienation," which in this book designates the third-person determination of thought by a reality foreign to its essence, has a wholly different sense from the one it receives in *L'essence de la manifestation*.

But now Descartes affirms that the reason for our various passions lies wholly in the movements of the animal spirits, blind movements without design. It follows, on one hand, that human passion is deprived of all signification — and this is the ruin of every moral science of man; on the other, that man is no more responsible for his passions than for the circulation of blood in his veins — and this is the ruin of all morality in its reduction to a kind of mechanic's knowledge. The progress of a consciousness rising above its passion is in no way a progress of that passional consciousness itself; it results neither from the experience it has of the contradictions rending its life, nor from the movement carrying it of itself toward a beyond of these contradictions, nor from a reflection on their signification; it can be in truth only the product of an exterior and mechanical intervention executed on a mechanism itself mechanical.

The Cartesian who treats his passion is like the Freudian who wants to be delivered from a complex: both seek to act, by means certainly hard to find, on a third reality = x — physiological or psychological unconscious — that contains in its mechanism, whose unfolding escapes our knowledge as much as our will, the secret of our affective existence, our life, our destiny. The philosophical task of a positive interpretation of man's real alienation, starting from the illumination of the experience in which he lives that alienation, is completely abandoned. Because it no longer bears in itself the principle of its unhappiness, human existence is no longer the center of the problem of alienation of which it nonetheless seems the theater. The obstinate will to explain everything on the schema of a third-person relation between a cause and an effect conceived as objects means that the first conditions a theory of alienation must satisfy — those the phenomenological given delivers — are no longer even taken into consideration. Can the problem of alienation in fact be posed anywhere but in a philosophy of the first person? Is there any sense in saying, for example, that a stone is alienated? To reduce the ego to the condition of a third-person effect, as the theory of ontological alienation does, is not to solve the problem of man's real alienation but only to *suppress* it. Ontological alienation cannot constitute the foundation of a theory of existential alienation; it is not its explanation but only a projection, accomplished by a coarsely realist imagination, into the obscure heaven of myth. The stone question is the sharpest form of the point and generalizes well beyond Descartes: alienation is a first-person category, so any third-person mechanism offered as its cause has changed the subject.

Does the Cartesian theory of substantial union, however, only answer the concern to account for a confused thought opposed to the intellectualist ideal of clear and distinct thought? Does it not rather obey another requirement, insofar as it concerns an authentically philosophical problem: the problem of the action of soul on body? Such a problem cannot be eluded by philosophical reflection, but the pseudo-solution Cartesianism brings with the theory of substantial union has for its sole result to bring to light the ontologically improper character of the philosophical horizon within which the problem is posed by Descartes as it will be by his successors. The ontological theory of the body, more precisely the theory of subjective movement and its relation to the organic body, was in fact nothing but a theory of action, and of corporeal action. Where does one see that it placed us before aporias and difficulties like those Cartesianism meets, and after it almost all philosophical conceptions on this subject? One of the explicit aims of Biranian reflection was precisely to show that the problem that is the cross of so many philosophies bears not on a mysterious and incomprehensible event but on a very clear and self-evident fact, provided one will consider it as a *phenomenon*, hold strictly to the given and reflect on it alone, abstracting from all the transcendent constructions rendering it unintelligible. What matters, then, is not so much to render transparent the action the soul exercises on the body — the ontological theory of the body has answered that requirement — as to understand *how in the philosophical tradition such an action became incomprehensible*. This reframing is the chapter's real project and worth holding onto: the task is genetic, an account of how a clear phenomenon was made obscure.

The dogmatic exposition of the ontological theory of the body has already led to this task. The destruction of Hume's positions already introduced us to a general critique of Cartesian dualism and its philosophical posterity. The absurdity of Hume's skepticism is here the *truth* of Cartesianism: it shows, through the coherence of the doctrine and the rigor of its deductions, the absurdity of the point of departure. Hume proved the absurdity of Cartesian dualism, an absurdity the verbalism of the theories of parallelism, occasionalism, or pre-established harmony still masked in the great Cartesians. Henry adds in a note that Spinoza, for instance, makes no progress on the Cartesian problem of the relation of soul and body: he confines himself to solving *otherwise* the same problem Descartes posed, instead of posing that problem otherwise. Hume too does not modify the statement of the problem, but has at least the merit of showing that, posed in this manner, it is absolutely insoluble — whence there is room for a philosophy like Biran's, which will no longer reflect on the internal difficulties of the Cartesian problem but on the inadequacy of its philosophical horizon. That distinction — solving otherwise versus posing otherwise — is the criterion by which Henry sorts the whole post-Cartesian tradition.

The philosophical signification of the Biranian destruction of Hume's theses, and thereby of Descartes's, is specified in the critique of Engel's memoir to the Berlin Academy on the origin of the idea of force. Engel seems at first to oppose Hume, reproaching him with not having discovered the origin of the idea of force because he did not seek it where it effectively lies, in the exercise of the muscular sense. About the latter and the force of which it is the phenomenon, Engel employs a specifically Biranian expression: force wants to be *sensed* by the aid of its own sense. But it soon appears that Engel falls back into the classical ruts, and will therefore meet the same apparently insurmountable difficulty as Descartes and Hume. Having recognized that we experience our own force and that the idea of force in general takes its origin from that experience, Engel declares that we must renounce the hope of ever conceiving how two natures, one spiritual and one material, can act on one another. How does Engel come, after affirming the immediate character of our experience of action, to this strictly Cartesian and classical standpoint? What, in other terms, is *the genesis of the false problem of the action of soul on body and of its pseudo-Cartesian solution*? Engel is the perfect case for the genetic question because he begins from the right datum and still arrives at the impasse.

Consider the original fact of our action, the relation of subjective movement and the organic term toward which it immediately transcends: this relation is a *transcendental* relation. It is then easy to see that in the Cartesian standpoint, as in every philosophy that sooner or later meets the impossibility of conceiving an interaction between thought and extension, an unnoticed but radical modification has intervened in the conception of the terms in presence. On one hand the organic body, term of the transcendental inner experience of subjective movement *and of it alone*, has become the body-extension of the Cartesian essential analysis, object of an act of pure understanding; and so what was, in Biran's express declarations, "interiorly sensed and not represented," is taken for something represented. On the other hand absolute subjectivity, to which the original being of movement is immanent, has degraded: it has become thought-substance, has lost its authentic ontological character to take its place purely and simply in the general milieu of transcendent being, within which it now appears alongside intelligible extension or extended substance. The relation between these two substances can no longer be anything but a third-person relation analogous to those we discover in a world; it can no longer be anything but a causal relation. The problem of the relation of soul and body then offers itself as an insurmountable difficulty, and its solution — if not the most rational then at least the most significant — is doubtless parallelism, which consists at bottom in affirming and immediately denying the existence of such a relation. The genetic account is now complete and is the chapter's constructive result: two simultaneous degradations, of the organic body into represented extension and of subjectivity into a transcendent substance, place both terms in one region where only causal relations are available.

Engel's position illustrates this degradation strikingly: we have, he says, the representation of a determination of the will *in itself*; we have that of the movements of the muscles *in itself*; we draw the first from inner sense, the second from one of our external senses; the only thing lacking is the representation of the liaison or complication of the two. But the original being of our body, the being of subjective movement, was nothing other than the *experience* of that liaison — not, admittedly, its representation. Biran: it is just because this liaison is wholly comprised in the exercise of the sense of effort, and identical with the very fact of intimate sense, that we cannot prevent ourselves from admitting it. Henry notes that Engel's text displays with special clarity the transformation of the originally subjective being of movement into a "representation of the will in itself," and still more nettly the alteration of the organic body confused here with a representation furnished by the external senses.

The critique of Engel's memoir thus rejoins the one Biran directed explicitly against Descartes. Descartes confused the organic body with the body represented or conceived by the understanding; he then confused subjective movement with the mere representation of movement, the latter being reduced to a displacement in extension; he finally asked how to conceive a relation and an action between this pure representation and a body or movement in extension. Such a problem could arise only because one had long since abandoned the phenomenological given — the transcendental inner experience of subjective movement and its transcendent correlate; one had abstracted from this original "fact" two terms erected into "absolute" substances, that is, transformed into two objective realities. Reasoning then had to strive vainly to rejoin them, whence that very manifest contradiction between *a priori* reasoning founded on the absolute nature of substances and the primitive fact of experience founded on the testimony of intimate sense. Henry reminds the reader that this text takes on its full importance only if one keeps in mind the precise Biranian sense of "*a priori*" and "absolute" fixed in Chapter I, § 1 — where "absolute" names what has left the sphere of immanence. The degradation permitting such a contradiction is the immediate result of abandoning the standpoint of absolute immanence, which is an absolute standpoint, the ego's, from which everything grows clear. *Cartesian dualism is the product of such a degradation.*

The nature of this degradation is specified in another text of the *Essai* distinguishing the "phenomenal standpoint," said to be primitive, from the "noumenal standpoint," understood as derived. What offers itself in the phenomenal standpoint — when one holds to the phenomenological given — is on one hand the experience of subjective movement, the feeling of effort, and on the other the organic term on which this effort deploys. To the immanent and transcendent content of this experience, the noumenal standpoint substitutes the two "absolute" substances of soul and body, conceived as two noumena hidden beneath the two phenomenal terms. The problem of the relations of soul and body becomes, within this noumenal standpoint, the problem of the reciprocal action of two terms = x, of which we nonetheless know that one is extended and the other not. Whereas the relation of soul and body was perfectly clear within the *cogito* and coincided with the transcendental relation of subjective movement and its transcendent term, it is evident on the contrary that the mind will always make vain efforts to reduce to clear conceptions the means of correspondence or of reciprocal action and influence of noumena. The phenomenal/noumenal pair gives Biran's own name for what Henry has been calling the degradation, and the ordering is what matters: the noumenal standpoint is derived, so its insolubility is manufactured rather than discovered.

Doubtless the dualism the problem of action confronts us with is not inherent in the noumenal standpoint as such. Philosophical reflection holding strictly to the phenomenological given must also distinguish two "phenomenal" terms: the experience of subjective movement and the resisting continuum. But this latter dualism, which within the transcendental relation of being-in-the-world distinguishes what reveals itself within a sphere of absolute immanence from what manifests itself in the truth of transcendence, is nothing but *ontological dualism*. If, as Biran says, the noumenal standpoint is derived relative to the phenomenological, the two forms of dualism corresponding to them must not be unrelated: *Cartesian dualism is precisely a degradation of ontological dualism*, and Henry has already given the theory of that degradation. The pseudo-dualism it issues in consists in fact in casting both terms of the transcendental relation into the *same* ontological region, that of transcendent being — this casting implying, as regards absolute subjectivity, the forgetting of its radically immanent character, that is, at bottom, the destruction of its proper ontological character. Once such destruction is operated, nothing further opposes the institution of worldly relations, of causal links for instance, between two surreptitiously homogeneous realities: extension on one hand and, on the other, subjectivity, or rather its representation, its shadow in the milieu of exteriority. This denaturing of absolute subjectivity finally opens the way to the realist attitude, which inserts the ego into the world. The paradox is worth registering: Cartesian dualism is not too dualistic but insufficiently so — it is a *monism* of transcendent being wearing dualist dress, since both its terms occupy one region.

It is true that Descartes holds far from such an attitude: is the *cogito*, with its rigorous comprehension of the ontological character of subjectivity, not precisely its negation? Yet does the gap between the doctrine of the *cogito* and the theory of dualism — which destroys the transcendental relation of being-in-the-world and substitutes the juxtaposition of two substances — not already measure the extent of a fall that could only issue in the empirical realization of the subject, and in fact issued there in the classical psychology sprung from Cartesianism? Doubtless classical psychology bears other legacies no less heavy than Descartes's. If it is also commanded in particular by certain master theses of Kant, how not to see the deep affinity between these and the Cartesian dualism under discussion? Reflecting on it, one may see that despite very real differences, the Kantian theory of inner life, making it the product of a constitution parallel to the constitution of the exterior world, finally issues in a juxtaposition very similar to the one Cartesian dualism makes us conceive, if it is true that in the latter the radical ontological signification of the *cogito* is already lost. The convergence claim is a strong one and consistent with Chapter I, § 3: Kant's inner sense and Descartes's thinking substance both locate subjectivity in a constituted region, however different their routes.

Thus as soon as theory believes it can leave the absolute standpoint of subjective immanence, as soon as it surveys the original ontological regions from above, it is led to conceive between them relations that are no longer transcendental, that do not belong to the structure of our naive experience either, but constitute the system in which we *represent* such experience. *But it is at the moment when it ceases to be understood in its transcendental signification that the relation of soul and body becomes unintelligible.* The historical importance of Cartesian dualism comes from its opening a horizon within which solutions to the problem of the relations of soul and body would multiply for the reason that such a problem was thenceforth insoluble.

And then, as happens in such cases, what had necessarily to result from all the transcendent hypotheses by which one vainly tried to force the obstacle was *boredom* — a dull and secretly discontented boredom. The parallelism of the psychologists is the expression of this state of mind much more than a doctrine properly speaking. Discontent resulting from impotence easily becomes aggressive: the problem of soul and body was no longer anything but a purely philosophical problem, and the care of debating it indefinitely could be left without damage to the metaphysicians whose time is not taken by positive research. But the moment when one thus declared the problem of the relations of soul and body a mere pseudo-problem corresponded not to a calling into question of its philosophical horizon but on the contrary to a definitive acceptance of it, that is, of Cartesian dualism. The diagnosis of dissolution-by-boredom is a genuine insight and applies well beyond its occasion: declaring a problem spurious while retaining the framework that generated it ratifies the framework rather than escaping it.

It is within this preexisting dualist horizon that the theory of substantial union already intervened in Cartesianism. Reflecting on the singular position of the primitive nature of the union, one perceives that this nature is precisely nothing primitive. Does it not evidently presuppose that the existence of those two "natures" of which it is then envisaged as the mixture has already been affirmed? The attempt of the *Letters to Elisabeth* to found the doctrine of the union on the *cogito* thus appears eminently suspect. Far from expressing a fact reasoning would not be entitled to combat, the doctrine of the union is on the contrary the product of a reasoning whose premises are constituted by the theory of dualism as Descartes understands it. The point returns the chapter to its opening wedge and completes it: the third "primitive" nature is derivative twice over, presupposing the two natures it mixes and the dualism that separated them.

And when Descartes declares that it is by using only life and ordinary conversations that one learns to conceive the union of soul and body, when he counsels us to abandon ourselves to the relaxation of the senses, these celebrated texts can lend not the least aid to the dogmatic content of Cartesianism nor render it theoretically acceptable. To cease philosophizing in order to live and experience the union is a strange counsel on a philosopher's part: must one abandon the philosophy of the relations of soul and body, or only a *certain* philosophy, a certain conception of those relations? More precisely: does returning to fact and experience imply the rejection of philosophy?

Or does the requirement, on the contrary, of a philosophy of fact and experience — in the case at hand, of a transcendental phenomenology of the ego and the subjective body — not arise before this failure of speculation? Does such a failure not stem solely from an insufficiency in the ontological elucidation of the problem reputed insoluble? The return to a phenomenological standpoint will signify, here as elsewhere, a reflection on the horizon that previously draws the frame of our research. None more than Biran understood the necessity of satisfying this first philosophical requirement. Speaking of the great metaphysical questions left unanswered, the author of the *Essai* says that their object, never having been well and clearly circumscribed, meant one did not well know what one was asking or seeking — a sure means of not finding. And further: one would have to try to show in what the insolubility of the problem consists, to say how and why it is insoluble. To this last task the theory of the subjective body taken as a whole responds, as regards the relations of soul and body — a theory consisting finally in substituting for Cartesian dualism the original reality of which the latter is only the travesty, namely ontological dualism, the correct philosophical interpretation of the natural life of the ego, of the absolute immanence of subjectivity facing transcendent being. The closing formula names the chapter's whole result and its relation to Chapter IV: the two dualisms are not rivals but original and counterfeit, and the counterfeit's insolubility is the mark of its derivation.

## Chapter VI. Critique of Maine de Biran's Thought: The Problem of Passivity

The ontological theory of the body is incompatible with a dualism of Cartesian type. How is it that Maine de Biran, whose philosophical effort resulted in the deepest theory of the body most conformable to the requirements of a phenomenological ontology that tradition has delivered, remained in many respects the dupe of such a dualism? The revolution he accomplished was so total that it is doubtless reserved to history to understand its full signification little by little. So strong was Biran's opposition to his century that his philosophy had necessarily to bear in it heterogeneous elements, conformable to that century's thought but foreign to his own aim. The will to grasp the deep intuition of Biranism and remain faithful to it therefore implies rejecting everything in Biranism that does not belong to it properly but stems from philosophical positions against which it was gradually constituted without ever completely eliminating them. The chapter's critical principle is stated at once, and it is a principle of fidelity rather than of refutation: what Henry attacks in Biran is what Biran borrowed.

Biran's personal experience is one of alienation. It is the experience of an incessantly changing affective life, of a mood now gay, now sad, more often sad, whose modifications seem independent of the will of the self undergoing them. This servitude is doubly painful, by reason of the predominant affective tonality of the *Erlebnisse* considered (lassitude, boredom, malaise, discouragement), and by reason of the fact that these *Erlebnisse* impose themselves in an experience identically that of our impotence regarding the course of our history. Biran is conscious of his own life as an enemy destiny: the power of destiny, says the *Essai*, one of the most powerful dramatic springs, is perhaps only the expression of that fact of intimate sense manifesting at the depth of our being a kind of organic necessity opposed to moral liberty.

At the very moment Biran expresses this consciousness of a servile affective life, of a passion, he has clearly already transformed the *existential* alienation to which our experience doubtless testifies into an *ontological* alienation, which is no longer an experience but a principle of explanation. This principle strangely resembles the Cartesian schema of the *Treatise on the Passions*, and for that reason Henry will not discuss it again. That Biran admitted it spontaneously, and from his youthful writings on, lets Henry affirm it is *anterior* to the building of the ontological theory of the body, exterior to its essential content, that is, to true Biranism. The dating argument is the chapter's methodological lever: the dualism is not a consequence of the discovery but a prior deposit the discovery failed to dislodge.

The influences Biran underwent before arriving at his own philosophy explain his adherence to the dualism Henry denounced in Descartes but which has continued to reign over almost all conceptions of the soul–body problem. Whether Rousseau or Bonnet, the ideologues (notably Cabanis) or the physiologists of his time, Biran found everywhere psycho-physiological schemas of explanation that he adopted not so much by reason of their intrinsic theoretical value as of the painful persistence of an experience with which they seemed to accord. It is this primitive association of a Cartesian-type dualism with a personal experience from which Biran could never wholly free himself that explains how this dualism could survive the prodigious discovery of the subjective body and remain in Biranism like a dead limb, still present, juxtaposed to the ontological theory of the body — or rather covering it and masking from nearly all commentators its inestimable philosophical import. To denounce the theses in Biranism stemming from traditional dualism is to bring to light its authentic and original ontological content by drawing it out of an inessential context under which it risks remaining buried. The critique of Biran's thought is in reality an effort to grasp that thought in its own movement; its signification is *positive*. Registering the diagnosis of motive is worth the effort: Biran kept a bad theory because it matched a real suffering, which is a different failing from arguing badly.

The Biranian theses stemming from dualism concern essentially instinct, sensibility, affectivity, imagination — precisely the points on which Biran shows no originality: *what he borrowed from others is what is false*. Dualism nonetheless casts its shadow over other developments, for instance his phenomenology of memory, and there it calls into question fundamental ontological theses and sometimes the very structure of subjectivity.

Instinct, sensibility, affectivity, imagination refer to man's "organic life." Insofar as such a life exists in him alongside his intelligent and voluntary life, man is a "double man" (*homo duplex in humanitate*). Insofar as man is double, insofar as his nature is a "mixed nature," the science of man cannot completely identify with a transcendental phenomenology, for room must be made alongside it for a "mixed psychology" no longer moving in the sphere of pure subjectivity but admitting the mixture and complication of heterogeneous elements, considering the facts of intelligence only at their point of contact with those of sensibility, those of sensation in their relation to objects and organs, acts of will in the sensible affections determining them, the passions in their influence on physiological phenomena and reciprocally. Thus to the knowledge of an order of mixed phenomena will be applied a method of analysis mixed as well, physiological and reflexive. The stake is now explicit and it is large: the *permixtio* readmitted here would cost phenomenology its claim to be first philosophy.

Must one insist on the fundamental ambiguity of the concept of organic life at the origin of these considerations? This organic life is defined sometimes within the *cogito* and sometimes from third-person physiological processes absolutely foreign to psychology. One affirms that this duality constituted by the opposition of intelligent and organic life is itself a fact of intimate sense, and one speaks elsewhere of a general affection the self can *become without apperceiving it*. In fact Biran confuses under the terms organic or animal life three very different sorts of reality: certain *Erlebnisse* (affections, images); objective and, for him, mechanical physiological movements; and a kind of unconscious psychological life situated between the first two orders, to which the expression "organic life" doubtless more particularly suits.

So understood, organic life is inadmissible in an ontology of subjectivity; and if it is present in Biranism, that is because a philosophy is easily the dupe of the one it opposes. Biran finds in Condillac a conception of human reality tending essentially to make it appear a sensible existence. The rejection of Condillacian sensualism is then made by discovering the active life of the self, which the *Mémoire sur la décomposition* opposes to sensible life *without however calling into question the conception the ideologues formed of the latter*. Thus is born a dualism opposing voluntary and motor effort to the passivity of sensible life while conserving of the latter a notion absolutely incompatible with the Biranian theory of subjectivity that is the mainspring of the ontological analysis of the body. Biran's man's sensible and affective life is analogous to the life of the statue that becomes the smell of a rose. The "sensitive," "vague," "impersonal" existence the self "becomes without apperceiving it" has no other origin. This is the chapter's central genetic claim and it generalizes far beyond Biran: a philosophy that inverts its opponent's valuation while keeping his description of the devalued term inherits the description intact.

Leibniz's influence confirmed this improper theory of sensible and affective existence. The metaphysics of the organic microcosm, the nesting of monads only some of which are endowed with the power of representation, the inadmissible distinction of "perception" and "apperception" — so many elements that contributed to giving the theory of animal life an unfortunately definitive form. The ontological nonsense of an "obscure perception without consciousness" was to find welcome in an ontology of subjectivity whose importance remains unequalled in French philosophy.

The incompatibility of this conception of animal life with that ontology's fundamental theses could not pass wholly unnoticed: it manifests itself in the difficulties the theory of inner life meets. Intuition and imagination bathe in total obscurity; Biranism can confer no precise status on the "internal organ of intuition" any more than on the "internal organ of imagination." Having operated a genuine scission of the *cogito*, casting to one side the modes of clear thought (intelligence and will) and to the other obscure affections, images, and interior sensibility, Biran commits two grave errors regarding the second category. First, he very quickly comes to misrecognize their character as *Erlebnisse*, their fundamental ontological structure; he treats them as infraconscious modalities without our being able to determine them otherwise than in this purely negative way. Second, precisely because in their fundamental ontological indetermination these *Erlebnisse*, cast so low from properly psychic life, are so to speak no longer *Erlebnisse*, he assimilates them to modalities of organic life, that is, to natural and transcendent elements. Biran is then the dupe of a disastrous confusion he had himself tirelessly denounced under the name of *the double use of signs*. The irony is exact and is Henry's sharpest internal argument: Biran's own diagnostic instrument from Chapter IV convicts him here.

By organic or animal life, then, one must understand sometimes natural processes, sometimes unconscious psychological modalities determined by these processes, sometimes *Erlebnisse* properly speaking whose conditioning by physiological reality is nonetheless always affirmed. To this last category belong our images, whose unfolding obeys, according to Biran, a purely mechanical law independent of the ego. Likewise our affective life, our sympathies and antipathies, are said to be "foreign to the self." It is therefore on the plane of the *cogito* itself that a division now appears: *cogito and ego no longer coincide*. It is the very foundation of the ontological theory of the ego that is struck.

Doubtless the experience of passion is a subjective experience situated on the plane of the *cogito*. One may consequently range our different *Erlebnisse* under the two opposed rubrics of liberty and servitude.
