But precisely because the experience of servitude is still a mode of the life of absolute subjectivity, the opposition instituted between autonomy and alienation cannot receive an ontological signification nor call into question the foundations of the philosophy of subjectivity: it has only a moral and existential bearing, the dualism it introduces is of axiological order, whereas Biran makes it a dualism of Cartesian type. And since inner life implicates the body by which one claims to explain it, it follows that in many respects the Biranian theory of the body, or at least of its relation to certain of our psychic states, strangely resembles the theses of the *Treatise on the Passions*, so that once more the sense and originality of the Biranian philosophy of the body risk passing unnoticed. The category error is the same one Chapter V identified in Descartes: a legitimate value ranking is converted into an illegitimate ontological division.

Because, to oppose the sensualism of Condillac and the ideologues, Biran believed himself obliged to define the true being of the self as effort and will, he tends to confer an exclusive privilege on the subject's active modes, as Descartes had done for the modes of mathematical-type thought. And just as in Cartesianism the confusion of mathematical thought with the essence of pure thought makes necessary the intervention of a foreign element, the extended body, to explain the so-called inferior forms of subjective life, so Biran, having identified ego and effort, finds himself totally destitute when he must account for affective life, imagination, and sensibility. He confines himself to borrowing from other philosophies conceptions that seem integral to Biranism but in reality only mask its essential lacuna: the absence of all theory of affective, imaginative, and sensible life, that is, *the absence of all ontological theory of passivity*. The parallel with Descartes is exact and is the chapter's organizing insight: both philosophers narrow the *cogito* to a privileged mode and must then import a foreign cause for everything left outside.

The limitation of the *ego cogito* to the subject that makes effort profoundly falsified the ontological signification of Biranism. In such limitation must be seen the principal reason for the incomprehension so generally shown toward Biran's thought, for its interpretation as a philosophy of will and force opposed to a philosophy of substance. It is this same identification of the self with activity apprehended within motor effort that will render unintelligible to nearly all commentators the evolution of Biranian thought and oblige them to speak of a change of standpoint, indeed a radical conversion, whereas, as Henry will show, the philosophy of the three lives represents no truly absolute change, nor even, in truth, an evolution. The problem of passivity had sooner or later to find itself on the path of a thought that seems at first to concentrate solely on the experience of motor activity. Before broaching that question, Henry deepens the close solidarity between the limitation of the *cogito* to activity and the absence of any satisfying interpretation of passivity.

Consciousness being identified with action, the experience of passivity must be explained from a principle exterior to that consciousness. Doubtless to say the ego is passive is to say it finds itself before a radically different reality, a foreign being of which it has experience. But it is one thing to describe phenomenologically this experience as lived by the ego within the transcendental relation of being-in-the-world, and quite another to claim to explain the *Erlebnis* as the effect of a third-person causal process acting on consciousness and as it were behind it. If consciousness is in its very essence an action deploying itself, sensing, undergoing, and being-affected cannot belong to that essence as such. Impression seems incompatible with an ontological structure exhausted in motor activity, that is, with the ego as Biran conceives it. Yet this impression belongs to the concrete daily experience of such an ego. It must be understandable from that ego's structure and appear as one of its ownmost ontological possibilities. Precisely because it is a fact of intimate sense, *the relation of the ego to its impressions constitutes a problem Biranism could not wholly elude*.

Biran first tries to build a theory of the pure impression, following the way opened by Buffon, Condillac, and Leibniz. He conceives a "simple affection without personality," an elementary mode lying outside the limits of the apperceptive faculty characterizing the conscious subject. Such is the smell of the rose the statue becomes, such generally are the "impressions or modifications of our sensibility." The ontological obscurity of the pure impression does not escape Biran, who perhaps confuses it, it is true, with a psychological obscurity: these last limits of sensibility present themselves under hypothetical, doubtless very obscure forms.

Graver uncertainties appear over the relation of this impression to the ego. On one hand Biran affirms the radical separation of these two elements, since the ego is defined by apperception of which the impression is wholly devoid; hence the impression is declared deprived of personality: there is a class of sensible impressions or internal affections from which individual personality and all its inherent forms are excluded. On the other hand one must come to envisage the relation of these impressions with the ego: by "impression" or "affection" Biran does not here mean a physiological modification, a blind process totally heterogeneous to subjective existence. Such a process can doubtless be considered in itself, like any natural event, abstracting from all relation to consciousness. But it is by a genuine and consequently illegitimate abstraction that the impression would be isolated from psychological life, of which it constitutes rather, on Biran's view, the first degree: it is an impression *for the ego*, a modification it *becomes* even if it does not apperceive it; it is the ego that experiences this modification, undergoes it, is affected by it. The impression therefore cannot be separated from an ontological power of the ego that is precisely the power of maintaining a relation with it. Whether one speaks of a "simple passive capacity" of the subject receiving sensations or of "a general capacity of sensing," can the sentient being bearing in it such an ontological power, on which sensible experience in general is founded, be distinguished from the ego itself? *Is this general capacity of sensing anything other than subjectivity?* The question is Henry's wedge and it is opened with Biran's own vocabulary: a capacity to be affected is already a power of the ego, hence already subjectivity.

Doubtless Biran would refuse such an assimilation: a text of the *Essai* explicitly opposes the sentient being and the individual person. It is on this opposition that the dualism of active and passive life rests. Impressions, affection, imagination belong to an autonomous region foreign to the self identified with active willing. But what is the ontological status of this region? Speaking of images, Biran says they are given us in a vision he calls passive because it is absolutely foreign to the activity of willing or of the self.

Can the theory of this passive vision, however, be indefinitely eluded? This is why, after affirming that the impression is without relation to the self, Biran comes to pose the problem of that relation, to say that the self *unites* with its impressions. Many hesitations appear: the *Essai* declares that if the self is the real subject of all the passive modes to which it unites, there nonetheless exists a multitude of obscure affections or perceptions with which it does not unite essentially. These uncertainties, far from permitting the maintenance of the anti-Kantian thesis of a sensibility without form because independent of the ego, betray rather, along with Biran's embarrassment, the orientation of his thought toward a theory of passivity that would call into question the signification assigned to the distinction of action and sensibility, and consequently the assimilation of that distinction to the distinction of self and non-self. The reading strategy is worth marking: Henry treats Biran's hesitations as evidence of a suppressed better position, not as mere inconsistency.

Looking closely at the Biranian critique of sensualism, one perceives that it does not perhaps confine itself to opposing something else — active life in general — to the sensibility of the ideologues; the very conception of that sensibility is called into question when the *Essai* declares that the statue could not become the smell of a rose, and generally could not undergo any modification whatever, *if it were not already something within itself*. This something is for Biran still only life in general, a vague and confused feeling of existence. But how not to see that this life posited as the condition of every particular modification or affection in fact plays the role of an *ontological foundation*; that the affective tonality Biran recognizes in it is likewise an ontological character of that foundation, insofar as it constitutes the foundation of all the particular determinations of our affectivity? How, finally, not to interpret the interiority that, still on Biran's account, characterizes the statue insofar as it must be able to be affected, as an *ontological* interiority, an original presence to self to which alone something can be present? This is the chapter's decisive textual find and the pivot of the whole reconstruction: Biran's own condition for being affected — being already something within oneself — is immanence, and it applies to sensibility as much as to effort.

In other terms: passive life is not deprived of intentionality; intentionality is not reserved to the properly active modes of willing and effort but intervenes also, as *passive synthesis*, in the determinations of the ego's life described as affectivity, sensibility, imagination. By a kind of necessity stronger than the arbitrary limitation of the ego's being to that of active life, Biranism had — at the very level of the *Essai* and the philosophy of motor effort — to recognize, or at least to sense, this presence of intentionality within passive life itself. Henry's importation of the Husserlian term is deliberate: what Biran lacked a name for is what genetic phenomenology calls passive synthesis, and the affinity is structural rather than verbal.

Passive life thereupon comprises a pure, genuinely transcendental element making it a modality of the life of absolute subjectivity, a passive operation accomplished in a sphere of radical immanence and letting us say that *to sense is still to think*. It is this element that truly makes affectivity or sensibility a life, that is, a capacity of reproduction not of the empirical matter but of its transcendental apprehension. There is, says Biran, a non-affective part properly characterizing such an odor or savor as distinct from any other, and serving thereafter as foundation for the reminiscence or memory attached to that particular sensation. Doubtless this pure part, the intentionality constituting the properly ontological element of the act of sensing, is described here as *non-affective*: the Biranism of 1812 has not yet risen to a transcendental theory of affectivity. At least *passivity as such is understood as an act* at the very moment a positive theory of sensing is sketched.

Studying the passage from intuition to image, which he still conceives as its prolongation (due to a "vibratory property" of the organs), Biran says: it suffices that the self was present at the first representative sensation, *even without expressly participating in it by its activity*, for personal reminiscence, which is the consciousness of the past self, to be found again in the image that sensation leaves behind. What then is a presence of the self without express activity, if not a passive synthesis?

Is the recognition of the ontological character of such a synthesis — the character making it a power and not an act individuated in time — not implied in another text concerning the subjective apprehension of an odor? If the odor came alone to impress the organ, *without being bound to any feeling of the action necessary to produce it*, the being that became it at each renewal would have no means of recognizing it as the same, or what amounts to the same, of recognizing in it the identity of its own sentient virtue. A few lines later Biran speaks of the "feeling of the self" or the "personal element" *enclosed in the first sensation*.

The act constituting the essence of sensing is first defined as an activity properly speaking, which could very well be, and sometimes effectively is, a voluntary activity. Biran calls it "feeling of the action." But the odor can affect a subject who does not expect to perceive it and has not decided to go toward it by an explicit effort of inhalation. What then makes possible the perception of the odor and, later, its recognition is the *identity of the sentient virtue* of the subject. This is nothing unconscious; like voluntary motor action it is a feeling, or better, a transcendental inner experience — and for precisely this reason sensing is something *for us*. Between the deliberate effort of inhalation and the involuntary subjective apprehension of an odor there is no essential ontological difference: *activity and passivity are rather two different modalities of one and the same fundamental power, which is nothing but the original being of the subjective body*. This is the chapter's positive thesis and the correction it makes to Biran; note that it does not abolish the difference between acting and undergoing but relocates it from the ontological to the existential register.

Did Biran really advance to this conception of a foundation deeper than the existential difference separating active and passive modes in the concrete life of the ego? Did he attain an authentic ontological comprehension of that foundation, its identification with the essence of life, with the very structure of the ego's being, which could then no longer be limited to the active modes of willing? A text of the *Mémoire sur la décomposition* declares that the feeling of causality, identified with that of personality, associates itself in various manners with the different impressions, *either by a relation of derivation if these impressions issue from the will, or by a simple relation of coexistence or simultaneity if they are passive by nature*. It is on this text of exceptional importance (and others like it) that the interpretation Henry gave of the decomposition of the faculty of sensing rests — a decomposition that is an essential piece of the theory of the body. Thus is justified the project of *expounding the ontological theory of the body without taking account of the existential distinction of activity and passivity*. The retrospective justification matters: Chapters II and III bracketed passivity throughout, and this text is the warrant for having done so.

Understanding the validity of such a project is at the same time a deepened understanding of the dogmatic content of that theory: our body is an act, but often an act that does not act; our body is essentially movement, but it is equally a question of an *immobile movement*. The common root of our acting and our sensing is a deeper power founding both: it is *habit*, on which rests the unity of our corporeal life through all the modalities in which it deploys; it is the original being of the body, finally, that is, the ego. What this common root is more precisely, only a theory of original ontological passivity would let us understand. But what we can understand already is the necessity of the existence of a principle founding the unity of our corporeal life, a unity of which this life is the very experience.

Henry then shows briefly, from one of Biran's most remarkable analyses, how the unity of movement and sensing, and hence the identity of a common ontological foundation, are required by the development of the philosophy of the body. Consider the union of the sense of hearing with the voice. When I hear a sound emanating from an exterior object, it is by the operation of a *passive synthesis* that this sound is apprehended by the ego — in Biranian terms, the feeling of causality or personality has associated itself by a simple relation of coexistence with the passive sonorous impression. When I hear a sound I have voluntarily produced by speech, the corresponding transcendental inner experience doubles: it is the experience of the act of uttering the word and the subjective, passive apprehension of the uttered sound. The sonorous impression, as transcendent element, sustains a double relation with the ego: a relation of derivation (as word uttered) and a simple relation of coexistence (as word heard). What is in question is the ontological homogeneity of the act of uttering and the act of hearing, that is, *the unity of the original being of our body*.

Henry has shown that this homogeneity and unity reside in the subjective nature of the two acts. For confirmation one need only reflect on the problem arising when I voluntarily reproduce a sound I primitively heard in a passive synthesis. How is it that I can reproduce such a sound voluntarily? There exists a knowing that does not merely coexist with my first passive apprehension of the sonorous impression but *coincides* with that apprehension: insofar as it is subjective, insofar as it is a transcendental inner experience, it is precisely an original knowing of this sort; and it is because I am in possession of such a knowing that I can both recognize this impression when it recurs and reproduce it myself voluntarily when I wish. This voluntary reproduction is possible only because it is the *motor modification* of an intentionality of which the ego is already the possessor, within the ontological phenomenon of habit. Henry says modification and not, for example, motor actualization, to signify clearly that intentionality is not absent from the first experience of passive apprehension but rather constitutes the essence of hearing exactly as it constitutes the essence of speech. Doubtless speaking is not hearing, but the difference separating these two phenomena is an *existential* difference, not an ontological one. The choice of word — modification, not actualization — is load-bearing: actualization would make the passive experience a mere potentiality, which is what the chapter denies.

Speaking and hearing are two *Erlebnisse*; the milieu where they are accomplished, where the two phenomena of activity and passivity in general are accomplished, is ontologically the same: that of absolute subjectivity. Its ontological nature makes the unity of this milieu nothing other than the unity of a *knowing*. It is precisely because the unity of a knowing underlies the lived experiences of hearing and speech that a passage is possible from one to the other, that we can repeat what we have heard and hear what we say. To say the ontological unity is that of a knowing is to refuse to situate this unity in a beyond or a hither side of the phenomena; it is to render it possible as an *effective* unity. The argument is a genuine phenomenological proof rather than an assertion: the everyday fact that we can repeat what we hear requires that hearing already be the kind of thing speaking is.

But this is to say that ontological unity is also in a certain manner an existential unity, that despite the differences separating our experiences as to their transcendental phenomenological content, that content is nonetheless the same in many respects. This is in truth the mystery of the unity of the ego, of its phenomenological unity across the diversity of its multiple experiences. This mystery is not, however, a beyond of our reason; or rather it is only in reason's eyes that it can appear an incomprehensible fact. For the ego itself the mystery vanishes, since it is its immediate life — not precisely a mystery but an absolute transparency. If the ontological phenomenon of habit grows troubled before the exterior regard, that is because one cannot understand life while misrecognizing its fundamental ontological character, that by which it is defined as a milieu of radical immanence.

Biran doubtless rose to such a conception of the original being of our body when he speaks of an *immanent state of effort* and founds on it the unity of our self. It is no longer a question of a determinate effort presiding over the accomplishment of this or that movement or the active exercise of this or that sense, but of a kind of *latent tension* with which the very being of our absolute body coincides and which holds in the unity of its own life our organic body and perhaps the effectivity of all presence to the world in general. This latent tension makes the essence of sensing and of the power-to-be-affected as much as that of motor effort; it is the proper inner quivering of knowing insofar as that knowing is a life and not a dead knowing. It defines the state of waking, that is, for Biran, the actuality and reality of experience; and at this level, which is truly that of an origin, there is not yet room for a distinction, still less an opposition, of activity and passivity. The "latent tension" recovers the description from Chapter IV of the body at rest as held out of nothingness, and now names what is common to acting and undergoing.

In going back to a common root of activity and passivity, in affirming their homogeneity within one ontological status, do we not suppress the very manifest difference separating these two modes of our life, or at least minimize the essential opposition Biranism seems in many respects to establish between them? In fact, *the ontological homogeneity of activity and passivity is the only possible foundation of their distinction*. Insofar as Biranism does not recognize such homogeneity, affirming as it does explicitly in the *Mémoire* that the active modes alone are homogeneous among themselves, it must be incapable of assigning a foundation to the difference instituted between these and the other determinations of our experience.

Comparing first the Biranian distinction of activity and passivity with the same distinction as Condillac conceived it, one easily measures the progress accomplished: it was from a purely exterior standpoint that the author of the *Treatise on Sensations* considered the opposition. The statue, he said, is active when the cause modifying it is in it, passive when that cause is exterior. But Biran understands the necessity of placing oneself *within* the statue, in a sphere of absolute immanence, in order to give a phenomenological and hence real signification to a distinction the individual can make because it is established *within* him. It is because it has the transcendental inner experience of its activity that the self recognizes it as a state distinct from the one that is its own when this activity is not exercised.

What happens, however, in this latter case? Is there simply privation and absence of the feeling of action? But then there would be *nothing*, if it is true, as Biran so often affirms, that the being of the ego is identified with that feeling of action. We would not have to oppose an active and a passive state of the subject; the latter would exist in one case as active subject, but in the other it would no longer exist, the experience of passivity would be refused it, and no comparison between these active and passive modalities of its life would any longer be possible for it. It is therefore only if an experience of passivity is *originally given* to the ego that we are in the presence, in this case of passive life, of a phenomenological content that can then, but only then, be compared and opposed to another equally original content, that is, to another mode of the ego's absolute life. If, from a strictly Biranian standpoint, it is not possible for the ego to establish an opposition or even a comparison between its activity and its passivity, that is because in reality one of the two terms is lacking, because too often the conditions necessary for the existence of an effective experience of passivity are not recognized. This is the strongest form of Henry's argument and its logic should be registered: a merely privative passivity could not be experienced, hence could not be compared with activity, hence the very distinction Biran wants would be unavailable to him.

These conditions can consist only in the admission of a *passive intentionality*, a passive synthesis, not as hypothetical explanatory principle but as real phenomenological experience given to the ego in a sphere of absolute immanence. Because our experience of passivity is indeed an original experience of this sort, we do not need, in order to recognize and define it, to oppose it to the active modes of our existence; in itself precisely, abstracting from all context and contrast, this experience lived itself as passive, its truth did not have to be discovered elsewhere — it was already its certainty.

The absence of a positive ontological theory of passivity places Biran before other difficulties. If subjectivity is effectively present only when it determines itself to act according to a specific motor intentionality, then each time such an intentionality fades or is interrupted, this subjectivity must cease to be the very real experience with which our existence coincides; it must cease to be the experience of an existence — it is no longer anything but nothingness. The embarrassment of every philosophy claiming to make subjectivity something other than what it is first of all, namely the original experience of its own life, Biran knew each time he had to circumscribe the nature of the determinations of that subjectivity's life which were not motor determinations. The uncertainties of his analysis of sight are particularly revealing.

So long as it is a matter of detecting within our power of vision the immanence of a motor effort orienting the gaze and choosing the visual sensations it gives itself, Biranism moves in a familiar domain. But *seeing is not always looking*. There is a passive vision from which all motor effort is absent, identifying rather with that latent tension constituting at once the essence of every passive synthesis and the original form of the life of our body. This passive synthesis is nonetheless a determination of subjectivity and consequently an experience. Biran is too rigorous a psychologist to misrecognize the phenomenon of passive vision, but when the moment comes to oppose it to active vision and the deliberate gaze, he can do so only in these terms: the distinction between simple seeing and looking is founded entirely on the *relative absence* or the immediate presence of the will. What must one understand by this "relative absence" of will, if not the mode of existence that is subjectivity's in passive vision — and as such a *positive* mode? How account in Biranism for the positivity of such a mode? The phrase "relative absence" is a symptom Henry reads well: a purely privative account would have said absence *tout court*.

The insufficiencies of the Biranian theory of vision have a curious effect. It is to the sense of sight that Biran attaches all the philosophical errors of empiricist tendency, skeptical idealism as well as the doctrine of transformed sensation. It is because the philosophers who upheld these doctrines reasoned as an intelligence reduced to the sense of sight might do that experience dissolved in their eyes into imaginary compositions, phantoms, transitory modes where all is accident and nothing is substance. But does this dissolution of experience, so well described by Biran, really stem from the philosopher's taking into consideration the sense of sight alone, to the exclusion of every other? Henry notes in a footnote that if this conception of the sense of sight were correct, no error could result from it. Does the dissolution not rather threaten every philosophy that lets inner experience fly off toward the transcendent flux of its representations because, lacking an ontological theory of passivity, it has previously made subjectivity vanish into nothingness, so that nothing thenceforth retains it in the region where the effectivity of its life is nonetheless accomplished, in a sphere of absolute immanence? The correction is important: the empiricist dissolution of experience is not a fault of the visual model but of the missing theory of passivity, and Biran's own theory shares the lack.

The insufficiency of the Biranian theory of passivity rebounds on the conception of *activity* itself, with the gravest consequences. As soon as a philosophy has let hover over it the threat of an eventual identification of subjectivity and nothingness, it becomes the prey of an interior dialectic whose destructive power is not easy to arrest. Consider active vision: it depends on attention, an eminently active mode of the ego's life. But the motor effort directing the gaze in the desired direction ceases well before our contemplation of the object ends. And as soon as our attention no longer coincides with an explicit motor effort, Biran is incapable of preserving its proper ontological character, its subjective and immanent character. This is abolished when attention is represented as losing itself in the elements toward which it surpasses itself, as being absorbed in these modes. *The agent that represents*, says Biran, *disappears or hides beneath the thing represented*. Where does this absorption of subjectivity into the object lead — this disappearance of the element of its own life to the profit of a transcendent being that intentionality can nonetheless aim at and reach only on condition of continuing to be such a subjective aim — if not to a destruction of the very concept of subjectivity in its proper ontological signification?

The ontological signification of the concept of subjectivity finds its expression, as we have seen, in the affirmation that subjectivity is *reflection*. The moment this signification is misrecognized by Biran will be the one where he declares that attention is not a reflection: that voluntary power we call attention, and which is in no way commanded by the vivacity of the modes although it attaches solely to them, *without reflecting itself within*. On the contrary, it is because each mode of the life of subjectivity belongs to it and participates in its radical interiority that it is possible for us to form an idea corresponding to that mode and generally to give sense to "reflected abstractions," to "simple ideas of reflection." Because he misrecognizes the subjective nature of attention, Biran comes naturally to say of even active vision: there are, in the modes relating specially to the exercise of sight, neither reflected abstractions nor simple ideas of reflection. Henry calls this manifestly false — how could we speak of vision, how could we know what we mean by it, if it were not precisely, as transcendental inner experience, the content of our idea of vision and of the different modifications of its exercise? — and it leads Biran to add lines that shake the philosophy of subjectivity to its foundations, at the price of an ontological absurdity: it is here that all the faculties and operations of the perceiving being can be characterized and judged *from outside*, for they are, for the subject itself, only what they appear to the spectator, and the two standpoints coincide. The collapse is complete and the sentence is the precise negation of everything Chapters I–IV established: the first-person and third-person standpoints are declared to coincide.

The concepts philosophical thought employs are closely bound. From the moment the ontological structure of attention has been misrecognized, the reflection one must then oppose to it in order to recover the essence of subjectivity just lost sees its signification falsified: it no longer expresses the essence of subjectivity but a particular determination of it. The term "reflection" then receives in Biranism a *new* acceptation: it no longer designates immediate subjective experience, nor a return of consciousness on itself in the classical sense, but a kind of *recovery by sense of an originally motor activity*. Registering this as a third sense of "reflection" completes the terminological warning issued in Chapter I, § 1, where Henry noted Biran used the word in three ways.

This signification is heard clearly from the analysis of the relations of hearing and voice. Reflection was primitively the transcendental inner experience of effort, but this consciousness of effort, according to Biran, *envelops itself in the passive affections with which it is united from the origin*. Attention is thus absorbed in the thing, the consciousness of effort vanishes into the passive impression and disappears in it, subjectivity is no longer anything but nothingness. What then, Biran asks, can restore to us the distinct feeling of our effort? To prevent this absorption of subjectivity, which would issue in its destruction, the *being-there* would have to manifest in it a character by which it would give itself as emanating from an effort of the self, that is, for Biran, as bound to the consciousness and hence to the real existence of that self. Only then would this consciousness and existence be conserved within the apprehension of the being-there, and consequently attention would still be a reflection.

Now the being-there will manifest this character of being a product of our effort when it effectively is such. Thenceforth its simple perception (a perception which, as attention, would signify the self's own dissolution in the object) will not forget in its operation the being of the ego accomplishing it, because the mode to which it attaches — the being-there considered — bears in it the imprint of that ego, the mark of an effort of which it gives itself as the product. These conditions are naturally fulfilled in the union of the sense of hearing with the voice. When the auditory impression is the word just uttered, it is perceived precisely as the product of the ego's effort, an effort conscious of itself in the transcendental experience of the act of speaking. This impression can therefore no longer be the being-there where the self founders in the self-forgetting of attention; it is rather what *reflects it to itself*, since it is its own word in the world. In the perception of a sonorous-impression-produced-by-it, the ego will no longer lose itself as in ordinary attention but will truly find itself again. It is the whole phenomenon — the relation of speech and hearing bound by the mediation of an impression perceived by the second as produced by the first — that must be present to mind to understand this third, specifically Biranian signification of "reflection." Henry has reconstructed the doctrine with real sympathy before rejecting it, and the reconstruction shows exactly what work the voice was being asked to do: to rescue an ego that the theory of attention had allowed to dissolve.

Such a signification, however, can only be secondary and derived, for *subjectivity needs no mediation to experience its own life*. It is rather, by itself, such an experience; the simple hearing of any sonorous impression is a real moment of our life, on the same footing as the perception of sounds emitted by us or the effort of voluntarily producing them. One must therefore say against Biran that simple "attention" is already a "reflection," that it already bears in it the depth of subjectivity. Doubtless the phenomenon in which I voluntarily impress myself is in many respects privileged: *here is the animate harp that plucks itself*. Yet the difference between the intentionality in which I am impressed by a sensible element I otherwise know emanates from my will, and the one in which I am impressed independently of my willing, is not an ontological difference but a difference between two modes of existence. One cannot rest the consciousness of the self, that is, the real existence of the ego, on a particular intentionality without meeting the insurmountable difficulty of knowing what becomes of such an ego when that intentionality is no longer realized — what becomes of it, for example, when it hears not the noise of its own words but a symphony or any sounds from the exterior world. Henry adds a note that one may give the phenomenon of interior speech a very great extension, even see in it an ontological condition of hearing in general — a thesis not Biran's, which would only confirm his own analyses of the ontological homogeneity of the acts of speaking and hearing. The symphony objection is decisive and simple: on Biran's account, listening to music would leave the ego without existence.

*It is always from itself that the ego knows itself.* It is not an impression, however privileged, that can bring it the revelation of its being, for such a revelation is always original and can be accomplished only within a sphere of absolute immanence. The privileged character of the impression considered — this sound that is my word — itself rests in reality on that original revelation of the ego, whether accomplished within the motor effort of speaking or the passive synthesis of hearing. To be itself, the ego has no need to find its shadow in the world; and if it happens to encounter it, this perhaps privileged experience nonetheless leans on the general conditions of experience and in truth on its fundamental ontological condition, which is precisely the phenomenon of the ego itself, its original mode of revelation.

The absence of a positive theory of passivity entails further difficulties. The admirable phenomenology of memory is altered as soon as it is a question of the memory of the passive modes of our existence. Memory rests on the ontological structure of the ego's being, on the unity that is its own insofar as it is an original ontological power. Wherever there is a self there is habit and consequently the ontological possibility of effecting acts of remembering. But where the ego is absent there can be neither habit nor memory. If there exists an "affection without self," a pure affection as Biran also says, it is by principle removed from the conditions making memory possible. All our affective life, but also all our sensible life and all our imaginative life, must then escape — and in fact do escape, by Biran's express declarations — all possible repetition as well as simple recollection.

The ego certainly does not have the power to repeat its sensations and affections if one means a repetition of the transcendent element, the sensation or affection in its very materiality. Henry adds in a note that as regards images such a power evidently does belong to it. But the intentionality that in the synthesis of passive apprehension had risen toward such an element can perfectly well *reproduce itself*, and this is why determinations of our power of sensing and affective attitudes can be reborn in us. Consequently we can form sensible and affective memories bound to these determinations and attitudes according to a relation of foundation that is a particular case of the general relation uniting habit and memory. These modalities of the life of absolute subjectivity are very evident and very clear by themselves. To contest, for example, the role of memory in our affective life is to deny the most manifest experience; and when Biran declares that children have no memory, or generally that affective life is not susceptible of repetition, his thesis manifestly runs counter to the deepest laws of the human psyche — laws contemporary psychology had necessarily to bring to light, however improper, in a Freud for example, are the vocabulary and concepts that served to recognize and express the permanence of the deepest intentionalities of our affective and sensible life. Note the careful concession: Henry has elsewhere placed Freud in the lineage of the philosophies of the unconscious, and grants the finding while refusing the framework. Considering various psychological problems — for example madness, which Biran is incapable of characterizing positively and distinguishing from the imaginative life of a normal man — would reveal other insufficiencies likewise stemming from the absence of a positive theory of passivity.

The problem of the relations of activity and passivity is posed anew, in seemingly very different terms, in Biran's last philosophy. The doctrine of the three lives makes appear, above the sphere where subjective movement deploys, a superior zone of existence where experiences of a new type are accomplished, privileged experiences whose elucidation issues in a psychology of grace. This new region seems characterized by a passivity with respect to a transcendent principle the ego welcomes into itself and experiences in a "sublime passion." The passivity at issue must not be confused with the one making the essence of organic or animal life. Whereas the latter is the psychological translation of the union of soul with body, that is, with an inferior principle, the passivity whose experience coincides with grace expresses doubtless the intervention in thought of a heterogeneous reality — but this time a *superior* reality, no longer the body but divine life itself. More: the experience of passivity in which the soul feels the presence of grace as a kind of "addition to its own life" is possible only when the first form of passivity has vanished, that is, when the soul has freed itself from union with the body to become available to the influence of transcendent life.

Yet if Biranism is essentially a philosophy of motor effort, if it defines the self from conscious and voluntary activity, how understand the intervention of the doctrine of the third life — a life that is no longer an activity where the ego experiences its own autonomy and a personal power, but a passion where it is delivered over and abandons itself to a foreign force? On the terms of the first Biranism, should passivity not signify for the ego not an increase of its own life but rather the destruction of its being, if it is true that this being begins and ends with motor effort? How accept, in a philosophy of the "I can" and of immanence, the irruption of a transcendent absolute and the dissolution of all personal reality in such an absolute: *how reconcile that with my psychological doctrine of the self?* This celebrated question, often cited, seems to have embarrassed the commentators as much as Biran himself. The solution — which is rather an absence of solution — consists in juxtaposing to the first two forms of animal and motor life a third life in which man abandons himself passively to the influence of grace. The analysis of a certain primitive fact would have led Biran to elaborate a philosophy of effort and will; the discovery of a "new primitive fact" leads him to revise this philosophy or rather to adjoin to it a new piece constituted by the psychology of grace. The first primitive fact was that of the union of soul and body; the second expresses the experience of their separation, the liberation of the soul restored to the absolute life of spirit.

How, however, are these two successively discovered facts reconciled? Must Biranism meet the difficulties inevitably arising when one confronts the power of the self's will with that of a grace or a malediction emanating from another source? Must it know the bitter destiny of foundering in the interminable and sterile discussions resulting from the juxtaposition of two different principles placed at the origin of our being? Does its originality exhaust itself in the resurrection of Orphic images and Greek conceptions of a soul first mired in a body and finally freed from its constraint? And first, is there any philosophical sense in speaking of the existence of *two primitive facts*? Is what is primitive not what renders possible? How would what renders possible need, from an ontological standpoint, something else in order to exist? The second primitive fact could therefore only be *contingent* relative to the system of existence resting on the first. How could something contingent be regarded as fundamental from the ontological standpoint? The argument is clean and is not merely verbal: "primitive" is a functional term, and two mutually independent primitives cannot both discharge the function.

That there exist three lives is the *letter* of Biranism. Are we truly incapable today of recovering its *spirit* — of understanding that there are not three lives but one; that the opposition of animal and motor life is foreign to the fundamental intuition of Biranism; that the ontological structure that intuition reaches is immanent to our sensible life, whose essence it constitutes as much as that of subjective movement; that the philosophy of effort has nothing to do with any union of soul and body as one can understand it in the perspective of traditional dualism; and that there is finally no opposition between motor activity first recognized as constitutive of the ego's being and the experience of an absolute life, immanent in fact *to that activity as much as to any other existential determination of subjectivity*? The object of Biran's last philosophy is the same as the one the ontological theory of the body furnished the first elucidation of. That object is the ontological structure of absolute subjectivity. To understand Biranism would perhaps be to understand how the progress of the elucidation of such a structure issues in bringing to light an *original ontological passivity* which as such should be confused neither with the passive modes of sensible life (certain *Erlebnisse* opposed to the active modes of willing), nor with that quasi-ontological but incomprehensible and inadmissible passivity by which dualist theory wants to account for the existence of passive *Erlebnisse* and confused thought, nor finally, perhaps, with other equally passive but privileged *Erlebnisse*, those in which the subject experiences grace. This three-way distinction is the chapter's terminal result and names what Henry's own later work will pursue: a passivity belonging to the structure of self-revelation as such, not to any class of experiences.

Is it not to these last, however, that the philosophy of the third life explicitly refers? Yet if the third life is immanent to life in general, if it is present in all its forms even the humblest, is the passivity in question not more than an existential character proper to certain determinate *Erlebnisse* — is there not ground to speak of an *ontological passivity* whose structure must be reached not by the mediation of hypothetical and transcendent constructions and deductions, but as a real condition implied in the phenomenon of the original revelation constituting the very being of the ego? This is the book's deepest question and Henry leaves it open here; the ego's being given to itself without having produced itself is the passivity at the heart of auto-affection.

The dogmatic exposition of the ontological theory of the body was made from the theses of the *Mémoire sur la décomposition de la pensée* and the *Essai sur les fondements de la psychologie*. It is in these two texts that the theory of the body receives full development and that the intuition forming its center is most easily detectable, despite heterogeneous elements already threatening to cover it. In the immediately following works these parasitic elements extend dangerously, at times calling into question the true foundation of Biranian philosophy, the phenomenological footing of the ontology it builds. A text of the *Essai* already opposed to the phenomenal standpoint a noumenal standpoint whose legitimacy Biran curiously recognized at the very moment he explained that it is only within such a standpoint that the relation of soul and body becomes unintelligible. Subsequently the value of the noumenal standpoint and the philosophical necessity of admitting it are affirmed more explicitly and insistently, so that the whole ontology of subjectivity seems called into question.

Significant in this regard is the modification of the doctrine of principles in the *Rapports des sciences naturelles avec la psychologie*. The principles are no longer founded on the primitive fact of intimate sense. As foundation and origin of all our principles, that fact played in the *Essai* the role of an absolute. But the theory of belief casts the absolute outside the sphere of transcendental inner experience; the principles are no longer the simple translation of it but answer internal requirements of thought, which is led to formulate judgments positing the existence of these principles, thenceforth envisaged as conditioning it. The permanence of the self, for example, becomes a *principle*: it is no longer an ontological determination whose affirmation rests wholly on the phenomenological content of transcendental experience, but a simple affirmation of reason constrained by logical necessity — "to think one must be" — an affirmation to which something must correspond somewhere. Transcendent noumena again cast their shadow over the philosophy of subjectivity. The theory of the ego is gravely compromised, because the ego becomes precisely a noumenon, a hypothetical term situated beyond the phenomenon, that is, beyond immanent experience.

Thenceforth the being of the ego no longer coincides with that of subjectivity. Under the influence of Kant, or rather of his Parisian friends, Biran confesses to having confused the intimate sense of our individuality with the very depth of the substance of the soul. The latter must remain inaccessible, escaping the grasp of a thinking subject reduced to the knowledge of "phenomena." Or, if we are permitted to acquire some idea of this substance, our absolute being, that can be done only by turning away from the sphere of phenomena and effecting a passage toward something else, a passage the new theory of principles is charged with rendering intelligible, indeed necessary. But if by this bias the absolute regains citizenship, it is only through the mediation of a theory of belief which doubtless seeks to overcome Kantian agnosticism but cannot, precisely because it develops within the same philosophical horizon, except at the price of byzantine distinctions (among effort, force, substance, substantial force) and an indefinite series of reasonings that can only ever issue in a hypothetical object, an object of belief. The separation between felt force and substantial force will never be lifted so long as one distinguishes between the absolute and the phenomenon and feels the need to transcend the latter toward a reality of another order.

One must guard, however, against giving too much importance to the theses immediately following those of the *Essai*: taking them literally would perhaps lead to missing the essential of Biranian thought. What appears in these texts is a *devaluation of the concept of phenomenon*. Its signification is nonetheless the inverse of what it appears at first: far from leading to an abandonment of phenomenological perspectives, it leads back to them, after a detour that finally deepens the idea of a phenomenological foundation of ontology. To understand this devaluation one must recall the signification of the concept in the French philosophical context of the early nineteenth century. By "phenomenon" Biran's contemporaries in no way understood some phenomenological absolute; integrating that concept into an ontology of subjectivity was impossible for the reason that the latter was not yet born — it was reserved to Biran to build it. "Phenomenon" could then designate only a *natural* phenomenon, with which ideology, sensualism, and empiricism were led to identify, as to its deep ontological status, the interior phenomenon or psychic fact. The term therefore has an essentially sensualist and empiricist signification, *and it is by reaction against such a signification that Biran feels the need to appeal to the idea of the absolute*. The intervention of the idea of the absolute in Biranian philosophy has the same signification as the affirmation of the existence of non-empirical phenomena and of a *sui generis* region of being where such phenomena are originally revealed. This is the chapter's most valuable act of historical interpretation and it rescues the late work: Biran's turn to the absolute is a defense of transcendental phenomena against a vocabulary that had no word for them.

This signification, reducing to the opposition of the empirical phenomenon and a *transcendental* phenomenon, of natural science and psychology as pure psychology — an opposition dominating all Biran's writings — remains masked and partly falsified by the historical circumstances surrounding "the irruption of the absolute in 1813" in Biranism. His uncertain knowledge of Kantianism, conversations with Royer-Collard, Ampère and above all Degerando, the fear of appearing a materialist and sensualist philosopher in the latter's eyes and generally in the eyes of the metaphysicians of the absolute who identify phenomenon with natural phenomenon — and for whom therefore *a phenomenology can signify only a return to empiricism* — led Biran to speak of noumena and of an absolute situated beyond our experience. It is then that the new philosophy of principles and the theory of belief appear. But let one reject the ontological monism that knows only transcendent phenomena, let the idea of a transcendental appearance arise — an immediate revelation of the being of the *Erlebnis* in the original sphere of immanence of subjectivity — and the doctrine of principles, like the theory of the noumenal self as simple object of belief, will appear only as a dead piece in the system; the distinction of felt force and substantial force will have no reason to be; and generally the opposition of a noumenal to a phenomenal standpoint will show us its true origin, referring us to the indigence and misery of a philosophy that can only founder in agnosticism when it is not in possession of the idea of an *absolute phenomenon*.

It is precisely on the recognition of the absolute value of the phenomenon that Biran's last philosophy rests, and this is why it is not different at bottom from that of the *Essai*. This recognition, placing phenomenology at the very foundation of ontology, does not follow only from the assuredly complex ensemble of theses appearing in the last writings; it is often explicit: *being and appearing coincide in the consciousness of the self*. The "absolute" no longer opposes the "relative" as the noumenon opposes the empirical phenomenon and the sensible given; rather the relative receives the signification Henry has given it throughout: it is nothing other than the absolute. One can say that the relative and the absolute coincide in the feeling of force or of free activity — that is, for Biran, in a sphere of experience where the real and absolute being of the ego is precisely revealed to us. This being, however, is not something that, through the mediation of the subjective experience of effort, would manifest itself adequately but as a reality situated beyond that experience; rather, as absolute being, it is nothing other than the very revelation in which it gives itself to us, an original revelation constituting precisely the ontological phenomenon of the ego. The continuity thesis is now established on Biran's own texts, and it disposes of the whole "conversions" literature: there is deepening, not evolution.

If it were otherwise, if the ego as real and absolute being were different from subjective experience, the self present in that experience and identical to its essence would be *separated from its true being*. The latter would doubtless be accessible to it in an adequate knowledge, similar for example to the one God can have of this true being or noumenal self; but the two knowledges, that of the concrete ego and that of God, would remain two different knowledges, and one would perhaps seek in vain what lets us affirm that one is similar to the other. Doubtless the texts where Biran relates self-knowledge and God's knowledge of the self often seem not to go beyond affirming their similitude. In an ontology of subjectivity, however, such an affirmation cannot remain exterior. To understand it is rather to have present to mind its ontological foundation, the internal structure of subjectivity. It is in self-knowledge that we must *read* that it is similar to the knowledge of the real being of the ego that is God's. Does it then suffice to say that this self-knowledge conforms to its object, the real being of the self? What does this conformity signify, and what must one understand by "object"?

In truth, subjectivity cannot be absolutely conformed to an object unless that object is nothing other than itself; its knowledge can be an absolute knowledge only if it is no longer a transcendent knowledge but an original revelation where there is in fact room for no adequation but only for the pure *unity with self of life* — a life not separated from itself and which, in this absence of all phenomenological distance, nonetheless knows itself because its being is nothing other than the experience it has of itself. The ontological structure of such a phenomenon, defining the very being of the ego, obliges us to reject the concept of the adequation of self-knowledge, and *a fortiori* the idea that this adequate knowledge would grasp adequately only a *part* of the ego's being, whose other aspects would be known to God alone. Such aspects would have nothing to do with the real being of this concrete ego, and one does not see at all what would prevent us from gratuitously attributing them to any other ego, to whose being their adjunction would add only the nothingness of unconscious transcendent noumena. The move that dissolves the problem is the same one used against the "two aspects of one thing" solution in Chapter IV: where there is no distance there is no adequation, because there are not two terms to be adequate.

If self-knowledge is an absolute knowledge, the problem certainly arises whether the idea of a knowledge God would have of the ego, alongside that ego's own immediate self-revelation, retains a sense. Would deepening this problem not lead rather to setting aside the idea of a genuine duality of the two knowledges, to the affirmation that resemblance presupposes in a certain way *identity*, and let us finally give a rigorous ontological interpretation of the *similitude* of God's knowledge and the ego's? If Biranism did not advance to this ultimate ontological interpretation, it at least affirmed the existence of such a similitude. A philosophy that rose to the concept of an absolute subjectivity thereby posited the foundation of the possibility of an absolute knowledge. It is the recognition, implicit or explicit, of such knowledge as a possibility proper to man's being that led Biran to the idea of a relation and perhaps a unity of self-knowledge and divine knowledge. Henry refers in a note to Eckhart's explicit theory of the identity of self-knowledge and divine knowledge, treated in *L'essence de la manifestation* — the reference that most clearly signals where his own thought is heading.

Because this idea finds its foundation in the ontological structure of absolute subjectivity, whose elucidation Biranism as a whole constitutes, it had necessarily to express itself in other forms: the rejection of fideism, the assignment of psychology as the foundation of theology, the phenomenological conception of grace are so many affirmations commenting on a single fundamental intuition and corresponding exactly to the critical directions already appearing in the *Essai* and the *Mémoire*, notably the general critique directed against the idea of a transcendent absolute. Here again there is no ground to speak of an evolution but only of a *deepening* of Biranian thought. Far from Biran's last philosophy calling into question the results of the ontological analysis of the body, it is rather its confirmation. So we shall find it natural and not astonishing that the incomprehension so often shown toward the philosophy of the last writings is *the same* as the one the central theses of the *Essai* met. In both cases it is the incomprehension of the Biranian ontology of subjectivity. When it is no longer understood within the philosophical horizon that alone preserves its authentic ontological signification, the psychology of grace can evidently no longer be anything but an empirical psychology. To confuse the life of spirit with the "psychological" determinations of the concrete subject is an insufficiency the true philosopher is willing to deplore. To found theology on such a psychology is a danger, not to say a sacrilege, the theologian cannot prevent himself from denouncing.

Henry closes with a long polemical note. Brunschvicg, showing an almost complete incomprehension of Biranian philosophy, reproaches it with having conceived the life of spirit in an empiricist, almost materialist manner; the reason for this insufficiency, in his eyes, is that Biran had not read Kant to the end. These somewhat presumptuous affirmations do not prevent Brunschvicg from declaring, in a letter to Politzer, that as for psychology he defers to Biran. Henry remarks that if Brunschvicg had formed a more exact idea of Biranian psychology, he would not have believed it compatible with the status his philosophy of spirit assigns to psychology. In an infinitely more comprehending manner, Gouhier reproaches Biran with having granted too much to the "impressions of grace," with having sought an experience where the transcendent would enjoy a sensible evidence, and with not having gone as far as an act of faith in a *non-felt presence*. Similarly Fessard speaks of a "transcendent empiricism" in Biran. The reality Biranism occupies itself with is in fact neither empirical nor transcendent: it is that of a *transcendental inner experience*, to which only an ontology of subjectivity can do justice.

One cannot understand, within the traditional philosophical horizon that grants psychology only an empirical status, a thought whose whole sense is to break with such a horizon, to shatter the frame in which psychology had been enclosed, and whose essential content consists in building an ontology of subjectivity within which the problem of psychology is posed in an entirely new manner — where, more precisely, the problem of the *foundation* of psychology perhaps becomes for the first time the theme of philosophical reflection. But the principle presiding over the elaboration of such an ontology, and furnishing us precisely the foundation of psychology, remains immanent to Biran's last philosophy: it is the idea of an absolute subjectivity, the central intuition of Biranism, its beginning and its term. The chapter thus ends where Chapter I began, and the critique proves to have been what Henry promised at the outset — an act of retrieval, since everything struck down was borrowed and everything that survives is the single intuition.

## Conclusion. The Ontological Theory of the Body and the Problem of Incarnation: Flesh and Spirit

The problem of the body occupies a central place in the preoccupations of a philosophy of existence. Can such a philosophy escape the reproach falling on nearly all theories and opinions concerning the body — that the ensemble of problems relating to corporeal life and the phenomenon of incarnation has never been brought to the clarity of the concept nor submitted to the jurisdiction of ontology? Outside such jurisdiction thought can only move in vague and uncertain representations, whatever the permanence of the experience to which these representations refer, and whatever the number or depth of the moral or religious conceptions proposing a knowledge of man and his destiny in terms of the role and status they assign to his body. Precisely because one has not first asked ontology to determine that status rigorously, one cannot form a precise notion of the value to accord interpretations that may issue from genuine experience but often stem from an insufficient idea of the body (for instance the idea of the objective body) and are consequently incapable of playing the almost always exclusive role assigned them.

As for the cultural or religious heritage humanity disposes of as a guide for practical life and as a positive contribution to its tireless effort to understand itself, its content — as complex as it is rich — is not the object of an objective discrimination resting on the real data of a positive science like ontology; it is purely and simply affirmed, denied, or interpreted by each according to a subjective preference that no longer even seeks a rational foundation. It happens moreover that all effort of interpretation ceases, so that, like the content of a letter passing from hand to hand unopened, the dogmatic contribution generations transmit is no longer, in the case most favorable to tradition — when tradition is still respected — anything but a dead element without relation to the life of concrete existences. This is how religions die: *at the same time as science*. For science, absolute science, that which rises on an apodictic foundation and whose body is constituted by the ensemble of propositions manifesting an eidetic necessity, alone lets us eventually give a sense to the content of dogmatics by comparing that content with its own results. *It is the life of science that gives its life to tradition*, if tradition is still capable of living. The unopened-letter image and the claim that religions die with science set the Conclusion's program: ontology is not to arbitrate between tradition and its critics but to supply the discrimination that makes either intelligible.

The return to the data of ontology is all the more indispensable when treating the body, since to its being are bound not only important speculative problems but a great number of questions belonging to the categories of existence and morality. In dogmatics or in the great metaphysical representations humanity gives itself, all these planes — theoretical, existential, moral — are inextricably bound. It matters to dissociate them, not to study practical and speculative problems separately, but to submit them first to a single fundamental discipline without which they cannot even be posed. Ontology alone can rid us of the vague notions obscuring theoretical consciousness and at the same time furnish ethics the bases on which it can constitute itself as an autonomous discipline. The ontological analysis of the body must constitute the first elucidation letting us judge tradition, determine in it the part of what is dead and what is living, and form for our own account an exact conception of the essence of the body and hence of the hierarchy of problems ordered from it.

The fundamental question at the origin of these researches is: must the ontological analysis of subjectivity be considered part of a problematic concerning the body? Can it prescribe to the body, for ontological reasons, a determinate status? To say it can is to affirm the identity of the being of subjectivity and that of the body. It is not a matter of reading in the eidetic structure of certain intentional acts the correlative and eidetically determined structure of their objects. Such a noetic-noematic correlation holds everywhere and always, for all objects. The thesis Henry affirms is more original and more particular: it concerns only that well-determined body I call mine, and consists not in reading the eidetic structure of this body in the correlative nature of the intentionalities that would constitute it but, on the contrary, in *denying* that it can be, in its original being at least, the product of such a constitution. Registering this is important because it separates Henry's thesis from a Husserlian one it might be mistaken for: the claim is not that the body has a distinctive constitutional style, but that it is not constituted.

If the study of subjectivity necessarily comprises that of the body as subjective body, it would be inexact to say conversely that the ontological elucidation of the original being of our body constitutes an exhaustive analysis of the sphere of absolute subjectivity — of the totality of intentional possibilities belonging to it. If we run through the ensemble of eidetically (not simultaneously) compossible intentionalities constituting, by that compossibility, the being of the absolute ego, we are sure to find among them the totality of intentionalities composing in their unity the original being of the subjective body: intentionalities we express by "see," "hear," "feel," "move oneself," "desire," and so on. It is evident, however, that corporeal *Erlebnisse* represent only a part of our possible *Erlebnisse* — the eidetic types answering to the body's intentionalities in no way exhaust the intentional eidetic types in general. These corporeal *Erlebnisse* are nonetheless *Erlebnisse*, and as established against Descartes their structure is that of absolute subjectivity without the adjunction of any heterogeneous element. Thenceforth the body — the absolute body — is in the strong sense a subjective body whose being reveals itself originally in a sphere of absolute immanence, and in such a manner that it coincides with that very revelation.

Henry now makes a series of concluding remarks, beginning with those concerning subjectivity itself, which will help set aside definitively every idealist interpretation of it. With the ontological theory of the subjective body, the concept of subjectivity acquires the reality it too often lacks. Having risen to a correct interpretation of its object, the philosophy of subjectivity can no longer be considered an abstract philosophy, an intellectualism. Subjectivity is not an impersonal milieu, a simple "transcendental" field dissolving, at the term of classical thought, into a pure mirage, an empty continuity, a representation deprived of all content. "Transcendental" designates not what subsists after this flight from reality — a pure nothing — but a perfectly determinate and absolutely concrete region of being. *What deserves to be called nothingness is not subjectivity but its shadow, its dream, its projection into the element of transcendent being.* That subjectivity cannot be confused with the pure universal and empty milieu floating in representation follows immediately from the fact that subjectivity is nothing transcendent. What characterizes it eidetically is rather that it is a life in a sphere of absolute immanence, that it is *the* life. *What is abstract is what is transcendent.* The transcendent element is a dead element that must be maintained in life by something more concrete than itself, for to maintain in life what is dead is, as Hegel says, what demands the greatest force. But what maintains in life is what *is* life — not precisely the understanding, but the effective life of absolute subjectivity in all its forms, that is, the body, and generally what common language too calls life. The reversal of the usual charge is complete and worth marking: idealism's abstraction is not immanence but transcendence.

Can the concrete character of absolute subjectivity be doubted if it welcomes into itself, as its deepest determinations, the intentionalities composing our corporeal life? If it defines the being of the body, is subjectivity a fiction, an abstraction of idealism? What is denser, more real, than a gaze? Less illusory than an appetite? Doubtless the concepts of subjectivity and body are closely bound as regards their correct ontological interpretation: what use is it to say the body is subjective if one forms a false idea of subjectivity, if one considers it a milieu of inert elements or a pure "void" facing the world, if one gives it a psychologistic or nihilistic interpretation? Research must rather progress so that the analysis of the body, conducted according to the conditions prescribed *a priori* by the structure of absolute subjectivity with which the body's original being is identified, can in return help us form a more precise idea of that fundamental structure. Thus the idea that subjectivity is not an impersonal and empty milieu finds confirmation in the fact that within the ontological analysis of the body the being of subjective movement and of sensing were determined as belonging to a *concrete individual*. A power is never an indeterminate power; it is always an ego's. The analysis of sensing led to an analogous conclusion, for the idea of a sensible passive synthesis without reference to an ego appeared deprived of sense. Likewise the connection between the idea of ontological possibility understood as foundation and the idea of original reality — not the reality rendered possible by the foundation but the reality *of the foundation itself* — is clearly indicated by the analysis of the body at the moment the body's being is interpreted as the reality of ontological possibility.

Another idea, closely bound to the preceding and no less decisive, concerns the possibility of an *absolute knowledge*. The idea of such knowledge implies that it not be reduced to form alone, form to which a contingent and foreign content is then inevitably opposed. Certainly subjectivity is always a life in the presence of a transcendent being. In itself, however, it is not an empty form; it already has a content not constituted by transcendent being but an original content, namely that of transcendental inner experience *as such*. Of this original, transcendental content we can say that it is what makes the density of life, a first and irreducible ontological density subsisting even when this life dwindles in despair and seems to lose all seriousness. Even then life is something other than a form; it has by itself, independently of the being toward which it surpasses itself, a content. Insofar as our experience relates to a transcendent content it is receptive, and the knowledge it realizes must be called *finite*. But when it is a question of the original content of our transcendental inner experience, the knowledge relating to it, no longer transcendent, is nothing but an *absolute* knowledge, for it has a content of which it is in a sense the origin. Such precisely is life, an absolute knowledge of this sort, and that is why it deserves its name.

The ontological analysis of the body rendered the nature of this absolute knowledge more manifest when it showed that a knowledge of the body was given us *before* its surging into the element of transcendent being, and interpreted such knowledge as an original revelation endowed with a transcendental content of its own, irreducible to any conceivable transcendent content. The obstacle to recognizing the proper ontological structure of these two original phenomena — the second being only a particular case of the first — and hence to admitting the possibility and existence of an absolute human knowledge, is *ontological monism*: the conception by which something real can be given us in general only in the element of transcendent being. This conception ends by emptying subjectivity of all reality; or, if it persists in considering subjectivity a reality, it can only be a reality manifesting itself in the sole milieu of being it knows, a milieu of radical exteriority — and thought founders in psychologism. *As regards the theory of the body, ontological monism had the decisive consequence of constantly preventing philosophical reflection from rising to the idea of the subjective body.* The body, a real element in the effectivity of being, could only belong to the ontological milieu of being in general; it was necessarily something transcendent. Thus reduced to its objective manifestation, it was mutilated of what constitutes its essential being.

Consequently the idea one formed of human nature had to be profoundly altered: for if reality is defined for us by consciousness, one can no longer understand the relation consciousness maintains with the body so long as that body is envisaged as an element of the world, a determination posited in being among other indifferent determinations. In such a perspective the phenomenon of incarnation necessarily appears to consciousness as a *paradox*, a mysterious synthetic adjunction to its own being, itself reduced to a pure form. An opposition is established between what is now only a pure nothingness and, on the other side, a fixed determination, a simple being-there. In fact consciousness is not the void of nothingness and the body is not an object. Subjectivity is real and the body is subjective. *The phenomenon of incarnation signifies nothing more than the reality of an ontological possibility that is not abstract but reveals itself identical with the very being of the ego.* This dissolution of the paradox of incarnation is the book's central promise redeemed, and it is achieved by removing both terms of the supposed synthesis rather than by explaining their union.

Henry turns to a second series of remarks bearing more particularly on the body. Contingent is the being contained in a universal milieu relative to which it presents itself as a particular determination. Contingency is a category referring to a determinate ontological region, that of transcendent being. Everything manifesting itself within such a region is contingent *because it has a horizon*. This horizon, which relative to the thing is like the "more" coming from the milieu in which it bathes, signifies for the mind the possibility surveying the present determination and, in showing its particularity, showing at the same time its possible transformation or disappearance. An extended thing is contingent relative to space: it can always be other than it is, have a different form, or be suppressed. This contingent character is not proper to spatial things; it affects every transcendent thing. On the contrary, what belongs to the sphere of subjectivity is by principle deprived of all horizon. The ontological characteristic of absolute subjectivity is transcendental immanence, by virtue of which the sphere of subjectivity opposes radically that of transcendent being, in the sense that it cannot be considered a milieu transgressing the determinations arising in it. To say transcendental intentionality is immanent is to say it is not immersed in a milieu vaster than itself. The immanence of a transcendental determination signifies on the contrary that subjectivity *exhausts itself* in it, because such a determination does not belong to a world, because it is not surrounded by an element surpassing it. Thus life in general, as absolute life, is nothing contingent. The argument from horizon is the Conclusion's most compact instrument and it does a great deal of work: contingency requires a surrounding milieu, and immanence by definition has none.

Because it too reveals itself in a sphere of pure immanence, the life of the body does not escape this rule.
