Doubtless the nose, the eye, the limbs manifest themselves as determinations whose strange configuration or absurd character no functional justification suffices to mask from a lucid mind. But the body is not an ensemble of determinations of this kind, determinations surveyed by a pure spirit. In its original being our body is rather a structure made of virtually compossible intentionalities, conformably to their eidetic types — an organic totality of transcendental determinations accomplished in a sphere of absolute immanence. From the standpoint of this totality the body was characterized as an *ontological habit*, foundation of all our psychological habits. The absolute body therefore escapes by principle the category of contingency; and if the problem of the body's situation is posed ineluctably, one must see clearly what being-in-situation signifies for the absolute body.

The problem of situation can be envisaged seriously only because it concerns not merely the objective body, which seems situated like any other object. To say of the absolute body that it is situated must be understood in a wholly different sense. For the objective body is situated *in the world*, a general milieu comprising other objects among which third-person relations of position are instituted. For the object, situation is synonymous with contingency. But the absolute body cannot be submitted to contingency; it could be only if it became something transcendent. Now being situated cannot imply for the original being of the body a change of its fundamental ontological status. The idea of such a change is ontologically an absurdity, since it would entail not a modification but the *annihilation* of its being. Being-in-situation signifies for the absolute body being in a certain relation with transcendent being, but this time a *transcendental* relation. To say our body is situated is to say it relates to the world and, within the world, to this or that determination of transcendent being. To sustain such a relation with the world, however, is not to belong to it as a thing belongs to its element. On the contrary, *our body can be in the world only on condition of being nothing of the world. It is because it is subjective that our body is situated.* Being-in-situation thus finds its possibility in the ontological structure of the original body. Far from leading to an abstract idealism leaving human being afloat and unable to account for its insertion into the world, the philosophy of subjectivity is on the contrary what lets us account for such insertion. The inversion is the section's central result and it answers the standard existentialist charge against philosophies of subjectivity directly.

Inversely, this interpretation forbids us any naive or "realist" representation of being-in-situation, of the phenomenon of the "here." In its situation the body does not abandon its subjective character, since it is by virtue of that character that such a situation is possible for it. It is because it is an intentionality that the body is oriented in a determinate way; it is because this intentionality is a transcendental inner experience that the orientation of the absolute body in no way alters its original ontological status. The body maintains itself near itself in its relation to the world or to itself. Nothing is changed in the latter case, for the transcendent body toward which our original body then surpasses itself is in no way that original and absolute body. When the body relates to itself, its subjective status is not modified; only the content of its transcendent experience changes, characteristically it is true, since the object seems here to be the body itself. To this transcendent body, however, the proper essence of life does not belong; life is accomplished outside it, as it is accomplished generally outside the world, for it is the life that is and remains immanent.

Being-in-situation also concerns our transcendent objective body — as when we say our body is "here," in the drawing room, in the garden. But despite appearances the situation of our objective body does not constitute an autonomous phenomenon assimilable to that of any object immersed in the transcendent milieu of the world. Our objective body is not at all any object whatever; its situation presents, like any of its ontological properties, a proper character it in fact *borrows* from the situation of the absolute body. For even in our representation our body does not present itself as a pure being-there posited in simple juxtaposition to the other transcendent things surrounding it — the room, the chair. It is so little in such a relation of juxtaposition that it in fact *uses* the chair, *goes to* the wardrobe, *turns* the key, *leaves* the room and, for that, *opens* the door. Now if it is evident that it is by the mediation of determinations of this sort ("open," "turn," "go toward," "leave") that the situation of our transcendent objective body is defined, it is no less evident that such determinations do not belong originally to the milieu of transcendent being from which one nonetheless seeks to define the situation of our body-object: they stem from a wholly different sphere of existence, corresponding to the intentionalities in which the absolute life of our subjective body expresses itself. The verbs are the argument, and they are well chosen: every locution that actually situates my body in a room is a verb of the subjective body's powers.

The task of a rigorous phenomenological analysis of the situation of the transcendent objective body would be to distinguish among the constitutive characters of that situation those referring to the general milieu of transcendent being according to third-person relations expressed by prepositions like "beside," "above," "at such a distance," and those which, while equally present in representation, can be for us what they are only by a *borrowing* from another region of being, the intentional sphere of the subjective body. Among the latter, rank those by which my objective body gives itself as an object going toward another object, turning the door handle. It is not difficult to see that these last deserve to be considered essential, because they belong exclusively to that well-determined object which is our transcendent objective body, whereas the first define any object of the world in general.

A deeper analysis would show that the characters that can in principle concern any object (the book can be "beside" the inkwell as much as my hand) in fact undergo a radical modification of value when they qualify my objective body. To say my hand is beside the inkwell is to say it can seize it at any moment; its relation to the inkwell becomes in a sense *interior* to it, whereas the book's relation to the same inkwell is exterior and indifferent. Our transcendent objective body is a *magical* object never statically situated like a stone at the foot of a wall. All the determinations relating to its situation reduce finally to that first group of characters by virtue of which this objective body situates itself in the world not as an inert being-there but as a moving object *secretly inhabited by a subject*. And these essential characters must be understood as founded and not original, since our objective body borrows them from the absolute body whose objectification in the general milieu of transcendent being it represents.

It appears then that the relation of the situation of our transcendent objective body to that of our absolute body is strictly parallel to the relation uniting in general the objective and absolute bodies of the ego. Whence this essential remark: *it is not because our body is also a transcendent body, a body such as philosophy understood it before the discovery of the subjective body, that man's being is a situated being. On the contrary, our transcendent objective body is situated in a determinate sense proper to it only because our absolute body is already situated, as subjectivity in transcendental relation with the world.* Thus are destroyed the naive representations dominating the philosophical tradition, according to which the metaphysical being of man understood as a pure consciousness and an abstract subjectivity would be situated, determined, indeed individualized only by its mysterious relation (as the myths of the soul's "fall" into the body show) with an objective body. Far from the character of being-in-situation being communicated from the body-object to the absolute body, the "communication" is effected in the opposite direction.

One must therefore not interpret the being-in-situation of the absolute body from the situation of our objective body, still less from that of ordinary objects. The nature of the original body in no way resembles that of the transcendent thing, whose eidetic status prescribes that it give itself necessarily in a multiplicity of indefinitely variable aspects. Already, it is true, the manner of giving of our transcendent objective body does not reduce totally to that of the transcendent thing in general; it escapes it in that we are not free to adopt toward our own body whatever perspective we want. But this is far from being the only difference. Classical psychology could think so only because its analysis of the body stopped at the objective body alone. But neither the absolute body *nor the organic body* presents an ontological status authorizing us to characterize them relative to the transcendent thing by a simple *limitation* in the free variation of our perspectives. This is evident for the absolute body, which presents us no "aspect," since it is nothing transcendent and no phenomenological distance separates us from it.

As for the organic body, Henry formulates a paradoxical yet phenomenologically evident proposition: the transcendent being of the organic body gives itself to us neither in a multiplicity of indefinitely variable aspects, nor through a determinate aspect or series of aspects escaping the free variation of our perspectives. Although its phenomenological status is radically different from that of our original body, our organic body gives itself in a kind of *absolute knowledge*. Because it is the strict *non-represented* correlate of the intentionalities of our absolute body, it is always wholly present for us and we possess it in a knowing excluding all limitation and all possibility of error. If it is situated relative to our absolute body, this situation is in a sense an absolute situation; the idea of any change occurring to the organic body and altering the state of immediate disposition in which it stands relative to the intentionalities of our absolute body is by principle inadmissible. Henry adds a note recalling a point insisted on earlier: the schema of our body — the organic body — is a *complete* schema, not a lacunary representation. It is precisely because it is not a representation that it has this finished character, and one sees how far the philosophy of the absolute and organic bodies stands from the idealism reducing everything to representations.

These researches have at least removed all equivocation about the original being of the absolute body, which is not a transcendent thing but *that relative to which* every such thing is sketched in a series of determinate aspects. Because this determination of aspects operates from the absolute body, the latter must be considered not so much something situated as *what originally situates*, what situates every thing relative to us. Our original body is an *absolute center*, and far from being freely submitted to the general category of situation, it is rather in situation in a determinate sense by virtue of which it must be described finally as the ontological foundation of every possible situation. Henry adds in a note that our original body founds only the situation of intramundane determinations, utensils and things; what situates the original body itself is subjectivity understood not as the transcendental relation of being-in-the-world but in its internal structure, as immanence — for which he refers to *L'essence de la manifestation*. Registering this note matters: it marks the point where the present book hands the problem over to the larger work.

That the original body is not situated, in the sense that it is nothing transcendent, leads to rejecting a number of existential theses about the body and about existence's alleged taking-a-position toward it. In such a position-taking, existence would "assume" its body in this or that fashion, and minute existential descriptions would be needed to account for the different ways existence relates to its body, accepts it, refuses it. But the presupposition at the base of these descriptions is that our body is something transcendent — a presupposition merely taking over the unelucidated conceptions of traditional philosophy and common sense, according to which our body is nothing but an object (whatever proper characters one seeks to recognize in such an object to distinguish it from other objects of the world). There certainly exist intentionalities in which we direct ourselves toward our own body. But despite the complexity of the phenomenon, the original being of the body is to be sought in these intentionalities that direct themselves toward, against, and not in the transcendent body toward which they direct themselves, the latter being only a founded body.

To the affirmation "I have a body" one must therefore oppose the more original affirmation: *"I am my body."* Yet this statement risks remaining ambiguous so long as one prefers to the rigor of ontological analysis the subtleties or verbalism of a philosophy of ambiguity. "I am my body" does not mean "I am my body while not being it," "I have to be it in the mode of not being it," and so on — propositions resting in fact on the presupposition just denounced, by virtue of which the body arises originally in transcendence as a term toward which one can then, and only then, comport oneself in various ways. "I am my body" means very exactly: the original being of my body is a transcendental inner experience, and consequently the life of this body is a mode of the absolute life of the ego. "I have a body" means: a transcendent body also manifests itself for me and gives itself as submitted, by a relation of dependence, to the absolute body which, as the theory of the constitution of one's own body showed, founds both this objective body and the relation of possession binding it to the ego. The refusal of the Sartrean formulas is pointed and is the Conclusion's clearest statement of method: what looks like dialectical subtlety is the symptom of an unexamined presupposition.

These remarks should serve as introduction to a *new philosophy of life*. The concept of life can designate either the object of a determinate science, biology, or the object of a perception bearing phenomenological characters making it appear a living thing. But when it relates to the human being, the concept acquires its original sense only if it designates for us *a life in the first person*, the absolute life of the ego. Such a life no longer manifests itself by determinate characters affecting an element of transcendent being; it reveals itself in a sphere of radical immanence so that it is nothing other for us than the very life of our original body. If such is the ontological nature of this life we call ours, then the opposition given as fundamental by so many philosophies and by tradition generally, between *life* and *consciousness* or self-consciousness, must be called into question.

This opposition receives a decisive signification in Hegel, where it represents a moment of the fundamental opposition between the positivity of being and the negativity of spirit. Doubtless life is already in a certain sense negative spirit, the concept: as a process that cannot be fixed and traverses the different parts of the organism, it opposes the figure anatomy describes, which is only the abstract and dead side of existence. Yet the true concept is not life but self-consciousness. In it the concept manifests its deep, authentic negativity, its inequality relative to life, which is then designated a "natural position," the positivity of vital being. The negation of life as positivity is the mediation essential to positing the self. The latter, as authentic concept, is therefore not life but rather its contrary. If life was, as "subsisting independence" and positive expansion, the immediate mode in which self-consciousness first manifested itself, it appears in fact that *self-consciousness is not life*. This is why, to prove its own being, consciousness must show it does not identify with this life which is an element in being; it is ready to sacrifice it and confront death. The two elements opposed in the struggle of consciousnesses are not so much two particular consciousnesses as, within each consciousness, the positivity of life and pure negativity as the authentic operation of the concept. The fundamental opposition remains that of consciousness and the living thing. Life is thus envisaged by Hegelian philosophy as an object consciousness negates and surpasses in order to conquer itself as self-consciousness. Henry's reading of the master–slave dialectic is worth marking: he relocates the struggle inside each consciousness, so that risking life is the negation of one's own positivity rather than a contest between persons.

To say life is an element that must be transcended amounts to saying it is a transcendent thing, exterior to the deep nature of subjectivity, to the pure negativity of spirit. In such a perspective existence qualifies itself according to how it transcends this life, relates to it, negates it, assumes it. One is very close to the contemporary theses previously criticized. If one remains faithful to the teaching of the ontological theory of the body and conceives the life of the body as a radically immanent life, one understands on the contrary that the possibility of its ever being transcended is set aside *a priori* by the eidetic status of such a life. Human life thereby becomes a *grave* thing that no pure spirit henceforth has leisure to survey. Subjectivity is not that pure spirit enclosed in its own nothingness and incapable of descending into the determination of life; it *is* that life. *That subjectivity is life — that is the seriousness of existence.* Existence is not the pure negativity of the concept, an abstract and empty form incapable of equaling anything and condemned to leave outside itself what makes the effectivity of being. Insofar as it is life, subjectivity certainly has not the power to equal transcendent being, but the very project of such an equality is ontologically an absurdity. True equality, however, life possesses, insofar as it is a life in a sphere of absolute immanence. In such a sphere subjectivity reveals itself originally to itself; it has a content that is nothing transcendent but its effective and real being. For it to experience this being, there is no need that it be *recognized*, nor that a life with which it coincides be negated. For life is equal to itself; it *is* its equality with self, an equality prescribed by eidetic reasons. The dismissal of recognition is the sharpest anti-Hegelian moment in the book: the self-equality Hegel makes the prize of a struggle is, for Henry, the starting condition of immanence.

Analogous remarks hold for the problem of *action*. The difficulties generally met here come from the horizon within which it is debated never being clarified. Reflecting seriously on the presuppositions constituting that horizon, one would find they reduce essentially to two general schemas.

The first, that of intellectualist philosophies, consists in thinking practical life in the light of the concepts of "means" and "end." In such a perspective, means and ends become at the moment of action the theme of determinate thoughts whose complexity parallels that of their objects. Rational action would imply a perfect knowledge of these terms — which is to say there is for man no perfectly rational action. The data one would need to consider go to infinity; in most cases we cannot wait for the examination of the situation to be completed. Urgency prevents the systematic elucidation of a determinate situation, an elucidation impossible in principle since the situation changes ceaselessly and since we often have only rudimentary knowledge of the means we employ — as is the case for the body, whose mechanism, it is said, escapes us. This is why Jaspers affirms that human action is impossible without a "force of resolution" supplementing the finitude of our knowledge, incapable of rising to the certainty of constraining evidence. He praises Descartes for insisting on the always provisional character of morality, that is, for perceiving the inevitable gap between our action and our knowledge.

The ontological theory of the subjective body lets us understand how these conceptions are in fact deprived of all philosophical foundation. In action, and first in corporeal action, the means do not form the theme of an intellectual knowledge, and the end is never something represented but the transcendent term toward which subjective movement (not a theoretical intentionality) immediately surpasses itself. It follows that the knowledge appearing in this original phenomenon is in no way an imperfect knowledge but rather an absolute knowledge whose possibility rests on the ontological status of the original body as subjective body, that is, as an absolute knowing. *There is no gap between our knowing and our action, because action is itself, in its own essence, a knowing.* The affirmation of an inadequation between our knowledge and our action — belied by the daily experience of our humblest gestures (washing, manual work, playing a sport, driving a car) — results from the insufficiency of the concept of knowledge one disposes of, which reduces knowledge to theoretical or representative knowledge alone. Henry adds a note anticipating the reply that habit made the required representations unconscious: the theories of habit and the unconscious invoked here imply as their presupposition the intellectualist theory of action whose absurdities they seek to attenuate, and cannot therefore serve to found it. To the thesis positing the finitude of our knowledge in action is inevitably bound the affirmation of its irrational character. Herein appears the true origin of the contemporary conceptions invoking a "resolution" proper to the human being as it throws itself into action according to an "engagement" whose risk it "assumes." Far from issuing in a renewal of ethics, these romantic themes must be thought in their close solidarity with the presuppositions of classical intellectualist philosophy — as Jaspers's admiration for Descartes's morality attests. The genealogy is the section's payoff: existentialist resolve is the child of intellectualism, not its overcoming.

The traditional interpretation of action also operates through the concept of *intention*. The second general schema prescribes that action is the *realization of an intention*, this realization understood in turn as an *objectification*. Such an interpretation, whose origin goes back to Hellenic conceptions of creation — itself conceived on the exclusive model of artistic creation, in which a subjective image presides as directing ideal over the confection of the concrete work — implies presuppositions whose ontological destruction would now impose itself as a heavy task if the ontological theory of the body had not already accomplished it. These presuppositions are: that intention is a subjective state leaving outside itself what makes the effective being of real presence; and that the realization of intention therefore implies its passage into the sole element where real being can be effectively present, that is, into objectivity.

To this schema the Hegelian dialectic of action notably conforms, which far from calling traditional conceptions into question rather underlines them systematically. It is precisely because in Hegelianism subjectivity is conceived as being by itself deprived of all reality that action imposes itself on it as the task of transforming its pure thought into being and making itself a thing. The subjectivity that does not assume such a task, that does not alienate itself in the element of being, is no longer anything but the unreal subjectivity of subjective idealism — for instance the "beautiful soul." Not to act signifies for it not to recognize the value of reality, to shrink from it by refusing to alienate itself in it. Action is precisely an alienation of this sort. Which is to say it is necessarily finite, for when the Self that accepted the ordeal of being finds itself again as a determinate element in the general milieu of effectivity, it perceives itself as a finite and contingent determination to which spirit as absolute negativity cannot equal itself. Whence the necessity of new dialectics in which the Self will aim at surmounting its alienation.

The Hegelian conception rests on the presuppositions Henry has denounced: that action must be understood essentially as an objectification, a passage and transformation of subjectivity — by itself deprived of reality — into the element of transcendent being. What can such a transformation signify ontologically? We are here before a fundamental ontological absurdity on which the whole Hegelian edifice rests. Expressions like "the subject objectifies itself," "the self objectifies itself" are particularly improper; they mean at most that *the representation but not the being* of the ego manifests itself in the milieu of transcendence. For what is subjective cannot manifest itself in such a milieu; it is an eidetic prescription of its ontological structure that it can only reveal itself originally in a sphere of absolute immanence. To objectify itself, for the ego, is therefore only to *represent itself to itself*. But precisely, to acquire such a representation of oneself *is not to act*; it would rather be to live a contemplative life. In fact Hegel fails to account for human action, and this because, too pressed to discover the sense of the multiple experiences in which the history of men is realized, he neglected the essential: the philosophical elucidation of the ontological status of action itself. The objection is exact and turns Hegel's own commitment against him: on his premises the alleged self-objectification of action can only be self-representation, which is the contemplative attitude action was supposed to overcome.

Pursuing this elucidation leads to recognizing: that subjectivity has no need to objectify itself to be real, both because in itself it is already an absolute reality and not a mere desire to be, and because objectifying itself is for it in principle impossible; and that, precisely because the idea of an objectification of subjectivity is set aside for eidetic reasons, action cannot be understood as such an objectification. Phenomenological analysis manifests with perfect evidence what action is: *a subjective essence*. Action certainly does not coincide with intention; it is separated from it in that it implies the intervention of the body — but the body that acts is neither the represented body nor the organic body; it is the absolute body, and consequently action is nothing but a modality of the life of absolute subjectivity. Though it is not the intention, action is no less intentional. In the process of action, intentionalities unify synthetically while correlatively intentional unities organize themselves whose linkage is that of the modifications affecting transcendent being. Throughout this process action remains intentional, that is, maintains itself near itself in a sphere of immanence, without ever "leaving" itself to manifest itself in person in the world — for again, it is not a life that represents itself to itself but a life that *acts*. And at the end of the process this action has remained in its own milieu, in the form of a last intentionality synthetically bound to all those preceding, whose totality constitutes the phenomenon we call a determinate action.

One can recognize the proper ontological status of action only if one disposes of a parallel ontological interpretation of the original being of the absolute body. For if the body is envisaged only as an object of the world, the action occurring through its mediation itself belongs to that world; it is no longer, for a subjectivity reduced to a pure intention, anything but a third-person process. The close link uniting the Hegelian dialectic of action to the Cartesian conception previously criticized — according to which the subjective element in the total phenomenon of action is only a desire whose realization implies the mysterious intervention of the extended body — hardly needs remarking. The theory of the subjective body lets us rise on the contrary to a philosophy of action in the first person, a philosophy of *human activity*. Action would not truly be an ego's if the ego's part in it were confined to formulating a wish to which impersonal processes responded as if by miracle. *Action is not magic*; it is rather, as Maine de Biran teaches us, effort, subjective tension, struggle against the transcendent element. The simple philosophical realization of the necessity of accounting for the phenomenologically evident distinction between the mere wish for action and the effective action we accomplish in effort suffices to reject all the traditional conceptions.

The rejection of these speculative theses has important consequences for moral conceptions. In particular, the critique of a morality of intention must no longer be confused with a general critique of the philosophy of subjectivity, nor serve as a pretext for it. *It is rather from the philosophy of subjectivity and it alone that a valid critique of the morality of intention can come.* In every other philosophy, intention is the only subjective element of action; what makes action's essential being can then only be a represented (objective) element ceasing to be comprised in the immanent sphere of life — the sphere to which moral essence nonetheless belongs. Thenceforth moral analysis finds in action, as properly human, subjective, moral element, only the intention, to which it is obliged to hold while sensing that it lets the essential escape. This essential — action itself — the philosophy of subjectivity recognizes for what it is: an intentionality, a subjective element falling under the categories of morality, whereas action envisaged as an objective third-person process can never have anything but *the innocence of a stone*. The philosophy of subjectivity, and it alone, can submit to ethics the very element of action; the ontological analysis of the subjective body, and it alone, can give a sense to that unheard-of affirmation: *the bodies will be judged* — a phrase Henry takes from Rimbaud's *Une saison en enfer*. The Rimbaud line is the Conclusion's refrain and it states the ethical stake exactly: only a subjective body can be a moral subject.

The determination of the original being of our body as pure subjectivity, the absolute character of the knowledge relating to it, the idea of an absolute and non-contingent content proper to such knowledge, the ontological interpretation of being-in-situation making the body an absolute center and the foundation of the category of situation, the rejection of all theses presupposing the transcendence of the original being of our body, and correlatively the introduction of a new philosophy of life understood as absolute life rather than a determination susceptible of being negated or transcended, and of a new philosophy of action interpreted not as objectification but as a subjective essence bearing in it its own knowing — all these elements issue equally in the *negation of the link so often established between the phenomenon of incarnation and finitude, contingency, indeed absurdity* as characters inherent in human reality.

The recognition of a link between our corporeity and our finitude is not the privilege of contemporary reflection; it commands the general idea of man that a tradition dominating at least Western thought expresses. This idea is that man must be considered a double being, a synthesis of two opposed terms, body and spirit. The relation between these heterogeneous elements can only be paradoxical. In the regard of pure spirit, incarnation manifests itself as a contingent phenomenon marking human reality with an insurmountable finitude. By reaction against certain forms of religious thought, humanist or even naturalist tendencies may strive to rehabilitate the body; in their eyes, however, it remains what it seems to be for the beliefs they oppose: the inferior element one can want to rehabilitate only because one has not ceased to consider it such. Whoever says *mens sana in corpore sano* continues in any case to think these two elements in the light of a radical heterogeneity; the axiological appreciation he formulates may diverge from the correlative religious appreciation, but it rests on the same foundation — the idea of the duality of human nature, which dominates not only the whole Western philosophical tradition but also permeates popular conceptions. The point about rehabilitation is worth holding: to defend the body against its detractors on their own terms is to concede the framework that made it need defending.

As for the religious conceptions arising in this cultural world, they seem to give the idea of duality an almost infinite signification. Is the conception of a relation between human finitude and the phenomenon of incarnation not expressed generally in Christianity by the idea that sin is essentially *sin of the flesh*? In many respects the spiritual combat seems to exhaust itself, for the Christian tradition, in a struggle between flesh and spirit. The body is thought as "weighing down the soul," a weight the soul must rid itself of if it wants to tear itself from finitude and sin in order to participate in the divine life which is a life of spirit. The idea of finitude is so deeply bound to that of the body that many spiritual exercises aim at mastering it, whether directly as in asceticism (fasting, abstinence) or indirectly by an attitude of distrust toward sensible life in general. The unity of the Western cultural world manifests itself here in the often rigorous parallelism of these religious conceptions and certain themes of the philosophical tradition prescribing to consciousness, as the necessary condition of its progress, a kind of asceticism toward the sensible and imaginary worlds — as for example among the Cartesians.

The question must then be posed: does the ontological theory of the body imply the rejection of everything tradition teaches about incarnation? Does it claim to remove all real ontological foundation from our experience of finitude? Is the latter not precisely an *experience*, the irrecusable experience from which alone the human condition can be correctly interpreted? Everything making that condition what it is — anguish before finitude, vertigo before the flesh, the fundamental dissatisfaction inhabiting all our experiences and particularly those relating to our corporeal and sensible life — can all this be purely and simply denied? Or can ontology receive the qualification of being a positive science only so long as it assumes the task of elaborating the foundation of our *real* experiences and not of imaginary ones without relation to our true condition? To seek the foundation making possible the experiences that are truly ours is the goal of ontology. Because ontology is a positive science, this research is rigorous. What it shows is that if finitude is for human reality an irrecusable experience, such an experience cannot receive the foundation too often assigned it, which consists in a philosophically naive interpretation of the phenomenon of incarnation. Note the care here: Henry is not denying the experience of finitude but refusing the account of its ground, and the distinction governs everything that follows.

As soon as finitude or contingency is at issue one finds oneself before schemes of thought most often without real philosophical foundation. Thus the affirmation of the finitude of human action takes place in Hegel within a horizon whose ontological destruction revealed improper presuppositions. Likewise the connection by which the elucidation of incarnation so often raises the idea of contingency rests on the inadequacy of such elucidation insofar as it takes for guiding thread the status of the objective body alone. In other terms, ontology repels *a priori* only the *naive* interpretations of the ideas of finitude or contingency. For such ideas and the experiences they translate it seeks on the contrary a positive foundation. It is by such research that ontology exercises its jurisdiction over the cultural — philosophical, religious, or moral — patrimony of humanity, and manifests itself as the only really positive science capable of giving a sense to tradition. This task, the highest to which philosophy can pretend, imposes itself all the more imperiously as the obscurity bathing the questions is more fundamental. Where these questions relate to the body and finitude, it is no exaggeration to say this obscurity is nearly total.

Tradition establishes a relation between corporeity and the finitude of human nature, deepened in specifically Christian thought to the extreme point where the body receives the signification of *being* sin. What must one understand by such a body? This body, also called the flesh, presents itself to the believer as the symbol of his possible perdition; it is perhaps for him nothing other than the anguish before the possibility of that perdition. The body is thus felt and thought as the obstacle to be vanquished. Salvation, envisaged in radical opposition to the life of the body, is called true life, a new life, a life of "spirit." Insofar as it relates to the possibility of perdition and fall, *the body designates nothing other than a determinate mode of human existence*. This mode is understood as essentially bound to finitude, that is, to sin; it nonetheless remains a mode of our life, a *sui generis* mode to which another conceivable and sometimes real mode is opposed, in which salvation consists. *By "body" one must therefore in no way understand that objective body which is a thing; what is signified is a clearly defined modality of the life of absolute subjectivity.* For this same reason the "body" of Christian anthropology assimilated to sin cannot be confused with our organic body either. The latter, like the objective body, is never anything but *an existent*; it therefore has nothing to do with a *mode of existence*. This distinction between an existent and a mode of existence is the hinge of the entire final movement.

Shall one say the body of the Christian vision must then be assimilated to the absolute body? That would be a grave confusion, forgetting the difference that must always be maintained between the *existential* and the *ontological* standpoints. "Body" in the Christian sense designates a particular mode of existence; it refers to a specific intentionality offering itself as one possible determination among an infinity of others. The body envisaged ontologically as absolute body refers to no particular intentionality of our corporeal life; it designates nothing but the *common being* of all these intentionalities, the original ontological milieu to which they all belong. Such intentionalities are compossible within the original being of our absolute body; describing them according to their existential articulations is not ontology's task, which accomplishes only the preparatory work.

One must therefore carefully distinguish the "body" as a defined mode of our historical existence — the mode in which existence manifests itself to religious consciousness as a sinful existence — from the ontological milieu of the absolute body in which all the intentionalities of corporeal life deploy, and of which that determinate existence Christianity also calls body or flesh is only a particular and contingent form. Contingent the existence delivering itself to sin is in the highest degree, in the sense that among all possible forms of existence the determinate form it assumes is in no way prescribed *a priori* by the ontological structure of the absolute body; and this absence of any eidetic prescription that would make sin necessary, Christian theology expresses by saying sin is a historical accident whose link with human "nature" cannot be interpreted in the light of a necessity comparable to that belonging to the order of essences. *In other terms, it is a purely existential signification that Christian anthropology confers on the word "body" when it makes it designate a state neighboring on sin. The body so understood in no way designates an ontological reality: it is neither the objective body, nor the organic body, nor the absolute body as such.* What it translates is a state of existence presupposing the fundamental ontological structure of the three bodies, on the same footing as any of our intentionalities: on that structure rests equally the determinate and privileged state of existence in which salvation consists.

Neither salvation nor sin can therefore be related as such to ontological structures. "Flesh" and "spirit" both designate in Christianity specific modes of existence, opposed radically as to the religious value conferred on them but remaining two modes of existence, two intentionalities belonging as such to the same ontological sphere of absolute subjectivity. *From the ontological standpoint there is therefore no difference between "flesh" and "spirit."* It is then the very unity of the Western tradition that is called into question.

That unity seems to rest on a dualist conception of human nature whose origins go far back. But *it is in Hellenism, in no way in Christianity, that these origins must be sought*, if it is true that the dualism of flesh and spirit takes on no ontological signification in Christian anthropology. In Greece, on the contrary, the idea one formed of man corresponded to the one the humanist tradition delivers today, seeing in human nature a relation between two opposed terms whose requirements must equally be satisfied. The difficult balance between these terms is the goal of an ethic thus submitted to the Greek idea of harmony. But in the eyes of such a humanism of Hellenic origin the two elements are far from being on the same plane: they are two ontologically heterogeneous realities of which one remains superior. As body, man is only an animal; it is spirit that raises him to properly human dignity. Of this spirit one asks only the liberalism of not despising the body. Because the body is held to be an objective reality, it remains something contingent, perishable, inferior. This reassignment of the flesh–spirit dualism from Christianity to Hellenism is the Conclusion's boldest historical claim, and everything turns on the distinction already established: an ontological duality of two heterogeneous realities is Greek; a duality of two modes of one subjective life is not a dualism at all.

To this standpoint Christianity opposes itself radically. Insofar as it considers the body no longer as a determinate and contingent mode of our historical existence but as an ontological reality constitutive of human nature, it formulates a series of surprising affirmations that can receive a philosophical sense only within the doctrine of the subjective body. For it is only if our body is, in its original being, something subjective, that the brief allusions dogmatics makes to its metaphysical destiny can be anything but extravagant conceptions. Extravagant indeed had affirmations like the dogma of the resurrection of the body necessarily to appear to the Greeks. This is why the Corinthians went off sneering when Saint Paul claimed not to reserve to the soul the privilege of that resurrection. It is clear on the contrary that if the original being of our body is something subjective, it falls, like the "soul," under the category of what is susceptible of being repeated and *judged*. It is manifestly to the content of Christian theology that Rimbaud borrowed his strange affirmation.

Ontological analysis alone can bring clarity to a domain that could otherwise only be one of confusions or contradictions. For it has not been sufficiently remarked that the Christian tradition proposes about the body two radically opposed theses, affirming on one hand that the body is sin and on the other that it is called to resurrection. Must one understand that sin is promised to divine glory? *The dissociation between the existential and ontological significations of the word "body" alone sets aside such an absurdity.* And yet such a dissociation must be thought in the light of a more fundamental unity, which the theory of the subjective body furnishes. For in fact *the body is susceptible of being sin only because it is also what is susceptible of being resurrected*. Sin or resurrection, finitude or salvation, arise only within the category of subjectivity. This resolution of the two Christian theses is the finest local result of the whole Conclusion, and it turns the apparent contradiction into a proof: only a subjective body could bear both predicates.

Singularly hasty appears, in this regard, Nietzsche's reproach to Christians of being "despisers" of the body. Such an assertion belongs to the category of gratuitous affirmations that can serve culture only insofar as they invite us to take clear consciousness of the indigence of their foundation. In formulating such a critique Nietzsche is the dupe of a grave confusion of which his philosophy is not the origin but which it greatly contributed to reinforcing: attributing to Christianity themes wholly foreign to it, stemming rather from the Greco-humanist tradition. Nietzsche imagines that Christianity attacks the body *as such*, as an ontological reality belonging properly and essentially to human reality, whereas by "body" Christian anthropology means nothing but a contingent, specific, clearly defined mode of our existence. As for the real being of our body as fundamental ontological essence, Nietzsche does not see that Christianity forms a far higher idea of it than he — a far more philosophical one too, since it rises to the idea of the subjective body or at least implies it as a necessary presupposition. For Nietzsche, on the contrary, the body remains what it is in traditional dualism of Hellenic origin, a reality heterogeneous to consciousness and subjective existence. It matters little then whether one conceives such a reality in the light of romantic vitalism when Cartesian mechanism is only an outmoded platitude, or whether one exalts it instead of devaluing it: the revolution one believes oneself to operate does not go beyond the simple promotion of subjective preferences; far from calling into question the traditional frame of philosophical reflection, it rather implies it and remains its dupe in its apparent novelty. The verdict on Nietzsche follows the same criterion applied to naturalism and to *mens sana in corpore sano*: inverting a valuation while keeping its ontology changes nothing.

Henry turns finally to elucidating *finitude*, which can be understood in a fourfold sense.

First, to be finite means to be submitted to the determination of being-there. My body is finite insofar as it occupies a determinate place in space and time. This signification is rooted in the transcendence of a horizon to which the being it liberates cannot by principle equal itself.

Second, to be finite means to be submitted to the determination of being-there not originally but only *derivatively*. Consider any intentionality: we cannot apply to it finitude in the first sense, intentionality escaping by principle the sphere of transcendent being. It is not excluded, however, that we may *represent* this intentionality to ourselves. The possibility of such a representation, as a radical modification affecting the original ontological structure of intentionality, is included in its status as a permanent eidetic possibility. The power inhabiting such a modification is none other than that of *destiny*, which consists in the objectification by whose mediation what first revealed itself within a sphere of absolute immanence now manifests itself in the general element of transcendent being. Such a destiny is necessarily tragic, for in it what made the intimacy of the self's life and the freshness of its experience dissolves into the insipidity of objectivity and becomes a dead element of representation. Corporeal life does not escape this rending destiny, precisely because it is a subjective life. Thus in love the gestures in which it consists may be submitted to that hard law making them no longer the very substance of our existence but soon mere objects of our consciousness, so that where life previously was, there will be room only for death. Henry cites Rilke's second Duino Elegy on the fate of lovers' kisses. Note that this sense of finitude is a *permanent eidetic possibility* rather than an accident: any immanent determination can suffer objectification, which is what makes the account general.

In the ontological interpretation of this destiny one must not forget that the *being* of intentionality as such cannot by principle become anything transcendent, and that consequently the objectification in which destiny consists designates not the objectification of intentionality itself but the *substitution for it of its simple representation*. Because the finitude here at issue is bound to this representation, the second sense does not differ essentially from the first, but shows only that what is now for us a determinate and finite being-there could precisely not have been such, in its original being at least. But the elucidation is not finished, for finitude can appear *on the transcendental plane itself*, in two determinate ways.

Third, intentionality can be said finite insofar as it relates to a *world*. So understood, finitude is an essential qualification belonging to the being of human reality as such. Every intentionality obeys, by reason of its principled transcendence, this concept. This third sense is distinguished from the preceding in that the term submitted to finitude can no longer be considered a transcendent element: it designates the intentionality considered in itself, in its original and subjective being. This being is said finite because it is oriented toward the world essentially, so that its thought cannot pretend to survey the world to which it is bound. Whence the further consequence: the determinations intentionality reaches within the world are always transcended toward it, so that they appear as finite determinations which, in their finitude, constitute all our thought can pretend to know, since they are also what hides from us the world that surpasses them all infinitely.

Such a conception, making finitude an essential and not synthetic determination of human existence, forbids us any discrimination among the different intentionalities in which existence expresses itself. It is therefore not a determinate mode of our historical existence that will be said finite, by opposition to others in which finitude could be lifted. In other terms, *it is the possibility of a salvation for human existence that is here denied*. Finitude so understood receives an ontological signification and lets us acquire a systematic view of human nature. The problem, however, is whether one has not yielded precisely to the requirements of the *system* in raising to ontological dignity a finitude that perhaps belongs essentially only to a determinate mode of our life. The suspicion is well placed and it is the same one Henry directed at Hegel: a thesis that secures systematic completeness at the cost of foreclosing salvation should be examined for whether the system, rather than the phenomena, required it.

Fourth, finitude is the finitude of a *determinate* intentionality. By such an intentionality consciousness assumes a mode of existence not belonging to it essentially but in a sense accidentally, though the possibility of that mode is naturally comprised in its own essence. In this standpoint distinction again has right of citizenship. Finitude ceases to be a general qualification affixed once and for all to human nature. It must rather be interpreted concretely; it appears in well-defined experiences distinguished qualitatively among the modes of existence. These are not all equivalent; ontology has not formulated its ethical appreciations once and for all. The difference specifying the mode said to be finite expresses itself thus: the world in which we live now takes on for us a particular face — it receives the signification of being *fault*. Correlatively, the intentionality living in the presence of such a world is a *sui generis* intentionality experiencing itself as finite. And it makes this experience in a double sense: it fails in its project and finds no fulfillment conforming to the sense animating it, and in this failure its finitude manifests itself as its own truth; and, more originally, this finitude was already its own as an *immanent subjective character*, marking it interiorly as a distinct existence having its own tonality — and it is on this very plane of transcendental affectivity that such finitude first revealed itself to it. The appeal to transcendental affectivity is the point at which Chapter V's positive doctrine does work in the Conclusion: finitude is first *felt* as a tonality before it is met as a failure.

Where our corporeal life is concerned, these descriptions find an example in the phenomenon of *sexuality*. Finitude signifies here that intentionality directs itself on a finite element of transcendent being. But every transcendent element is necessarily finite. If finitude belongs properly to this particular mode of existence, the latter must be characterized more precisely. It will be if we remark that, unlike our daily life in which we direct ourselves toward finite objects *while taking them as such*, the intentionality presiding over the form our life takes in sexuality orients itself toward its transcendent term *as toward something absolute*. Sexual intentionality does not simply "direct itself toward" but "concentrates on" the object in which it tries to apprehend what is *all*. This object remains something finite, but for it takes on a wholly particular signification.

On what condition can an object, as a finite element in the general milieu of transcendent being, take on this signification of being something absolute for the consciousness apprehending it? On condition of not being an ordinary object but in a sense a *magical* object, whose characters do not all coincide with those of the ontological milieu in which it nonetheless manifests itself. Such an object exists: our transcendent objective body. It is precisely that ambiguous element hiding, beneath the determination of being-there, the infinite of the subjectivity secretly inhabiting it. The ontological status of our transcendent objective body is the foundation making possible the existence of a *sexual world*, in which what constitutes the ambiguity of such a body is carried to its paroxysm. The mystery of sex holds in this: that in it *determination acquires a power without limit*. This power it owes to the fundamental obscurity of its being, an obscurity by which subjectivity is as it were present in the element of being-there. What the desiring consciousness aims at is precisely this subjectivity it seeks to apprehend through and in the object. The absolute desired is mediated by the contingent and finite term that is there but that could equally not be there. *What is absolute seems contingent.*

But here is the important thing: the implicit presupposition of sexual intentionality is that *the absolute is accessible only under this contingent face*. So it concentrates on the finite element and adores it. What it finds in such an experience, however, is not the absolute but only a lifeless presence, that realized by the pure being-there of a transcendent element. Thus what seemed accessible through such an element reveals itself rather to be the inaccessible: it is pure subjectivity, which is not the object. Such a subjectivity can be neither touched nor apprehended in any way in a finite determination, since by principle it is nothing transcendent. To want to attain spirit under the form of a sensible element is to use a method that eliminates *a priori* the object it claims to circumscribe. Insofar as it directs itself on sex understood as a being-there and an object, *sexual intentionality meets a failure prescribed by ontology*. When the anguish before sex dissipates, nothing remains but an objective determination. The subjectivity aimed at has vanished. Sex will never deliver its secret, for in the naked light of transcendence it is only a being-there without secret. The failure to which sexual intentionality thus arrives does not have as consequence the simple suppression of that intentionality; it becomes rather the principle of its *repetition* in indefinitely renewed acts. In the solidarity of this failure and this repetition lies the foundation of the sexual obsession of humanity. The structural account of compulsion here is one of the book's most striking applications: repetition is not explained by drive or by the unconscious but by an ontologically guaranteed failure of an intentional aim.

In what sense can finitude apply to the human body? The original being of our body does not fall under finitude in sense 1, which applies only to transcendent being. As for our objective body, the remarks made about the essential characters constituting its own being invite us to distrust the appearance that this first sense suits it adequately. Insofar as it finds its foundation in the original being of the absolute body, our transcendent objective body is in fact inhabited by a subjectivity accommodating itself poorly to the determinations of objectivity. For different but still deeper reasons, this applies also to the organic body.

Taken in its second sense, finitude applies to the *representation* of an intentionality. But what can then be said finite is in no way the original, real being of intentionality, only its being-represented. That an intentionality belonging to our corporeal life can be so represented, that a gesture can detach itself from our existence to appear as an inert element posited in representation, is in fact the most striking confirmation the theory of the subjective body can receive. For the destiny then striking the life of our body in one or another of its intentionalities is susceptible of reaching any conceivable intentionality; it raises its threat over the whole of our psychological life. The possibility for a determination of our absolute life to undergo this radical modification which tears it from its ontological milieu and signifies properly for it *death* is an absolutely general phenomenon whose elucidation concerns an ontology of subjectivity and cannot relate to the particular problem of the body. What results is that *neither sense 1 nor sense 2 concerns the original being of our absolute body*, that is, our corporeal existence properly speaking.

Here, however, comes the decisive remark. If, in senses 1 and 2, finitude has an indisputably ontological bearing, since it refers essentially to a determinate ontological region, this is no longer so in senses 3 and 4. This is evident for sense 4, according to which finitude receives a specifically existential signification as a clearly defined mode of existence. This mode can be said finite because in it existence directs itself on a transcendent and as such finite determination to which it nonetheless confers an absolute signification. The power of fascination exercised by the being-there rests on its being not just any being-there but that ambiguous object which is our transcendent objective body. *The infinite power of determination — that is finitude in the Christian sense.* The "body" of Christian tradition refers essentially to such a finitude. It has so manifestly an existential signification, it concerns so little the body understood as an ontological reality, that this body, promised to resurrection, can be the support of radically different modes of existence. The mode designated by "body," "flesh" is called finite because it orients itself toward a particular determination in such a way that it devotes itself to it: *it is this cult rendered to the finite that is properly sin*. The definition of sin as a cult of the finite is the Conclusion's terminal formula, and it is arrived at ontologically rather than borrowed from theology.

Applied to an intentionality exhausting itself in determination, the term finitude is perfectly understandable and acceptable, provided one sees that this is only a manner of speaking, *for the mode of existence said to be finite is in fact a mode of the absolute life of subjectivity and as such belongs by essence to an infinite sphere of existence*. Which amounts to saying: finitude, insofar as it qualifies no longer a transcendent element (senses 1 and 2) but a mode of existence, a determination of the life of absolute subjectivity (senses 4 and also 3), *has no longer any ontological signification but only an existential one*. Thus the intentionality orienting itself decisively toward the finite — so that determination becomes the object of a cult and the sensible makes itself the sensual — remains no less, as an intentionality, a mode of the absolute life of subjectivity, and as such an *infinite* mode. Finitude designates an imperfection of life, but this imperfection has only an ethical, existential, or religious signification: in fact, within that very imperfection, life remains what it is, an absolute life, a life in an infinite sphere of existence. *Finitude is a category of ethics; the absolute character of the existence that succumbs to it is an ontological determination belonging to it by principle.* Clear consciousness of a rigorous discrimination between these two planes is indispensable to anyone who wants to deepen the problem of evil with some chance of success, for such discrimination alone lets us understand that something can be at once finite and infinite — which is precisely what evil is — and that, as Kierkegaard saw in his critique of Socrates, sin must be thought, despite its finitude, as an *absolutely positive qualification of existence*.

Henry appends an important note guarding against a misreading: because sexual intentionality was chosen to illustrate a certain sense of finitude, one risks concluding an equivalence between sexuality as such and finitude. In fact, as finitude received only an existential signification designating certain defined modes of existence, so the sexual intentionality alluded to concerns only one possible and clearly defined form of our sexual life. *The possibility of a love without anguish* is not excluded by these descriptions; it finds rather in the theory of the subjective body its most fundamental ontological presupposition. For that theory shows that the sexual act is, in its original being at least, something subjective. As such it is outside the world; the fact that sexual intentionality orients itself toward objective being-there represents a *contingent specification* of an intentionality bearing in its freedom the possibility of a radically different orientation, by which existence can free itself from determination and aim at something quite other. *There is a love, and what is more a sexual love, from which the "body" is absent.* The great error of most existential descriptions of sexual intentionality is that a determinate mode is almost always envisaged exclusively and presented as prescribed by the essence of man's sexual life in general — notably in all descriptions limiting themselves to objective sexual determinations. The theory permits giving an infinite and free sense, from an existential standpoint, not only to sexual gestures but to *all* human gestures, since it shows on the ontological plane the subjective essence of all the original determinations of corporeal life. It should therefore lead not only to a new philosophy of sexuality but to a new philosophy of all man's "material" acts, a new philosophy of rite, of work, of cult. Registering this note matters: it prevents the sexuality analysis from hardening into a doctrine about sexuality as such, and it names the wider program the theory opens.

These remarks about sense 4 hold evidently for sense 3 as well. If one says existence is finite because it is ordered to a world, the affirmation is first an axiological appreciation, a judgment borne from above on human existence, whose content is perhaps not independent of the attitude such a judgment implies insofar as it leans over existence as over an exterior essence. To rule on existence from a strictly ontological standpoint is rather to understand it in the light of the milieu belonging properly to it, a milieu of radical immanence. The eidetic status of such a milieu confers on the life revealing itself in it the ontological qualification of being an absolute life and consequently withdraws *a priori* from the concept of finitude all ontological signification regarding such a life.

One can doubtless find an ontological foundation for human finitude insofar as one designates by it the insurmountable link of existence and world. The fact alleged, the primordial phenomenon of transcendence, has assuredly an ontological bearing. But the life that transcends itself is an absolute life and, in that act of transcending, remains in itself. *More original than the phenomenon of transcendence, anterior to it, is that of immanence, in which transcendence in fact finds its ultimate condition of possibility.* If the act that transcends can discover a world only insofar as it is first and constantly present to itself within its *auto-affection* in immanence, then the thesis binding finitude and transcendence rests on the forgetting of this most original ontological phenomenon. Is it by chance that it appears for the first time in modern philosophy in *Kant*, whose ontology is characterized precisely by the absence of all theory of absolute subjectivity? Only the elaboration of such a theory can throw down the ontological foundation of the concept of finitude insofar as it claims to apply to human existence. Thought as subjective, this existence is recognized as an absolute existence, *even when it is that of our body*. This is the book's final argument and its most consequential: the entire modern doctrine of finitude rests on taking transcendence as ultimate, and auto-affection is the phenomenon it forgot.

*It is to a single philosopher that it was given to unite these two fundamental teachings: the one revealing the structure of absolute subjectivity and the one determining such a structure as being also that of our body.*

That corporeal existence is an absolute existence is a proposition whose full comprehension would lead us to modify profoundly most of our conceptions about the life of the body. In our civilization these conceptions are commanded by the implicit presuppositions of *naturalism*, summarized in the affirmation that *need is something natural*. Interpreted in the light of the ontology naturalism refers to, this means that the principal activities in which our corporeal life expresses itself must be understood as manifestations belonging in their essence to the general being of nature, that is, as objective and impersonal processes. They are anonymous "functions" to be let play according to their own rhythm. Every attempt to modify their natural accomplishment is condemned *a priori* by the ethics resting on naturalist philosophy — as for instance regarding man's sexual life. It follows that individual and personal existence is exterior to the world of its needs, to their requirements, and to the acts by which these direct themselves toward a real or illusory satisfaction. Thereby the ego declines all responsibility for its corporeal life. *The soul can remain pure when the body is defiled.* This distinction, implying the objectification of needs — their rigorous dissociation from what constitutes the essential being of the ego — is the principle of a *bad faith* manifesting itself strikingly in Rousseau and also, to a certain extent, in Maine de Biran insofar as he remains submitted to the influence of the *Confessions* and to traditional dualism. Note that the last critical word in the book falls on Biran again, and on the same borrowed dualism Chapter VI isolated.

The theory of the subjective body, showing that the life of our body is in no way a third-person life, obliges us to modify this standpoint radically. It is not on the plane of morality and for properly moral reasons that naturalist ethics is condemnable; the critique must bear on its philosophical presuppositions, which constitute an ontological nonsense. Naturalism forbids itself *a priori* the comprehension of all human needs because it misrecognizes their proper essence. It claims to rehabilitate the life of the body, but this is its greatest illusion. Believing it defends the rights of the flesh against spirit, it reduces spirit to the condition of a disincarnate subject that can only survey the concrete determinations of an empirical existence unfolding in an impersonal mode commanded by objective laws. But such a conception is not proper to the naturalist who exalts the objective needs of his body; it is shared in fact by the moralist who despises them and claims the soul is not reached in its serene purity by the trouble they communicate. Thus the theses of naturalism coincide with those of traditional intellectualist and moralist philosophy as much as with those of empiricism in general. The convergence is the same one the book has demonstrated repeatedly — exalting and despising the objective body are two attitudes toward one shared ontology.

*But need is subjective.* It has the weight, the gravity of the infinite existence bearing it, and also the simplicity and transparency of the absolute life in which it reveals itself. Because need is subjective, it does not have the innocence of a movement of matter; because it is not a simple transcendent displacement one might consider neutral from the spiritual standpoint, *it offers itself to the categories of ethics. The bodies will be judged.* When one has reduced desires to innate inclinations, or made them the simple correlate of organic modifications, one has removed from human existence its effective and concrete content as well as the proper qualification it takes on in a defined mode of its intentionality and life. *It is not on the plane of abstract ideas, it is on that of needs that our existence is really played out.* This is why the satisfaction or non-satisfaction of our needs, and more deeply the manner in which that satisfaction is or is not accomplished, has such great importance in the history of each individual as of human groups.

The needs of the body are generally called *material* needs. The theory of the subjective body shows what to think of such a terminology, which stems not only from naive ontology but from moral conceptions which, though bound to such an ontology, have taken on a proper value and great development. The designation of corporeal life as material life often gives itself as a protest against intellectualist and idealist philosophy: *it is rather a consequence of it*. To affirm the importance of "material" life by opposition to the spiritual life of a disincarnate subject is indeed to oppose traditional idealism. But to call *material* this corporeal life one rightly holds to be a decisive element of human existence is to form of the body the same ontological conception as such idealism and as philosophy of Hellenic origin. Insofar, however, as it recognizes the primordial importance of "material" needs, that is, of corporeal life in general, *every materialist doctrine takes on in the eyes of the philosophy of the subjective body a decisive importance*. Materialism will nonetheless be able to receive its full development, and in particular to bring the human sciences the vast contribution they can legitimately expect of it, only when it is interpreted in the light of the results of the ontological analysis of the body. This is a genuine and unexpected rapprochement, and it should be read carefully: Henry credits materialism with locating existence at the level of needs while denying it the ontology of the "material," which is the prize his own later work on Marx will claim.

The theory of the subjective body, rejecting the traditional distinction of body and spirit, obliges us to assume on all planes the consequences of such a rejection. Their examination could here give rise only to a brief enumeration, letting us take consciousness of the vast field of research opening before the philosophy of the subjective body — and more generally before that of subjectivity — when it wants to examine the particular yet essential problems of existence in the light of its own general presuppositions. The importance of the latter holds not only in the apodictic character of the foundation and the absolute evidence of the propositions of the positive science they constitute, which is nothing other than first ontology; it is measured also by the fecundity these fundamental propositions manifest as soon as one applies them to a determinate domain. The theory of the subjective body is thus only a *first application* of the general ontology of subjectivity. The particular problems to which the theory of the subjective body applies in turn must give rise to rigorously determined researches, conformably to the sense prescribed *a priori* by the ontological content elaborated by the philosophy of the subjective body.

The book therefore ends not with a result but with a program, and the closing sentence places the whole undertaking in the position Chapter I assigned to Biran's own work: a guiding thread rather than a doctrine, and a first application of an ontology whose full elaboration lies in *L'essence de la manifestation*.
